## 1.2. Scientific advice and protocol assistance

Table 1. Scientific advice and protocol assistance

| Date          | Topic (quality/ non-clinical/ clinical)      | Reference number / Coordinator(s )   | Brief summary of the advice                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
|---------------|----------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 22 April 2022 | Quality, non- clinical, and clinical aspects | EMA/SA/0000 077078                   | The Scientific advice pertained to the following quality, non-clinical, and clinical aspects: - Acceptability of overall vaccine development plan, specifically adequacy of specifications for medicine development and acceptability of reproductive toxicity studies. - Design of Phase 3 study for selection of dose and immunogenicity endpoints. - Comparison of aQIVc vaccine versus licensed comparator aQIV to show non-inferiority or superiority of aQIVc. - Assessment of the immunogenicity of aQIVc versus QIVc for A/H3N2 heterologous vaccines strains. |

## 1.3. Eligibility to the centralised procedure

The applicant Seqirus Netherlands B.V. submitted on 26 June 2025 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Aujemflu (influenza vaccine (surface antigen, inactivated, adjuvanted, prepared in cell cultures)), through the centralised procedure. The eligibility was granted by the CHMP on 12 December 2024.

The applicant applied for the following indication: 'Prophylaxis of influenza in adults 50 years of age and older. Aujemflu should be used in accordance with official recommendations'.

## 1.4. Legal basis and dossier content

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

According to the Commission Notice on Handling of duplicate marketing authorisation applications of pharmaceutical products under Article 82(1) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004, a medicinal product that contains different excipients that result in significant differences in safety or efficacy is considered outside the scope of Article 82(1). A justification for this product not being considered a duplicate has been submitted by the applicant.

## 1.5. Information on paediatrics

Not applicable

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 1.6. Information on orphan market exclusivity

## 1.6.1. Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the applicant did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products from the start of the procedure because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

## 1.8. CHMP outcome

## 1.8.1. Considerations related to paediatrics

The requirements for the submitted dossier in relation to paediatrics are described in section 1.5 of this report.

## 1.8.2. Considerations related to orphan market exclusivity

The requirements of the submitted dossier in relation to orphan market exclusivity are described in section 1.6 of this report.

## 1.8.3. Opinion

Based on the CHMP review of data on quality, safety and efficacy, the CHMP considers by consensus that the benefit-risk balance of Aujemflu is favourable in the following indication:

Prophylaxis of influenza in adults 50 years of age and older.

Aujemflu should be used in accordance with official recommendations.

The CHMP, therefore, recommends the granting of the marketing authorisation subject to the conditions described in the following sections.

## 1.8.4. Conditions or restrictions regarding supply and use

Medicinal product subject to medical prescription.

## 1.8.5. Official batch release

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic Context

Influenza is an infectious acute respiratory disease characterised by the abrupt onset of respiratory and systemic symptoms, such as fever, cough, sore throat, and rhinitis (Temte and Prunuske 2010). Influenza occurs as annual epidemics in the Northern Hemisphere and Southern Hemisphere, generally affecting individuals during the winter months in temperate climates. In tropical climates, influenza can occur all year round.

Influenza in humans can be caused by the influenza virus type A, B, and C, with the type A and type B viruses being the most clinically relevant (WHO 2018). Type A viruses are associated with both annual epidemics and pandemics, and type B viruses contribute to annual epidemics. Type A viruses are further divided into different subtypes, of which the A/H3N2 and A/H1N1 viruses are the most clinically relevant for the annual influenza disease burden (CDC 2021). For influenza B, only a single type is known to exist, but two distinct genetic lineages have been identified: Yamagata and Victoria. Circulation of the B/Yamagata lineage has not been confirmed since March 2020. All influenza viruses experience some form of antigenic drift, but this is most pronounced in the influenza A virus for which new circulating variants are frequently observed over the influenza season. Such evolution of the influenza virus influences the host specificity and pathogenicity of these viruses, which can differ geographically, from season to season, and within a season.

Influenza is an acute and highly contagious viral infection with global circulation. Globally, there are an estimated 1 billion cases of influenza disease every year, of which 3 to 5 million are severe cases, resulting in 290,000 to 650,000 influenza-related respiratory deaths (WHO 2019; Iuliano et al. 2018). In the United States (US), the influenza-related hospitalization rate over 15 seasons was estimated to be 309 per 100,000 in individuals 65 years and older (Zhou et al. 2012). Although the rate of hospitalization associated with influenza is lower in subjects aged 50 to 64 years compared with those aged 65 years and older, the highest number of influenza illnesses and medical visits is reported for the 50-64 years age cohort, which indicates a high burden of disease in this age group (CDC 2021).

Vaccination is the primary method for preventing influenza and its potentially severe complications. Moderate-to-high efficacy of conventional unadjuvanted, egg-based, standard dose influenza vaccines has been demonstrated in the healthy adult population (18-50 years of age) (Samuel and Miller 2019). In contrast, the efficacy of conventional vaccines is significantly lower in elderly individuals compared with younger adults due to aging of the immune system (i.e., immunosenescence) and underlying medical conditions that can both increase the risk of influenza complications and interfere with immune responses (Goodwin et al. 2006; Sasaki et al. 2011; Dorrington and Bowdish 2013). In view of the limitations of conventional influenza vaccines to prevent disease in older adults, there continues to be an unmet need for a new generation of influenza vaccines that provide more consistent and broader coverage against all seasonal virus subtypes and variants (Reber et al. 2012; Wong and Webby 2013).

## 2.2. Aspects of development

MF59-adjuvanted trivalent subunit inactivated cell-derived influenza vaccine (aTIVc) combines 3 technologies to overcome immunosenescence, namely, cell-based technology, an adjuvant, and a higher antigen dose.

Cell culture-derived antigens may provide a better match to circulating influenza strains, addressing the issue of potential mismatch due to egg adaptation, which could improve vaccine effectiveness (Lambert and Fauci 2010; Hegde 2015). Hence, the use of cell-based technology in the production of aTIVc.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overcoming immunosenescence can be further achieved by enhancing the immune response with the addition of an adjuvant to the vaccine formulation, such as the MF59 adjuvant, which is a squalene and water emulsion adjuvant. The addition of MF59 increases antigen uptake, macrophage recruitment, macrophage migration, and the spectrum of antibody recognition of HA epitopes (Calabro et al. 2011; O'Hagan et. 2012). When added to influenza vaccines, MF59 increases vaccine immunogenicity against both homologous and heterologous strains. Fluad, CSL Seqirus' trivalent seasonal influenza egg-based vaccine adjuvanted with MF59, has been licensed for use in Europe since 1997 and in the US since 2015. This adjuvanted trivalent influenza vaccine (aTIV) has been shown to generate significantly higher geometric mean hemagglutination inhibition (HI) titres and rates of seroconversion than a nonadjuvanted trivalent influenza vaccine (TIV) comparator in elderly subjects (Frey et al. 2014). Inclusion of higher doses of the MF59 adjuvant in the aTIVc vaccine is expected to enable: (i) increased magnitude of immune response by stimulating higher antibody responses; (ii) increased breadth of immune response, by expanding antibody repertoire; and (iii) increased duration of immune response, with higher antibody response at 6 months after vaccination, which is important in case of prolonged influenza seasons and/or late season outbreaks (Frey et al. 2014).

Another way to improve the benefit of influenza vaccines in the elderly may be to increase the vaccine antigen amount per dose above the 15 µg of HA per strain found in standard dose inactivated vaccines. In a Phase 1 study, vaccination with higher doses of influenza virus antigen (up to 2 times the standard HA antigen dose) and MF59 adjuvant (up to 3 times the standard MF59 dose) has been demonstrated to increase the magnitude of the immune response by stimulating higher antibody responses to virus antigens (Otten et al. 2020). The use of serological surrogates as a measure of vaccine efficacy is generally accepted for regulatory purposes.

In summary, aTIVc is expected to offer potential important advantages over currently existing/licensed influenza vaccines in the ≥ 50 years of age category (e.g., Fluad, Fluzone High-Dose, Flublok) by combining the benefits of a cell-based platform, adjuvanted technology, and high dose of HA antigens.

The clinical development program to support licensure of aTIVc in individuals ≥ 50 years of age is based on the results of supportive dose finding Study V201\\_01, supportive dose confirmation Study V201\\_07, and the pivotal aQIVc Study V201\\_03 (a confirmatory controlled trial for immunogenicity and safety), which tested the aQIVc HD formulation against other (a)QIVc formulations and comparator vaccines; data from an additional supportive Study V200\\_10 (proof of concept) are also included, as this study first investigated the addition of adjuvant to QIVc, testing aQIVc at a standard dose of HA antigens and MF59. The vaccine naming convention used in this document is provided in Table 2. In the clinical study report for pivotal Study V201\\_03, aQIVc is referred to as aQIVc HD.

The manufacturing process and formulation of aTIVc are the same as that of aQIVc, with the exception of the additional B strain included in aQIVc. Therefore, clinical study experience with aQIVc provides data for assessment of aTIVc.

The data package to support the favourable benefit/risk profile of aTIVc in individuals ≥50 years of age includes immunogenicity and safety data from 4 completed studies. No studies are currently ongoing.

The design, conduct and analysis of studies in the development of aQIVc/aTIVc comply with international and regional standards for clinical research in humans, and for investigation of the immunogenicity and safety of seasonal influenza virus vaccines.

As a first step, having confirmed that the aQIVc HD formulation (45,2) elicited a superior immune response to the licensed, non-adjuvanted, equivalent, cell-based Flucelvax Quadrivalent (QIVc) for all 4 strains in the Phase 2b dose-confirmation immunogenicity and safety study (V201\\_07), enhanced influenza vaccines that are the standard of care for older adults were included as comparators in Phase 3.

For pivotal Study V201\\_03, since aQIVc HD contains the same adjuvant (MF59) as aQIV, albeit in a higher dose, the enhanced influenza vaccine aQIV was chosen as a comparator influenza vaccine to demonstrate the effect of the increased amount of adjuvant and increased antigen dose. With the approval of aQIV in the UK and EU for adults 50 years and older, demonstration of immunological superiority of aQIVc HD over aQIV in both the 50 years and older and 65 years and older age groups would provide the basis to apply for licensure in these regions.

QIVr is another enhanced influenza vaccine due to its high dose of recombinant HA antigen content. Inclusion of this vaccine as a second comparator in pivotal Study V201\\_03 provided further regulatory flexibility, allowing adaptation to the preferences of national advisory divisions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The chosen dual comparator study design is adequately sized and powered to meet the study endpoints and considered sufficiently robust to assess the effect of aQIVc HD formulation in terms of immunogenicity and safety separately for each comparator.

In this MAA, the comparison against aQIV is the basis of the data for licensure.

All studies were designed, implemented, and reported in accordance with the International Council for Harmonisation of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use (ICH) Guidelines for Good Clinical Practice, with applicable local regulations.

## 2.3. Description of the product

aTIVc and TIVc (Flucelvax) have the same qualitative composition in active substance(s) and are manufactured using the same MDCK cell-based platform from the same marketing authorisation holder. Although the two products contain the same active substance, aTIVc contains three times the content of antigen compared to TIVc (45 μg HA of each strain and 15 μg, respectively) and is additionally adjuvanted with MF59C.1 (MF59, 19.5 mg of squalene), which is designed to increase and broaden the antigen-specific immune response and to extend the duration of the immune response.

According to the Commission Notice on Handling of duplicate marketing authorisation applications of pharmaceutical products under Article 82(1) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004, a medicinal product that contains different excipients that result in significant differences in safety or efficacy is considered outside the scope of Article 82(1).

The Company considers that aTIVc is outside the scope of Article 82(1) based on the rationale that the difference in excipients (presence of the adjuvant MF59 in aTIVc formulation) results in significant differences in safety or efficacy between aTIVc and TIVc, and this is considered valid.

Results from the Phase 2 studies demonstrate that aQIVc with the (45,2) formulation presents an improved immune response in subjects aged 50 years and older. The dose-confirmation study demonstrated that aQIVc (45,2) has an enhanced immunogenicity compared with registered Flucelvax Tetra.

The safety assessment based on the Phase 2 studies comparing aQIVc formulations vs Flucelvax Tetra demonstrates that aQIVc has an acceptable safety profile. The frequent adverse events after vaccination with aQIVc are solicited local and systemic reactions. An increase in local and systemic reactogenicity with aQIVc due to higher MF59 dose compared to QIVc was expected and was observed. However, most of these reactions were mild to moderate in severity and resolved within 3 days. No unexpected risks for more serious or severe events were identified in clinical experience with aQIVc.

The overall safety profile of aQIVc, and therefore aTIVc, is similar to that established for QIVc, with a slightly increased reactogenicity due to higher antigen and the addition of MF59.

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

aTIVc is a trivalent seasonal surface antigen, inactivated influenza vaccine, prepared in cell cultures and adjuvanted with MF59C.1 (MF59), a squalene-based oil-in-water emulsion. aTIVc contains predominantly haemagglutinin (HA) and neuraminidase (NA) surface antigens from each of the three influenza strains (Type A/H1N1, Type A/H3N2 and Type B [Victoria Lineage]), as recommended annually by the WHO recommendation (Northern Hemisphere) for seasonal trivalent influenza vaccine.

The virus strains are propagated in Madin Darby Canine Kidney (MDCK) cells, after which the antigens are purified and solubilized. The manufacturing process for the monovalent bulk is derived from that developed for Flucelvax Tetra / Flucelvax. The same manufacturing process for the monovalent bulk is also used for the pandemic influenza vaccine Incellipan and for the zoonotic influenza vaccine Celldemic.

The MF59 proprietary adjuvant is the same as that included in the seasonal or pandemic/zoonotic influenza vaccines Fluad Tetra, Fluad, Aflunov, Foclivia, Incellipan, Celldemic and Zoonotic Influenza Vaccine Seqirus (H5N8).

The vaccine is provided in a 1.5 mL pre-filled syringe (type I glass) with plunger-stopper (bromobutyl rubber) presented with or without needle. Each pre-filled syringe contains 1 dose of 1.0 ml and they are presented in packs of 1 or 10 pre-filled syringes.

Other excipients are Sodium chloride Potassium chloride (E508) Magnesium chloride hexahydrate (E511) Disodium phosphate dihydrate (E339) Potassium dihydrogen phosphate (E340) Water for injections.

The aTIVc is a high dose vaccine, with a FP composition of three times the standard hemagglutinin (HA) antigen dose (45 µg HA of each strain vs 15 µg as standard in TIV such as Flucelvax or Fluad) and two times the standard dose of the MF59 adjuvant in aTIV (containing 19.5 mg of squalene vs 9.75 mg as standard Fluad). The administration volume is also two times more than Fluad or Flucelvax.

The proposed therapeutic indication is the prophylaxis of influenza in adults 50 years of age and older.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

The Active Substance (AS) consists of three monovalent bulks of purified virus antigen (hemagglutinin, HA and neuraminidase, NA) of influenza strains, Type A (H1N1), Type A (H3N2) and Type B (Victoria Lineage). The virus strains are chosen in compliance with the official annual strain recommendation from the WHO, CHMP, CBER, and CDC and produced in suspension cultures of Madin Darby Canine Kidney (MDCK) cells.

HA is a glycosylated, integral membrane protein. The HA and NA proteins are intended to elicit a serological immune response to each of the subtypes included in the vaccine.

The general properties of the AS presented by the Applicant are satisfactory.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, process controls, and process development

## Description of manufacturing process and process controls

Manufacture of the active substance is conducted by Seqirus, Inc., 475 Green Oaks Parkway, Holly Springs, NC 27540, United States. BioReliance Corporation (Broschart Road and Blackwell Road) Rockville, perform QC testing and WCB manufacturing and testing.  Additionally, SGS Vitrology, Glasgow performs adventitious agent testing for virus seed and active substance.  Satisfactory GMP at these sites has been demonstrated.

The AS in the manufacture of the product is designated as the Flu Cell Culture (FCC) monovalent bulk.

Each FCC monovalent bulk is prepared by propagation of influenza working seed virus (WSV) in MDCK 33016 PF cell suspension cultures.

Flow charts have been provided for upstream and downstream process stages, including non-infectious and infections (i.e. virus expansion steps).  The identification and corresponding ranges/limits of critical process parameters, critical process controls, and release criteria are provided in the figures.

A detailed description of the AS manufacturing process is provided. Process parameters and process controls are indicated as well as sanitization procedures for chromatographic columns used during the purification process. Batch size is indicated and the possibility of re-processing is also specified.

The composition of the AS may change annually depending on the recommended strains to be included in the vaccine. A general description of the studies that are done annually is given. each influenza strain that has been included in the vaccine that is object of this MAA is provided here.

## Control of materials

Seed viruses: The candidate vaccine viruses (CVV) for the preparation of the WSV lots are obtained from the WHO Collaborating Centers and do not require release testing, the certificate obtained from the supplier is used to release the material. The manufacturing process and control strategy for producing virus seed stocks is described and is acceptable.

The WSV manufacturing process as well as the In-Process Control and Release Testing of WSV are included and considered acceptable. Batch analysis of WSV produced for being used in the manufacture of AS batches are provided. Passage histories for each of the viruses have also been submitted.

Cell Substrates: Details of protocol are also provided. Cell bank qualification has been conducted according to state-of-the-art knowledge and technology with emerging technology and assay methodology and is also found adequate. A protocol for implementation of new WCBs is provided and is acceptable. Future WCBs will be tested and released according to the specifications of WCBs.

The information on TSE risk and adventitious agents is considered acceptable and discussed in A.2 Adventitious Agents Safety Evaluation. The cell line does not pose a risk of transmitting TSE. Media components of human and animal derived media components have been extensively investigated, and their TSE associated risk is found to be negligible.

Media, Solutions, and Raw Materials: Raw materials are described. Neither the media, solutions and buffers, chemicals or solvents used in manufacturing contain any animal-derived components. All filters, membranes and resins are also free from animal-derived materials.  Compendial raw materials are tested in accordance with the corresponding monograph, while specifications (including analytical procedures) for non-compendial raw materials are presented.

## Control of critical steps and intermediates

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The AS control strategy is provided in detail and is supported. The CPPs associated with the manufacturing process are provided as well as in-process controls (IPCs) and the release criteria of the product intermediate and/or final DS. All the methods used for the control of critical steps and intermediates have been described and validated or verified. This is endorsed.

## Process validation and/or evaluation

All process validation activities have been successfully completed per the AS Process Validation Master Plan. To ensure that the process remains in a validated state during commercial manufacturing, a Continuous Process Verification (CPV) has been established. Full validation reports and summary results from the AS process validation studies are provided and confirm the validated status of the AS manufacturing process. This has been accepted.

## Manufacturing process development

The initial process was validated for preclinical toxicity studies and trivalent influenza vaccine (TIVc) clinical trial use in 2002. Commercial scale manufacture has been successfully validated with comparability demonstrated each time a new change was introduced in the AS manufacturing process.

## 3.2.3. Characterisation and impurities

## Characterisation

Seqirus performs HA and NA characterization studies on each new influenza strain used to confirm the identity, purity, and suitability of the two antigens. The active substance has been sufficiently characterised by physicochemical and biological methods.

## Impurities

The impurities have been sufficiently characterised and process-related and product-related impurities are adequately controlled and found acceptable.

## 3.2.4. Control of Active Substance

The specification for the active substance includes tests for identity, protein content, purity, physicochemical and microbiological properties.

Specifications for the AS are the same as formerly approved for TIVc as the FCC AS can be used to manufacture both trivalent and quadrivalent formulations with different nominal dose concentrations. The HA antigen content specification is the same and maintained with appropriate controls to ensure the final concentration. The proposed acceptance criteria are properly justified and deemed acceptable.

Regarding process-related impurities, levels of PS80, CTAB and residual infectious influenza virus are controlled to acceptable levels through AS specifications. Other process related residuals (BPL, recombinant trypsin, simethicone and benzonase) are not routinely controlled at AS release as removal has been demonstrated through product development and process validation.

Regarding the process-related impurities associated with the cell substrate, MDCK cells, HCPs and host cell DNA are not tested at AS release, but HCPs and host cell DNA are tested at the FP release.

MDCK cell removal throughout the manufacturing process has been demonstrated through a series of process evaluations that leverage previous validation studies and new technical studies. The risk of intact cells remaining in the final product is considered negligible.

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The analytical methods used for AS release are indicated and a summary of the validation results for all the methods is provided.

Reference standards are indicated. In particular, the reference standards for the HA and NA antigens and antisera are provided by the applicable WHO Collaborating Center for Influenza or Essential Regulatory Laboratories (ERLs) as recommended.

## Batch analysis

Data for the monovalent lots regarding the virus seed lots employed is also provided.

## Container closure

The information on the monovalent bulk container presented on compatibility, integrity and suitability is considered adequate. The container closure system is autoclaved using a sterile load configuration.

## 3.2.5. Stability

Stability data are provided for the PPQ AS batches from each of the three strains (H1N1, H3N2 and B/Victoria).

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

## Description of the product

aTIVc is a preservative-free trivalent influenza vaccine that contains 45 µg HA/dose/strain of three virus strains (two A virus (H1N1 and H3N2) strains and one B/Victoria virus strain) and 0.5 mL F59C.1/dose adjuvant in a 1.0 mL nominal dose in a 1.5 mL prefilled syringe. Each syringe is intended for a single use. The quantitative composition of the aTIVc product is provided in Table 3, along with the function and quality standard for each ingredient.

## Pharmaceutical development

The vaccine is a trivalent composition. The clinical development of the product has been performed with the tetravalent formulation.  Based on the recommendation of the WHO (September 2023), Seqirus removed the B/Yamagata strain from the product composition resulting in a trivalent vaccine. The formulation process remains the same with the exception of the removal of the B/Yamagata monobulk.

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

## Manufacturers

The finished product manufacturing, packaging, testing and release sites are specified in the dossier. The finished product will be released by Seqirus Netherlands B.V., Amsterdam, the Netherlands.

The FP manufacturers are stated with the responsibilities of each manufacturing site.

Regarding the manufacturing sites located outside the EU:

- Seqirus Inc. site (Holly Springs, USA) has been inspected by the US FDA through 28/05/2025 to 05/06/2025 with a satisfactory outcome. The website of the FDA (https://datadashboard.fda.gov/oii/cd/inspections.htm) showing the inspections carried out.
- In addition, this site has been inspected by the Finish Medicines Agency and the GMP certificate is provided.
- Seqirus Vaccines Ltd. (Liverpool, UK): MIA certificate (UK MIA 18532) issued by MHRA is provided.
- Batch release is performed by Seqirus Netherlands BV.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For the EU manufacturing sites, valid MIAs are provided.

## Batch formula

The weight of the HA antigen required for each of the three strains and other drug product components are calculated according to a defined formula. The formula depends on the potency of each monovalent bulk and is understood and endorsed.

## Manufacturing process and Process controls

The manufacturing process consists in blending of monobulk lots of the three antigen strains, dilution with phosphate buffered saline buffers and MF59C.1 adjuvant.  The formulated bulk vaccine is filtered for sterilization.  The formulated drug product is filled into each syringe at a nominal volume of 1.0 mL dose for inspection and packaging.

## Process validation / verification

The aQIVc manufacturing process was developed and manufactured at CSL Seqirus HSP site to provide clinical trial materials to support phase 2 and phase 3 clinical studies. Validation data has been presented in support of the manufacturing process and is considered validated. It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner.

## 3.3.3. Control of Finished Product

## Specifications

The specifications includes tests for identity, protein content, purity, physicochemical and microbiological properties.

The proposed release specifications for aTIVc final formulated bulk and for aTIVc finished FP in prefilled syringes are in principle acceptable.

Regarding the HA content, an upper limit has been set  to limit undesirable reactogenicity and for consistency purpose of high dose product, and to align the dossier with the Ph. Eur. requirement (Following Pharmeuropa 37.3 publication of monograph 2149 on surface antigen).

The acceptance criteria for identity, protein content, purity, physicochemical and microbiological properties, and product-related impurities are controlled in the finished product release testing. Process-related impurities controlled at the monobulk level have been demonstrated to be reduced to very low/undetected levels during the manufacturing process.

The volume of MF59C.1 used in the aTIVc (0.5 mL) is double that used in other approved Influenza vaccines (e.g. aTIV, 0.25 mL). A toxicological risk assessment was performed evaluating a worst-case situation. The results show that the levels of product-related impurities (polychorobiphenyls/dioxins and arsenic, and carbonyl (acetone, formaldehyde and acetaldehyde) are below the safety levels. Besides that, the polysorbate 80 is a surfactant of MF59C.1 adjuvant and its content is measured during MF59C.1 release.

In accordance with Ph. Eur. 2619, a risk assessment to evaluate the presence of elemental impurities in aTIVc has been provided and is satisfactory.   A risk assessment was performed for all direct and indirect product-contact components used in the manufacturing processes for the aQIVc FP to determine the potential for N-nitrosamine introduction.  The process was considered to be of low risk with regard to nitrosamine impurities.

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There are seven non-compendial analytical procedures used for FP release (and stability) testing: Appearance (visual inspection), total protein (chemiluminescence), total DNA content (qPCR), HA content (SRD), squalene content RP-(UPLC), mean particle size (light scattering) and number of large particles (light obscuration with dynamic light scattering).

There are also seven compendial analytical procedures used for FP release (and stability) testing: pH, osmolality, endotoxin, sterility, visible particles, sub-visible particles and extractable volume.

All the non-compendial methods have been validated and all the compendial methods have been verified. A summary Table is provided indicating which tests are performed at the different manufacturing sites as well as a Table indicating the validation/verification reports provided.

The references standards used in the quality control test methods are obtained from official sources.

## Batch analysis

Because the aTIVc and aQIVc FP have the same product composition with the exception of an additional monovalent B strain in the aQIVc vaccine, the analysis results of several aQIVc FP formulation and filling PPQ batches from the different manufacturing sites and different presentations (SN and LL) are included. Batch analysis data for the final formulated bulk lots is provided. All analytical results passed acceptance criteria.

This approach is considered acceptable. All batches comply with the proposed specifications.

## Container closure

aTIVc is supplied as a single dose in a 1.5 mL OMPI EZ-fill Type I glass PFS with a Luer Lock-based (LL-PFS) integrated tip cap (ITC) or PFS with Staked Needle (SN-PFS), closed with a bromobutyl plunger stopper (BD FM257/2) attached to a plunger rod. A nominal volume of 1.0 mL dose of product is filled into each syringe.

For both PFSs, the primary packaging consists of an OMPI 1.5 mL syringe; the LL-PFS has a FM30 ITC and the SN-PFS has a 25G 1-inch staked needle covered by a rigid needle shield (RNS).

A description of the primary LL-PFS and SN-PFS container closure systems components and their manufacturers is provided. A representative Certification of Conformity for both syringes and plunger stopper is provided.

The components of the different parts of the syringes comply with Ph.Eur.

The silicone oil that covers the barrel and the stopper to provide easy and smooth plunger stopper motion complies with the Ph. Eur. monograph for Silicone Oil (Ph. Eur. 3.1.8).

With regards to the SN-PFS, the syringe includes a staked 25G × 1' stainless-steel AISI 304 needle. The needle is protected against contamination and damage by a rigid needle shield (RNS), which is made of polyisoprene rubber enclosed in a polypropylene cover.

Regarding sterilisation method, SN and LL syringe certificate of sterility mentions that they are sterilized with Ethylene Oxide and residues are in line with EMA/CHMP/CVMP/QWP/850374/2015 (Guideline on the sterilization of the medicinal product, active substance, excipient and primary container). Also, it is tested with regards to Ph.Eur requirement for sterility and endotoxins. They are both free from animal origin products. Stoppers are gamma irradiated to achieve at 10 -6  SAL before being assembled. This is acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

The stability studies for aQIVc are aimed to support licensure of the aTIVc product and to support a product expiry of 12 months, when stored at 2-8°C.

The conditions of the studies are:

- Long-term storage condition of 2-8°C for up to 15 months (time points: 1, 3, 6, 9, 12 and 15 months)
- Accelerated conditions (25°C ± 2°C 60% RH ± 5% RH) for up to 6 months (time points: 1, 3, 6 months)

Based on the current stability data, a shelf-life of 12 months was established for aQIVc FP in LL and SN PFS when stored at the long-term conditions (2-8ºC). Considering all mentioned, the aQIVc stability data presented supports an expiry of 12 months for aTIVc in both LL and SN PFS when stored at 28°C.

## 3.3.5. Adventitious agents

Three principal, complementary approaches are used by CSL Seqirus to prevent the potential viral (and no-viral) contamination of the aTIVc final medicinal product:

1. Selection of low-risk source materials. For the manufacturing of aTIVc, four materials of biological origin are used: MDCK 33016 PF cells, Influenza virus seed, Benzonase, and MF59C.1 adjuvant. Although these four raw materials may contain animal-derived substances, these materials were risk assessed in accordance with regulatory guidance and found to have a negligible impact on the overall product virus transmission risk and the overall product TSE risk.
2. Testing of cell lines, virus seeds, process intermediates, and bulk AS for contaminating virus at selected steps in the manufacturing process. Microbiological analyses are also performed at different manufacturing process steps, as well as the analysis of mycoplasma.
3. The manufacturing steps evaluated in the manufacturing process were inactivation and splitting. The results show a negligible risk of transmitting infectious particles of either the target virus (Influenza) for vaccine antigen production or contaminating viruses.

## Virus Safety conclusion

Due to selection of low-risk source materials, testing starting and raw materials for contaminating virus at selected steps in the manufacturing process, and the inclusion of steps with virus reduction capacity in the manufacturing process, it can be concluded that the risk of transmitting contaminating viruses is negligible.

## 3.4. Discussion on quality aspects

Seqirus has adequately described and documented all parts of the Quality module of the dossier.

aTIVc is a seasonal surface antigen, inactivated, adjuvanted with MF59C.1 Influenza vaccine prepared in cells. The vaccine is supplied as a single dose in a 1.5 mL glass pre-filled syringe containing 1 ml of sterile suspension for injection for active immunisation of adults of 50 years of age and older.

aTIVc contains predominantly purified HA and NA surface antigens from each of the three Influenza virus strains, types A (H1N1 and H3N2) and type B (Victoria lineage), recommended annually for immunisation by the WHO and the European Union (EU). The Influenza virus strains are individually grown in MDCK cells and inactivated using β -propiolactone (BPL) treatment before purification of the surface antigens and formulation with the MF59C.1 adjuvant. The MF59C.1 adjuvant is an oil-in-water emulsion composed of squalene as the oil phase, stabilized with the surfactants polysorbate 80 and sorbitan trioleate, in a citrate buffer.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The final product is a preservative-free trivalent influenza vaccine that contains 45 µg HA/dose/strain of three virus strains (H1N1, H3N2 and B/Victoria strain) and 0.5 mL MF59C.1/dose adjuvant in a 1.0 mL nominal dose in a 1.5 mL prefilled syringe.

Even though aTIVc is a high dose influenza vaccine, its active pharmaceutical ingredients are currently used in other CSL Seqirus products approved in EU: virus antigens are prepared from the MDCK cell FCC 3.0 AS as Flucelvax (TIVc), Celldemic (H5N1 zoonotic aMIVc) and Incellipan (H5N1 pandemic aMIVc), and the adjuvant MF59C.1 is also used in Fluad (aTIV), Fluad Tetra (aQIV), Aflunov, Foclivia, Celldemic, Incellipan and the zoonotic influenza vaccines Seqirus (H5N8) vaccines.

Although the current MAA is for a trivalent composition, all the clinical development was performed with a tetravalent composition (aQIVc). Based on the recommendation of the WHO (September 2023), Seqirus has modified the manufacturing process to remove the B/Yamagata strain resulting in a trivalent aTIVc FP. aQIVc manufacturing validation data has been presented in support of the current MAA for aTIVc. Since the product characteristics, stability and CQAs are the same between aTIVc and aQIVc, the aQIVc validation data are considered applicable to aTIVc.

In summary, from a quality point of view, aTIVc is approvable.

## 3.5. Conclusions on quality aspects

The quality of this product is considered to be acceptable when used in accordance with the conditions defined in the SmPC. Physicochemical and biological aspects relevant to the uniform clinical performance of the product have been investigated and are controlled in a satisfactory way. Data has been presented to give reassurance on viral/TSE safety.

## 3.6. Recommendation(s) for future quality development

In the context of the obligation of the MAHs to take due account of technical and scientific progress, as recommend by the CHMP, the Applicant commits to performing additional analytical and technical studies and providing the applicable requested data.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

The non-clinical development program for aTIVc, a trivalent, inactivated, adjuvanted influenza vaccine produced via cell culture and formulated with MF59C.1, supports its intended use in adults aged 50 years and older. Although most data were generated using the quadrivalent version (aQIVc), which shares the same manufacturing process and composition except for the B/Yamagata strain, the findings are considered applicable to aTIVc. The program included pharmacodynamic studies in mice, rabbits, and ferrets to evaluate immunogenicity and efficacy. Importantly, a repeat-dose toxicity study in rabbits was conducted under Good Laboratory Practice (GLP) conditions, confirming that aQIVc was immunogenic and well tolerated without signs of local or systemic toxicity. Additionally, the reproductive toxicity study conducted with TIVc (Study No. UBA00037) was performed in accordance with GLP standards. The adjuvant MF59C.1 has a well-established non-clinical safety profile from over two decades of use in licensed vaccines. Regulatory alignment was ensured through CHMP scientific advice, and a new Paediatric Investigation Plan was not required, as confirmed by the PDCO.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

Hemagglutination Inhibition (HI) assays were used in mice and rabbits to measure serum antibody responses to influenza antigens. The assay determines the highest serum dilution that inhibits hemagglutination. In mice, additional tests (ELISA, cytokine profiling, virus neutralization) further characterized the immune response.

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

The non-clinical pharmacology program for aTIVc aimed to assess immunogenicity and protective efficacy against influenza virus through in vivo studies in mice, rabbits, and ferrets. While species justification was not provided by the applicant, the use of these models aligns with established practices for similar vaccines (e.g., Flucelvax Tetra, Fluad Tetra), and no further rationale was deemed necessary.

None of the studies employed the full intended clinical dose. In mice, a reduced dose (0.3 μg HA and 0.2 mL MF59C.1) was used due to volume constraints. In the pivotal rabbit study three doses of aQIVc (identical to aTIVc except for the B/Yamagata strain) were administered at one-third antigen content and half the adjuvant volume compared to the human formulation. This deviates from WHO recommendations, which advocate for full-dose testing in animal models.

The vaccine's immunogenicity was supported by data from a ferret challenge study using aH1N1c. Ferrets primed with aTIVc showed enhanced immune responses to the 2009 A/H1N1 virus, although aTIVc alone did not confer protection. In mice, MF59C.1adjuvanted antigens induced a Th2-skewed response, characterized by dominant IgG1 titres and cytokine profiles (IL-5, TNFα, mild IFN - γ), consistent with known BALB/c mouse immunology and not considered a concern.

The absence of full-dose assessments and reliance on supportive efficacy data limit the robustness of the non-clinical PD package. However, given the availability of clinical data and the established profile of similar vaccines, further non-clinical studies are not required.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

The absence of secondary pharmacodynamics studies is deemed acceptable due to the nature of the product under assessment.

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

No dedicated safety pharmacology studies were conducted.

## 4.3.1.4. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

No drug interaction studies were conducted.

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

No dedicated pharmacokinetic studies were conducted for aTIVc, in accordance with the WHO Guideline on Nonclinical Evaluation of Vaccines (Technical Report Series No. 927, 2005).

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

N/A

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

N/A

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

N/A

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

N/A

## 4.3.2.6. Other pharmacokinetic studies

The vaccine includes the MF59C.1 adjuvant, whose oil phase contains squalene, a naturally occurring lipid involved in cholesterol biosynthesis. Literature data on MF59C.1-adjuvanted vaccines indicate low systemic exposure and independent clearance of the adjuvant from the antigen, with no accumulation or prolonged systemic presence observed.

## 4.4. Toxicology

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

No dedicated single-dose toxicology studies were performed. Safety endpoints were assessed upon administration of a single dose of the product in the GLP repeat-dose toxicity study. No local or systemic toxicity was evident.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.2. Repeat-dose toxicity

A GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicity study (Study No. 8411810) was conducted in New Zealand White rabbits using aQIVc, a quadrivalent formulation manufactured identically to aTIVc except for the inclusion of the B/Yamagata strain. Rabbits received three intramuscular doses spaced three weeks apart, exceeding the intended single-dose clinical regimen.

No mortality or systemic toxicity was observed. Mild, transient increases in neutrophil count, fibrinogen, and C-reactive protein were noted, correlating with expected immunogenic responses and injection site inflammation. Histopathological findings included increased splenic white pulp cellularity and mixed cell inflammation at injection sites, both of which showed reversibility by the end of the recovery phase. No specific organ toxicity was identified, and findings were considered non-adverse. Although a NOAEL could not be defined due to the single dose level tested, the study supports the tolerability of the vaccine, with exposure in rabbits exceeding human exposure on a mg/kg basis for both antigen and adjuvant.

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

Genotoxicity studies were not performed for aTIVc due to the nature of the product and the expected use in the clinical setting (single shot).

MF59C.1 is not genotoxic based on in vitro (Ames assay) and in vivo (mouse micronucleus assay) testing. Toxicity testing of MF59C.1 is discussed in section 4.9.8 of the AR (other toxicity studies).

## 4.4.4. Carcinogenicity

Carcinogenicity studies were not performed due to the nature of the product.

## 4.4.5. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

Dedicated DART studies were not conducted with aTIVc. Instead, the assessment relies on a weight-ofevidence approach using data from a GLP-compliant reproductive toxicity study performed with TIVc (Study No. UBA00037), which shares the same manufacturing platform as aTIVc but lacks the MF59C.1 adjuvant. In this study, female rabbits received five intramuscular doses of TIVc (three before mating and two during gestation) at the full clinical dose (45 µg HA total). The study evaluated fertility, implantation, gestation, parturition, lactation, and postnatal development. No treatment-related effects were observed in reproductive organs, maternal behavior, or offspring development. Although a slightly higher incidence of maternal mortality and litter loss was noted, these events were attributed to reduced feed intake and were not considered treatment-related. The absence of adverse findings supports the tolerability of the antigen component.

Additionally, MF59C.1 was assessed in separate GLP reproductive toxicity studies in rats and rabbits (Study Nos. 1303-001P and 1303-002), which showed no teratogenic or developmental toxicity.

Overall, the conclusions regarding reproductive safety are based on specific endpoints from repeatdose toxicity studies and dedicated studies with individual components. The combined data support the safe use of aTIVc in women of childbearing potential and pregnant individuals, in line with current regulatory guidelines.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.6. Toxicokinetics and exposure margins

No dedicated toxicokinetic (TK) studies were conducted for aTIVc, in line with WHO guidelines which do not require serum antigen concentration measurements for vaccines. Instead, immunogenicity was confirmed via Hemagglutination Inhibition (HI) assays in rabbits (Study No. 8411810), demonstrating adequate exposure.

Exposure margins were calculated based on body weight-normalized doses of antigen and adjuvant. In the repeat-dose toxicity study, rabbits received 60 µg total HA antigen and 0.25 mL MF59C.1 (9.75 mg squalene), equating to 20 µg/kg HA and 0.083 mL/kg adjuvant. In comparison, the human clinical dose is 135 µg HA and 0.5 mL MF59C.1 (19.5 mg squalene), corresponding to 2.25 µg/kg HA and 0.0083 mL/kg adjuvant for a 60 kg adult. This results in exposure margins of approximately 8.9-fold for HA antigen and 10-fold for MF59C.1 adjuvant in rabbits relative to humans.

These margins support the safety of the vaccine, as no adverse effects were observed in the animal studies, and the doses tested exceeded those intended for clinical use.

## 4.4.7. Local tolerance

Local tolerance was assessed as part of the GLP repeat-dose toxicity study (Study No. 8411810) conducted in rabbits with aQIVc. No stand-alone local tolerance studies were performed. Injection site reactions were monitored clinically and histologically. Mild erythema, desquamation, and oedema were observed, consistent with expected vaccine reactogenicity. Histopathology revealed minimal to moderate mixed cell inflammation at injection sites, which was reduced in severity and incidence by the end of the recovery phase. These findings were considered non-adverse and indicative of the intended immunogenic response.

## 4.4.8. Other toxicity studies

No dedicated antigenicity or immunotoxicity studies were conducted, which is acceptable given the nature of the product as a vaccine intended to elicit an immune response.

No studies on dependence, metabolites, phototoxicity, or excipients were performed, and their absence is endorsed.

Additional toxicity studies were conducted on MF59C.1, the adjuvant used in aTIVc, including repeatdose, genotoxicity, reproductive toxicity, safety pharmacology, and sensitization studies across multiple species. MF59C.1 was well tolerated, with reversible local inflammation and no systemic toxicity, genotoxicity, or teratogenic effects.

Furthermore, studies on the MDCK cell culture platform confirmed no oncogenic risk from residual cell components.

Impurity assessments for aQIVc HD (used as a surrogate for aTIVc) identified acceptable safety margins for total protein, CTAB, and PS80, although further justification regarding thresholds and relevance to aTIVc is requested.

## 4.4.9. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

The active substance is a natural substance, the use of which will not alter the concentration or distribution of the substance in the environment. Therefore, aTIVc is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

## Pharmacology

The non-clinical pharmacology programme for aTIVc was designed to assess two main aspects: the vaccine's immunogenicity and its protective efficacy against influenza virus challenge. These aspects were evaluated through studies conducted in mice, rabbits and ferrets. A justification of the species selection was not provided by the applicant but given the well-known pharmacological properties of similar products (Flucelvax Tetra -QIVc- or Fluad Tetra -aQIV-) and considering that those species are commonly used for the non-clinical development of vaccines, no additional information and justification was requested in this regard from the applicant.

aTIVc contains predominantly haemagglutinin (HA) and neuraminidase (NA) surface antigens from three influenza strains: Type A/H1N1, Type A/H3N2 and Type B Victoria Lineage. It shares the same MDCK manufacturing platform as aQIVc and differs only by the removal of the B/Yamagata strain. Therefore, the non-clinical data generated with aQIVc are considered supportive of the assessment of aTIVc.

None of the conducted studies evaluated the full clinical dose. Studies in mice assessed the impact of using MF59C.1 as an adjuvant for cell culture-derived antigens on the immune response.

Because most of the existing data of MF59C.1 immunogenicity were generated with egg-derived antigens, the effect of MF59C.1 on cell culture-derived antigens was investigated in mice. Wack et al. 2008 evaluated a range of adjuvants for their ability to enhance, in naïve mice, protective hemagglutination inhibition (HI) titres, virus-neutralizing titres and T-cell responses to a new generation influenza vaccine produced in cell culture (FCC) comprising two A virus strain and one B virus strain as the proposed clinical product. 8-12 female 8-week-old Balb/c mice were immunized intramuscularly at days 0 and 28 with 0.3 µg of either soluble MDCK-cell derived trivalent antigen or mixed with MF59. The adjuvant dose used in these studies has not been detailed, nor has there been a study to determine the dose of MF59 or the antigen. Although the design of the study does not allow to ascertain the added value of both the adjuvant and the high content of antigen, the limitations of this study are not an issue as other nonclinical studies (i.e. rabbits) are considered sufficient for the nonclinical assessment of the vaccine pharmacology. Furthermore, the reduced dosage may be considered acceptable due to volume limitations associated with administration in small animals.

Notably, the pivotal pharmacodynamic study conducted in rabbits (using aQIVc, which is manufactured identically to aTIVc except for the inclusion of the B/Yamagata strain) also did not utilize the full clinical dose. The formulation administered contained only onethird of the antigen content (15 μg HA per strain) and half the adjuvant volume (0.25 mL of MF59C.1 [9.75 mg squalene]) compared to the human formulation (45 μg HA per strain and 0.5 mL of MF59C.1 [19.5 mg squalene]). As per WHO guidelines, the full intended clinical dose should be assessed in animal models in absolute terms.

Importantly, the dosing schedule used in the study (three administrations spaced at least three weeks apart) exceeded the intended clinical regimen, in which subjects will receive only a single dose.

Regarding the efficacy, only supportive data were provided. The adjuvanted cell culture-derived H1N1 pandemic vaccine (aH1N1c) was evaluated in a challenge study in ferrets that had been either primed or not with aTIVc. According to data presented, priming with the seasonal vaccine, although insufficient on its own to confer protection against 2009 A/H1N1 virus, successfully enhanced the immunogenicity and efficacy of the 2009 A/H1N1 vaccine.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

It is well-established that BALB/C mice exhibit a Th2-skewed immune response. As reported by Wack et al., 2008, two weeks post-second dose of aTIVc, neutralizing antibody titres were predominantly IgG1 over IgG2a, and cytokine secretion was dominated by IL5 and TNF-alpha, with only mild IFNgamma production observed. This Th2-bias is well documented in the literature and does not pose a concern for the nonclinical characterization of the vaccine.

Even though the nonclinical pharmacology package for aTIVc is somewhat limited due to the absence of assessment of the full intended clinical dose in animal models, the availability of clinical data renders additional nonclinical studies unnecessary.

The absence of secondary pharmacodynamic studies, safety pharmacology assessments, and pharmacodynamic drug interaction evaluations is considered acceptable due to the nature of the product under assessment.

## Pharmacokinetics

Pharmacokinetic studies have not been conducted for aTIVc. As stated in the WHO Guideline on Nonclinical Evaluation of Vaccines (WHO Technical Report Series No. 927, 2005), PK studies for determining serum or tissue concentrations of vaccines components are not required.

aTIVc is formulated with the MF59C.1 adjuvant, whose oil phase consists of squalene-a naturally occurring compound found in vegetable and fish oils, and an intermediate in cholesterol biosynthesis. Literature studies conducted with MF59C.1 and different antigens showed low systemic exposure and independent clearance from the antigen.

## Toxicology

The absence of single dose toxicity studies is endorsed.

Regarding repeat-dose toxicity, no pivotal safety studies have been conducted with aTIVc. Instead, the applicant submitted a GLP-compliant toxicology study in rabbits using aQIVc, which is manufactured by the same process as aTIVc and differs only by the inclusion of the B/Yamagata strain. In this study, aQIVc was administered intramuscularly on three occasions at three-week intervals. This dosing schedule exceeds the intended clinical use of aTIVc, which consists of a single administration. The total study duration was eight weeks, including a two-week recovery period.

The study design is endorsed, as the dosing regimen maximised animal exposure to the candidate vaccine. The use of aQIVc as a surrogate for the toxicology assessment of aTIVc is acceptable, given that the two products share the same manufacturing process and differ only by the inclusion of the B/Yamagata strain in aQIVc. Although the use of aQIVc as a surrogate for the toxicology assessment of aTIVc is acceptable, the lots used in the non-clinical studies cannot be considered fully representative of the final clinical lots intended for marketing. In particular, the full clinical dose of aQIVc was not tested, despite rabbits being of sufficient size to allow administration of the full dose, if necessary, by splitting the dose across two injection sites on the same day. However, at the time the non-clinical study was conducted, the batches were representative of the clinical scenario, with dose adjustments taking place at later stages of clinical development of aQIVc.

In the toxicology study for aQIVc, the formulation administered (referred to as aQIVc (15,1) for simplification) contained only onethird of the antigen content (15 μg HA per strain) and half the adjuvant volume (0.25 mL of MF59C.1 [9.75 mg squalene]) compared to the final human formulation (45 μg HA per strain and 0.5 mL of MF59C.1 [19.5 mg squalene], referred to as aQIVc (45,2)). With this, the total antigen content in the clinical scenario for aQIVc corresponds to 180 μg HA. The dose aQIVc (15,1) assessed in the non-clinical study in rabbits corresponded to the full clinical dose used in the proof-of-concept, first-in-human study (V200\\_10) in absolute terms. However, a subsequent dose range finding study (V201\\_01) determined that the vaccine formulation containing 45 µg HA/strain and MF59C.1 with 19.5 mg squalene (45,2) was associated with the highest antigen-specific immune response of the tested dosages. This formulation was selected for testing in Phase 3 studies and is the one covered by this MAA. According to the applicant, clinical data demonstrated that the aQIVc (45,2) formulation, containing 45 μg of HA per strain and 0.5 mL of MF59C.1, elicited the highest overall immune response when compared with other doses, including the non-clinical aQIVc (15,1).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

According to WHO guidelines (WHO Technical Report No. 927, 2005: WHO Guideline on Nonclinical Evaluation of Vaccines), the full intended clinical dose should be evaluated in animal models in absolute terms, if feasible. Alternatively, it might be acceptable a dose that exceeds the human dose on a mg/kg basis and that induces an immune response in the animal model used. In this case, the dose tested in rabbits exceeded the human dose on a mg/kg basis by a factor of 2.2 for each individual antigen and 8.9 for the total HA. Regarding the adjuvant, the dose in rabbits was 10 times higher than the dose administered to humans.

Considering the totality of the data, the number of doses administered in the non-clinical study exceeding the doses in humans, the amount of antigen administered in a mg/kg basis and with the lack of relevant toxicological findings aQIVc-related, no further non-clinical data is required.

Due to the nature of the product and its single-dose clinical use, the absence of genotoxicity and carcinogenicity studies is considered acceptable and consistent with regulatory expectations for influenza vaccines.

DART studies with the product under assessment have not been conducted. However, the applicant has provided DART studies of aTIVc components (i.e. TIVc and MF59). A DART study with the product TIVc, which differs to aTIVc only by the absence of MF59C.1 adjuvant, was already assessed as part of the MAA of Flucelvax and therefore the overall design of the study has been already endorsed, with animals being administered TIVc before mating and during gestation, as indicated in current adopted guidelines (EMA/CHMP/VWP/457259/2014). No remarkable findings were uncovered during the study, attributable to TIVc administration. In DART studies conducted with MF59 in pregnant rats and rabbits treated with multiple doses, no relevant toxicological findings were detected.

As for children, developmental toxicity studies are usually not required for vaccines intended for immunization during childhood. In addition, the absence of findings in the study supported the administration of the product to children. Following the proposed therapeutic indication for aTIVc 'prophylaxis of influenza in adults 50 years of age and older', no further DART studies are required.

New Zealand White rabbits included in the pivotal toxicity study were approximately 17 weeks of age before dosing. This is in accordance with current guidance (WHO Technical Report No. 927, 2005: WHO Guideline on Nonclinical Evaluation of Vaccines), which recommends an age for rabbits at the start of the study of 3 to 4 months. Given that safety studies conducted in juvenile animals are usually not required for vaccines and having in mind the age of the animals employed in the pivotal toxicity study, no further clarification is requested.

Following current guidance, studies to determine concentration of antigens during the toxicity studies are not required for vaccines. In the GLP toxicity study (Study 8411810), immune response to the aQIVc vaccine was confirmed by a hemagglutination inhibition (HI) assay.

For the non-clinical development of vaccines, guidelines recommend using in the pivotal safety studies the highest dose to be used in the clinical trials (if feasible) or alternatively, a dose that exceeds the human dose on mg/kg basis and induces an immune response in the animals. For the specific case of aTIVc, the applicant has used aQIVc as a surrogate (differing only in the elimination of the B/Yamagata strain but otherwise having identical manufacture) and performed a comparison based on the mg/kg of HA antigen and ml/kg of adjuvant. Also, the number of doses administered to the animals exceed the number of doses proposed in humans (N+2).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Considering the totality of the data, as mentioned before, it can be agreed that the exposure in animals is superior in 8.9 times (with respect to HA) and 10 times (for adjuvant) as compared to the expected use in the clinical scenario.

The absence of stand-alone local toxicity studies is endorsed as this was assessed during the repeat dose toxicity study in rabbits, conducted with aQIVc as a surrogate for aTIVc. Although the data provided pointed out to a higher incidence of findings in the aQIVc dose animals, those were attributed to the expected variation of the study and were consistent with the administration of an immunogenic vaccine by this route

Given the nature and intended use of the product, the absence of antigenicity, immunotoxicity, dependence, and metabolite studies is considered acceptable and consistent with regulatory expectations for vaccines.

The applicant has provided a report assessing the potential impurities expected to be present in the product. The impurities selected for further toxicological analysis were chosen based on predicted amounts present in aQIVc HD manufacture.

The absence of phototoxicity and excipients studies is endorsed.

The amount of squalene tested in the toxicity study 90-6081 was higher than the amount included in the formulation of aTIVc (19.5 mg). A comparison with the proposed human dose for pregnant women showed that the ratios between human and non-clinical species doses with the lowest amount of squalene are sufficiently large to ensure adequate safety margins, that would be at least 5.5x.

For the MDCK cells, the risk for a person receiving a single annual shot of the vaccine manufactured in the MDCK cell culture platform is almost negligible and no further studies are required.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

Pharmacology studies conducted with aTIVc demonstrated immunogenicity in mice and rabbits, as well as protective efficacy in ferrets. Although certain limitations were identified in the assessment, the overall data package, combined with clinical experience, is deemed sufficient.

Pharmacokinetic studies are not required for aTIVc, and available literature data on MF59C.1 confirm low systemic exposure and independent clearance.

Toxicity studies are acceptable and adequate safety margins for both the antigen and the adjuvant MF59C.1 are established.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5. Clinical aspects

## 5.1. Introduction

## 5.1.1. GCP aspects

The Clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the applicant

Based on the review of clinical data the CHMP did not identify the need for a GCP inspection of the clinical trials included in this dossier (see section 2.4.3).
