## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.1. Methods

No classical clinical pharmacology or pharmacokinetic study was performed in the development of aTIVc because the kinetic properties of influenza vaccines do not provide useful information for establishing adequate dosing recommendations (EMEA/CHMP/VWP/164653/05 Rev.1). Instead, this section provides a summary of assays used for immunogenicity assessments across studies, and an overview of selection of the optimum aTIVc antigen and adjuvant dose and formulation.

Immunogenicity by validated haemagglutination inhibition (HI) antibody assay was assessed in all studies (V201\\_03, V201\\_07, V201\\_01, and V200\\_10).

Serum samples were analysed for HI antibody against homologous vaccine strains using cell-derived target viruses according to standard methods in all four studies. Cell-derived target viruses are known to be closer in antigenic match to circulating wildtype strains compared to egg-derived target viruses, thus immune responses to cell-derived target virus correlate better with clinical protection and are therefore more relevant from a public health perspective.

Samples were also analysed for HI antibody against vaccine strains using egg-derived target viruses in

studies V201\\_03, and V200\\_10, where an egg-based vaccine (aQIV) was used as a comparator. Assays with egg-derived target viruses were included to demonstrate consistency or correlation of immune responses between cell- and egg-derived target viruses. Consistent with the established routine for influenza vaccines, key immunological responses were assessed at baseline (i.e., prevaccination) and approximately 4 weeks after vaccination, when the hemagglutinin (HA) antibodies are at their highest levels in humans. To assess the duration of immune responses, serum samples were also assessed at 6, 9, and 12 months after vaccination in pivotal study V201\\_03.

As evolutionary changes in the amino acids of the surface proteins HA and neuraminidase (NA) of recent circulating A/H3N2 strains have resulted in a diminished capacity of HA to agglutinate red blood cells (RBCs) in the HI assay, or agglutination has been shown to be mediated by NA, all A/H3N2 strains were tested with oseltamivir to exclude NA mediated agglutination of red blood cells. A/H3N2 strains that displayed sensitivity to oseltamivir were assessed using the ELISA based virus microneutralization (EMN) assay (also referred to as MN assay) as the primary assay for the assessment of the immune response against homologous vaccine strains using cell-derived target viruses. The A/H3N2 seasonal strain failed to agglutinate in V200\\_10 and V201\\_01 studies, thus HI assays were not conducted for A/H3N2 in these studies. MN assays were conducted for all strains in all studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Samples are analysed for neutralizing antibody against non-homologous (also referred to as heterologous) cell-derived target viruses in studies V201\\_03, V201\\_01, and V200\\_10 as an exploratory endpoint (A/H3N2 strains).

Enzyme-linked lectin assay (ELLA) was used in studies V201\\_03 and V200\\_10 (though not validated for study V200\\_10) and flow cytometry analyses were performed in study V201\\_03 only.

A single laboratory (VisMederi Strada del Petriccio e Belriguardo 35 Siena 53100, Italy and Zona Industriale Belvedere SNC, Ingresso 2, 53034, Colle di Val d'Elsa, Siena, Italy) performed the assays and analyses for studies V201\\_03, V201\\_07, V201\\_01, and V200\\_10. All assay validation reports were provided.

## Sample Collection and Assay Methods

## Collection of Serology Samples

Blood samples for analysis of immune responses were collected in all studies at baseline before vaccine injection on Day 1, and 4 weeks after vaccination (Day 29). To assess the duration of immune responses, serum samples were also collected at 6, 9 and 12 months in pivotal study V201\\_03; results of these ongoing analyses will be provided when they become available. Serum was separated from the blood samples. The serum samples were stored at -20 ℃ and shipped to the laboratory for serum antibody analysis.

## Hemagglutination Inhibition (HI) Assay

The Hemagglutination inhibition (HI) assay is based on the ability of influenza viruses to bind to RBCs and cause them to clump or \"agglutinate\" giving a characteristic cloudy presentation in the well. However, in the presence of sufficient amount of specific anti-HA antibodies the binding of the virus to the red blood is blocked, preventing the agglutination and leading to characteristic 'red dot' at the bottom of the well and interpreted as a positive well result for the presence of anti-HA antibodies. The serum HI titre is defined as the reciprocal of the highest serum dilution that completely inhibits hemagglutination.

## ELISA-based Microneutralization (EMN) Assay

The enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) based microneutralization (EMN) assay for influenza viruses is a method used to detect the presence of virus specific neutralizing antibodies in a sample. In this assay, serum samples are first incubated with a known amount of live influenza virus, allowing present specific antibodies to neutralize the virus. The mixture is then added to a monolayer of susceptible cells, typically in a 96-well plate. After incubating serum samples with the virus and adding the mixture to susceptible cells, cells are fixed and an ELISA is performed to detect viral antigens within the cells. If neutralizing antibodies in the serum effectively block the virus, the production of viral proteins will be reduced, resulting in a lower ELISA signal. Conversely, if the virus is not neutralized, viral replication occurs, leading to a higher concentration of viral proteins and a stronger ELISA signal. The neutralizing antibody titre is determined by the highest dilution of serum that reduces the ELISA signal by 50% relative to virus control wells.

## Enzyme-linked lectin assay (ELLA)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The Enzyme-Linked Lectin Assay (ELLA) is used to measure the activity of the neuraminidase (NA) enzyme from influenza viruses, which plays a critical role in viral release from infected cells. The assay is based on the ability of enzymatically active neuraminidase (present on the surface of a HA mismatched pseudovirus) to cleave glycoproteins that contain sialic acid residues found on the surface. Following cleavage, lectin, which is conjugated to an enzyme, can bind to the cleaved sialic acid residues. The amount of lectin bound, measured via a colorimetric or fluorometric signal, is inversely proportional to the neuraminidase activity. The titre is recorded as the highest serum dilution that is less than 50% the signal observed in the NA-only control wells.

## Collection of Peripheral Blood Mononuclear Cells (PBMC) Samples

Peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) were collected at predefined time points: Day 1 (prior to vaccination), and on Days 8, 15, 29, and 181 to assess cell-mediated immune (CMI) responses. PBMCs were isolated from whole blood using density gradient centrifugation. Whole blood was diluted with buffer and layered over Ficoll-Paque, followed by centrifugation to separate the mononuclear cell layer. The PBMC fraction was carefully collected, washed to remove residual plasma and platelets, and resuspended in cryopreservation medium. Isolated PBMCs were then cryopreserved in the vapor phase of liquid nitrogen at -180°C and shipped to a central laboratory for CMI analysis.

## Flow cytometry (Cell-mediated immunity [CMI])

The flow cytometry-based antigen-specific T cell detection assay is used to identify and characterize antigen-specific T cells. In the context of vaccine development, this assay evaluates T cell responses induced by the vaccine antigen. PBMCs are collected from participants at various time points pre- and post-vaccination, cryopreserved, and later thawed for analysis. The thawed cells are cultured with a peptide pool representing the target antigen, enabling antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the PBMCs to present peptides via MHC-I or MHC-II pathways. This presentation stimulates T cell receptors (TCRs), leading to the expression of activation markers and the production of cytokines. Flow cytometry is then employed to detect these activation markers and cytokines, enabling the identification and characterization of antigen-specific T cells. The assay provides detailed insights into T cell populations following vaccination, including their functional subsets. For example, Th1 cells are identified by cytokine production profiles such as interferon gamma (IFNγ), TNF - α, and interleukin 2 (IL-2), while Th2 cells are characterized by interleukin 4 (IL-4) production.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

No classical pharmacokinetic study was performed in the development of aTIVc because the kinetic properties of influenza vaccines do not provide useful information for establishing adequate dosing recommendations (EMEA/CHMP/VWP/164653/05 Rev.1).

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

aTIVc provides active immunisation against three influenza virus strains (two A subtypes and one B type) contained in the vaccine. It induces humoral antibodies against the haemagglutinins. These antibodies neutralise influenza viruses. This vaccine contains the adjuvant MF59C.1 (MF59).

## Mechanism of action - MF59 adjuvant

MF59 is an oil-in-water emulsion composed of squalene stabilized by both a water-soluble surfactant, polysorbate 80 (PS80), and an oil-soluble surfactant, sorbitan trioleate, in a low ionic strength citrate buffer. MF59 is widely approved for use in seasonal and pandemic influenza vaccines across many countries and has a well-established safety profile. MF59 enhances immune responses to antigen by targeting three different cell types, namely monocytes, macrophages, and granulocytes. MF59 has a range of effects on these cells, including increased antigen uptake, release of chemoattractants, and induction of cell differentiation. The mechanism of action of MF59 involves the induction of a transient immunocompetent local environment within the muscle, which promotes potent immune responses by primarily inducing antibodies and increasing CD4 T cell responses with an unbiased Th1/Th2-profile in humans. The MF59 in aQIVc/aTIVc is the same adjuvant that is included in Fluad (aTIV), Fluad Quadrivalent/Tetra (aQIV), Audenz/Incellipan and Celldemic (aH5N1c), and Foclivia and Aflunov (aH5N1).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

No PK/PD study was performed in the development of aTIVc because this kind of studies do not provide useful information for establishing adequate dosing recommendations (EMEA/CHMP/VWP/164653/05 Rev.1).

## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

aTIVc includes 45 μg of each HA antigen (A/H1N1, A/H3N2, and B/Victoria) and MF59 (containing 19.5 mg of squalene) in a volume of 1.0 mL, formulated in a pre-filled syringe.

CSL Seqirus initiated the aQIVc clinical development with the proof-of-concept study V200\\_10. The comparators used in this study were Flucelvax Quadrivalent QIVc, Fluad Quadrivalent aQIV, and Flublok Quadrivalent (QIVr). The first aQIVc formulation (15,1) tested in this study contained the standard hemagglutinin dose, similar to QIVc (15 μg of HA/strain) and the standard adjuvant dose, found in aQIV (containing 9.75 mg squalene). The study showed that addition of adjuvant to QIVc improved the immune response in subjects aged 50 years and older.

Following confirmation of proof-of-concept, the dose-ranging study V201\\_01 was initiated, investigating 7 vaccine formulations based on combinations of 3 dosages of antigen (15 µg, 30 µg, and 45 µg HA/strain) and 3 dosages of MF59 adjuvant (containing 9.75 mg, 19.5 mg, and 29.25 mg squalene). In this study, the vaccine formulation containing 45 µg HA/strain and MF59 with 19.5 mg squalene was associated with the highest antigen-specific immune response of the tested dosages, with no safety concerns identified. This aQIVc HD formulation was also determined to have the optimal immunogenicity/safety profile in a Phase 2b dose-confirmation study with a larger sample size (Study V201\\_07).

Results of study V201\\_01 and study V201\\_07 indicated that the aQIVc HD formulation is associated with an enhanced antigen-specific immune response compared with the comparator vaccine QIVc, with no safety concerns identified. As a result, this high-dose formulation was selected for testing in a Phase 3 study aiming to meet the objectives to support the licensure of aQIVc for use in adults aged 50 years and older (Study V201\\_03). Following WHO recommendations, CSL Seqirus will transition from aQIVc to aTIVc, and will not include B/Yamagata antigen in the final product.

## 5.2.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.6.1. Discussion

No classical clinical pharmacology or pharmacokinetic studies were performed with aTIVc/aQIVc and this is endorsed, since these are not required for vaccines, as detailed in the CHMP guideline 'Guideline on Clinical Evaluation of New Vaccines' (EMEA/CHMP/VWP/164653/2005).

Assays supporting Immunogenicity Assessment The pharmacodynamic profile for vaccines is defined by their immunogenicity profile. To define the immunogenicity profile of aTIVc the clinical studies included four different assays performed with the quadrivalent (aQIVc) vaccine:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- The Hemagglutination Inhibition (HI) Assay is the traditional assay for influenza vaccines, as per the CHMP Guideline on Influenza Vaccines - Non-clinical and clinical module. The HI assay using cell-derived target virus is the primary immunogenicity endpoint for all studies, but an assay with egg-derived target virus is also used in two studies (V200\\_10 and V201\\_03). The comparison of the results with both assays is meaningful as the cell-based one could favour aTIVc over the egg-based comparator vaccine. The HI antibody response was considered an acceptable surrogate marker of activity reasonably predictive of clinical benefit.
- The ELISA-based Microneutralization (EMN) Assay complies with the CHMP guideline's requirement that neutralizing antibodies are determined in all influenza studies. It is also the primary assay for A/H3N2 strain in two studies where the virus failed to agglutinate (V200\\_10 and V201\\_01). The failed agglutination of H3N2 HA is not uncommon, and the substitution of the HI assay for EMN is standard practice.
- The Enzyme-Linked Lectin Assay (ELLA) is used to measure the neuraminidase activity in studies V201\\_03 and V200\\_10 (though not validated for study V200\\_10).
- Cell-mediated immunity (CMI) is determined by flow cytometry after antigen-specific stimulation of T cells and includes the quantity and quality of T cell response. Flow cytometry was used in study V201\\_03 only.

All assays across all studies were performed by the same central laboratory, and this is good practice that complies with the CHMP Guideline on Influenza Vaccines - Non-clinical and Clinical Module (EMA/CHMP/VWP/457259/2014).

The validation reports for all assays were submitted showing that the methods were suitable for intended use.

## Dose selection

Dose selection for aTIVc was based on three phase 2 clinical studies in a stepwise approach for dose development for HA antigen and MF59 adjuvant dose:

- V200\\_10, a proof-of-concept study that showed the potential benefit of including MF59 adjuvant in a cell-based influenza vaccine.
- V201\\_01, a study that tested seven different formulations: 3 antigen dosages (15, 30 and 45 µg HA/strain) and 3 adjuvant dosages (9.75, 19.5 and 29.25 mg of squalene). This study showed that the most immunogenic formulation was the highest antigen dose (45 µg HA/strain) and the medium adjuvant dose (19.5 mg of squalene, equivalent of 2 times the adjuvant dosage of the authorised Fluad).
- V201\\_07 a dose confirmation study testing three different vaccine formulations, all with 45 µg HA/strain and different adjuvant dosage (no adjuvant, 9.75 or 19.5 mg of squalene). This study confirmed 45 µg HA/strain and 19.5 mg of squalene as the final vaccine formulation to be tested in Phase III studies.

The dose selection for the Phase III study based on these studies is considered adequate. First, it was shown that increasing the HA dose alone is not sufficient to increase the immune response. Then, a series of different formulations and doses of HA and MF59 were evaluated. Increasing the HA antigen dose to 3X and MF59 dose to 2X provided an enhanced immune response. Finally, the highest antigen dose and different doses of adjuvant were tested. In the last study, only the aQIVc 45,2 formulation was superior to the QIVc for all strains in the vaccine, and this strength has been progressed in the Phase 3 study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.6.2. Conclusions

No classical clinical pharmacology or pharmacokinetic studies were performed with aTIVc and this is endorsed, since these are not required for vaccines as stated in the CHMP Guideline on clinical evaluation of vaccines.

The assays used to evaluate the immunogenicity of the vaccine are considered adequate and validation reports have been submitted.

The Phase 2 clinical studies support the dose selected.

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

## 5.3.1. Dose response study(ies)

## 5.3.1.1. Study V200\\_10

## Overall Study Design and Plan

Study V200\\_10 was a Phase 2, randomized, stratified, controlled, observer-blind, multi-center proof-ofconcept study in approximately 480 male and female adults aged 50 years and older who were healthy or  had  comorbidities  which  increased  their  risk  of  complications  from  influenza  infection.  The investigational vaccine was aQIVc (15,1), ie, 15 μg of each HA antigen and MF59 adjuvant (containing 9.75 mg of squalene), with QIVc (15,0; containing 15 µg HA antigen and no MF59), aQIV (containing 15 µg HA antigen and MF59 containing 9.75 mg of squalene) and QIVr (containing 45 µg HA antigen and no MF59) as comparator vaccines; although aQIVc (45,2) was not tested in this study, the study is included here as it provides the initial proof-of-concept that addition of adjuvant to QIVc improves the immune response in subjects aged 50 years and older.

Study  V200\\_10  aimed  to  perform  an  evaluation  of  the  immunogenicity  and  safety  of  the  MF59adjuvanted quadrivalent cell-based vaccine (aQIVc (15,1) versus three other licensed influenza vaccines: the non-adjuvated quadrivalent cell-based influenza vaccine (QIVc (15,0); Flucelvax Quadrivalent), the MF59-adjuvanted  quadrivalent  egg-based  vaccine  (aQIV;  Fluad   Quadrivalent)  and  the  quadrivalent recombinant influenza vaccine (QIVr; Flublok Quadrivalent). Immunogenicity and safety were assessed in the overall study population (adults ≥50 years of age) and in the age subgroups 50 - 64 years and ≥65 years.

Study V200\\_10 was conducted in 471 male and female subjects. Subjects were randomized to receive aQIVc (15,1), QIVc (15,0), aQIV, or QIVr in a 1:1:1:1 ratio as planned, with 116 subjects receiving aQIVc  (15,1),  119  subjects  receiving  QIVc  (15,0),  116  subjects  receiving  aQIV,  and  120  subjects receiving QIVr. Stratification factors were applied for age (50 to 64 and ≥65 years) and history of any influenza vaccination within 3 previous influenza seasons (yes/no).

## Study Objectives

The primary immunogenicity objective was to assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc (15,1) in comparison with the QIVc (15,0), aQIV, and QIVr comparator vaccines as measured by HI assay using cell-derived target virus at Day 29, in subjects 5064 and ≥65 years of age and overall (adults ≥50 years). Due to lack  of  agglutination  for  A/H3N2  strain  in  HI  assay,  immunogenicity  for  that  strain  was assessed  as measured by MN assay as an acceptable alternative.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The  secondary  immunogenicity  objectives  were  to  assess  the  immunogenicity  of  aQIVc  (15,1)  in comparison with the QIVc (15,0), aQIV, and QIVr comparator vaccines as measured by HI assay using cell-derived target virus at Day 181, in subjects 5064 and ≥65 y ears of age and overall and to assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc (15,1) in comparison with the QIVc (15,0), aQIV, and QIVr comparator vaccines as measured by MN assay using cell-derived target virus at Day 29 and Day 181, in subjects 5064 and ≥65 years o f age and overall.

Additional and exploratory immunogenicity analyses were to assess:

- the immunogenicity of aQIVc (15,1) in comparison with the QIVc (15,0), aQIV and QIVr comparator vaccines as measured by HI assay, to be assessed using egg-derived target viruses at Day 29, in subjects 5064 and ≥65 years of age and overall.
- the immunogenicity of aQIVc (15,1) in comparison with the QIVc (15,0), aQIV and QIVr comparator vaccines as measured by MN assay, to be assessed using three A/H3N2 heterologous strains at Day 29, in subjects 5064 and ≥65 years of age and overall.
- the immunogenicity of aQIVc (15,1) in comparison with the QIVc (15,0) comparator vaccines as anti-neuraminidase (NA) titre measured by enzyme-linked lectin assay (ELLA), to be assessed using cell-derived target virus at Day 29, in subjects 5064 and ≥65 years of age and overall.

## Evaluation of Immunogenicity

The primary immunogenicity objective was assessed by HI assay for all strains using cell-derived target virus homologous to the seasonal vaccine. In case of lack of agglutination for a specific strain using HI assay, immunogenicity for that strain was to be measured by MN assay. The assays were conducted on serum samples collected on Day 1 (before vaccination, baseline), and Day 29.

Serological specimens taken from subjects at baseline (Day 1) and after vaccination (Day 29 and Day 181) were used for measurement of immune responses using the HI (or MN) assay. The Per Protocol Set (PPS) was the main population for the primary, secondary, and subgroup immunogenicity analyses. No formal hypotheses were tested in the study and no thresholds for success were defined up front.

## Datasets Analysed

The  All  Enrolled  Set  consisted  of  471  subjects  who  were  enrolled  in  the  study.  There  were  no immunogenicity data on Day 29 in 4 subjects and one subject was excluded due to study withdrawal; therefore, the FAS consisted of 466 subjects for the analysis at Day 29. The PPS at Day 29 consisted of 449 subjects from the FAS Immunogenicity who did not have any protocol deviations medically assessed as having potential to impact the immunogenicity results. The PPS was the main population for the primary, secondary, and subgroup immunogenicity analyses.

There were no immunogenicity data on Day 181 for 16 subjects, one subject was lost to follow-up, and two subjects were excluded due to study withdrawal; therefore, the FAS consisted of 452 subjects for the analysis at Day 181. The PPS at Day 181 consisted of 441 subjects from the FAS Immunogenicity who  did  not  have  any  protocol  deviations  medically  assessed  as  having  potential  to  impact  the immunogenicity results. The PPS was the main population for the primary and secondary immunogenicity analyses.

## Results

Primary  Immunogenicity  Endpoints:  The  primary  immunogenicity  objective  was  to  assess  the immunogenicity  of  aQIVc  (15,1)  in  comparison  with  the  QIVc  (15,0),  aQIV,  and  QIVr  comparator vaccines as measured by HI assay (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria strains) and MN assay (A/H3N2 strain) using cell-derived target virus at Day 29.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The immune response against A and B strains was higher in subjects receiving aQIVc (15,1) versus QIVc (15,0) with an estimated GMT ratio of 1.31 (95% CI 0.96, 1.79) for A/H1N1, 1.18 (95% CI 0.98, 1.43) for A/H3N2, 1.36 (95% CI 1.08, 1.71) for B/Yamagata and 1.36 (95% CI 1.09, 1.71) for B/Victoria.

The immune response against A and B strains was higher in subjects receiving aQIVc (15,1) versus aQIV, using cell-derived target virus, with an estimated GMT ratio of 1.22 (95% CI 0.89, 1.68) for A/H1N1, 1.31 (95% CI 1.08, 1.59) for A/H3N2, 1.85 (95% CI 1.47, 2.33) for B/Yamagata and 1.81 (95% CI 1.44, 2.27) for B/Victoria.

The immune response against A strains was lower in subjects receiving aQIVc (15,1) versus QIVr with an estimated GMT ratio of 0.84 (95% CI 0.62, 1.15) for A/H1N1 and 0.79 (95% CI 0.66, 0.96) for A/H3N2. The immune response against the B strains was higher in subjects in the aQIVc (15,1) groups versus QIVr with an estimated GMT ratio of 1.15 (95% CI 0.92, 1.44) for B/Yamagata and 1.26 (95% CI 1.00, 1.57) for B/Victoria.

There were no notable differences in the percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40 at Day 29 across the 4 vaccine groups for the A/H1N1 strain. The percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40 at Day 29 was higher in aQIVc (15,1) group (B/Yamagata: 81.8%, B/Victoria: 90.0%) in comparison to QIVc (15,0), aQIV, and QIVr vaccine groups (B/Yamagata: 64.0%, 52.3%, 65.5%; B/Victoria: 72.8%, 69.7%, 79.3%, respectively) for B strains.

The seroconversion rates (SCRs) were higher in subjects who received aQIVc (15,1) in comparison to QIVc (15,0) and aQIV for all 4 strains. The SCRs were mostly comparable for strain A/H1N1, higher for B strains (B/Yamagata and B/Victoria), and lower for the A/H3N2 strain in subjects who received aQIVc (15,1) in comparison to subjects who received QIVr.

The immunogenicity results obtained in the PPS for the primary objective were supported by those obtained in the FAS Immunogenicity.

Secondary Immunogenicity Endpoints: The secondary immunogenicity objectives were to assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc (15,1) in comparison with the QIVc (15,0), aQIV, and QIVr comparator vaccines as measured by HI and MN assay using cell-derived target virus at Day 181 (HI) and Day 29 and Day 181 (MN), respectively.

For secondary immunogenicity endpoints of MN antibody titre at Day 29, results from the 3 cellderived strains (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria) were included, as the Day 29 MN antibody titre against the cell-derived A/H3N2 strain was included in the primary endpoint.

## aQIVc (15,1) vs QIVc (15,0)

- aQIVc (15,1) elicited a higher immune response as assessed by MN GMTs as compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains tested (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria) at Day 29. The GMT ratios were in the range of 1.02 to 1.13.
- Percentage of subjects with MN seroconversion at Day 29 was mostly comparable between aQIVc (15,1) and QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, B/Victoria).
- aQIVc (15,1) elicited a higher immune response for B/Victoria strain, lower immune response for A/H1N1 strain, and comparable immune response for A/H3N2 and B/Yamagata strains as assessed by MN GMTs as compared to QIVc (15,0) at Day 181. The GMT ratios were in the range of 0.92 to 1.16.
- aQIVc (15,1) elicited higher immune response as assessed by HI GMTs as compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains at Day 181. The GMT ratios were in the range of 1.04 to 1.10 for the 3 strains tested by HI (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, B/Victoria).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## aQIVc (15,1) vs aQIV

- aQIVc (15,1) elicited a higher immune response as assessed by MN GMTs as compared to aQIV for the 3 strains (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria) at Day 29 using cell-derived target virus. The GMT ratios were in the range of 1.20 to 1.30 for the 3 strains.
- Percentage of subjects with MN seroconversion at Day 29 was higher for aQIVc (15,1) as compared to aQIV for the 3 strains (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, B/Victoria).
- aQIVc (15,1) elicited a higher immune response for A/H3N2 and B strains and comparable immune response for A/H1N1 strain as assessed by MN GMTs as compared to aQIV at Day 181 using cell-derived target virus. The GMT ratios were in the range of 0.96 to 1.27 for all 4 strains.
- aQIVc (15,1) elicited higher immune response for B strains and comparable immune response to A/H1N1 strain as assessed by HI GMTs as compared to aQIV at Day 181. The GMT ratios were in the range of 0.99 to 1.53 for the 3 strains tested by HI (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, B/Victoria).

## aQIVc (15,1) vs QIVr

- aQIVc (15,1) elicited a lower immune response as assessed by MN GMTs as compared to QIVr for the 3 strains tested (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria) at Day 29. The GMT ratios were in the range of 0.73 to 0.95.
- Percentage of subjects with MN seroconversion at Day 29 was lower for aQIVc (15,1) as compared to QIVr for the 3 strains (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, B/Victoria).
- aQIVc (15,1) elicited a lower immune response compared to QIVr for all 4 strains in terms of MN GMTs at Day 181. The GMT ratios were in the range of 0.57 to 0.93.
- aQIVc (15,1) elicited higher immune response for B strains and lower immune response to A/H1N1 strain in terms of HI GMTs as compared to QIVr at Day 181. The GMT ratios were in the range of 0.79 to 1.39 for the 3 strains tested by HI (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, B/Victoria).

Subgroup and Exploratory: Immunogenicity analyses at Days 1 and 29 were stratified by influenza vaccination history, age, gender, and comorbidity score using cell-derived target virus.

In the previously vaccinated subgroup, pre-vaccination titre (HI and MN) was higher as compared to not previously vaccinated subgroup on Day 1. The post-vaccination Day 29 results showed a pronounced immune response in terms of higher antibody titres in 'not previously vaccinated' subjects compared to 'previously vaccinated' in all 4 vaccine groups for all 4 strains with the exception of the aQIV group where the immune response was either comparable or higher in 'not previously vaccinated' subjects compared to 'previously vaccinated'. Percentage of subjects with seroconversion and with HI titre ≥1:40 (seroprotection) were higher in 'not previously vaccinated' subgroup as compared to 'previously vaccinated' subgroup.

In the subgroup of subjects 50-64 years of age, post-vaccination titre (HI and MN) was mostly higher at Day 29 and Day 181 compared to subgroup of subjects ≥65 years of age. Despite relatively lower immune response in subject ≥65 years of age, higher GMT ratios, especially for aQIVc (15,1) compared to QIVr with the exception of Day 181 HI GMT ratio for B/Victoria strain, were observed when compared to GMT ratios in subjects 50-64 years of age. When comparing aQIVc (15,1) to QIVc or aQIV, the GMT ratios were mostly comparable or higher in subjects ≥65 years of age compared to subjects 50-64 years of age.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No notable differences in pre- or post-vaccination titres were observed in gender and comorbidity score variables.

One exploratory objective was to describe the immunogenicity of all 4 vaccines as measured by the HI assay using egg-derived target virus. In general, the HI assay using egg-derived target virus elicits a higher immune response in aQIV subjects compared to aQIVc (15,1) and QIVc, indicating the immune response from egg-based vaccine (aQIV) in the HI assay favors the egg-derived target virus.

The other exploratory objectives were to describe immunogenicity of all 4 vaccines to three A/H3N2 heterologous strains as measured by MN assay. aQIVc (15,1) showed comparable immune response to QIVc, comparable or higher immune response than aQIV, and lower immune response to QIVr for the 3 A/H3N2 heterologous strains. To study neuraminidase antibody titres in aQIVc (15,1) and QIVc groups, the ELLA was used. The results showed higher anti-NA antibody titres for aQIVc (15,1) as compared to QIVc for all strains.

In summary, the immunogenicity results provided supportive data that the addition of the MF59 adjuvant to a quadrivalent cell-based influenza vaccine may enhance the immune response against all four influenza strains in subjects aged 50 years or older. Comparison of aQIVc (15,1) versus QIVr indicated a comparable immune response against both B strains and a lower immune response against A strains in subjects receiving aQIVc (15,1).

## 5.3.1.2. Study V201\\_01

## Overall Study Design and Plan

Study V201\\_01 was a Phase 2, randomized, stratified, controlled, observer-blind, multi-center, antigen, and adjuvant dose-ranging clinical study in approximately 800 male and female adults aged 50 years and  older  who  were  healthy  or  had  comorbidities  which  increased  their  risk  of  complications  from influenza infection. An overview of the study vaccines is presented in Table 7.

Table 7. Overview of Vaccines Tested in Study V201\\_01

| Group   | Vaccine      | Type of vaccine   |   HA antigen content/ strain (µg ) |   MF59 squalene content (mg) |   Total injected volume (mL) |   Planned number subjects |
|---------|--------------|-------------------|------------------------------------|------------------------------|------------------------------|---------------------------|
| A       | aQIVc (15,1) | Investigational   |                                 15 |                         9.75 |                          0.5 |                       100 |
| B       | aQIVc (45,1) | Investigational   |                                 45 |                         9.75 |                          1.5 |                       100 |
| C       | aQIVc (30,2) | Investigational   |                                 30 |                        19.50 |                          1.0 |                       100 |
| D       | aQIVc (45,2) | Investigational   |                                 45 |                        19.50 |                          1.5 |                       100 |
| E       | aQIVc (15,3) | Investigational   |                                 15 |                        29.25 |                          1.0 |                       100 |
| F       | aQIVc (30,3) | Investigational   |                                 30 |                        29.25 |                         1.25 |                       100 |
| G       | aQIVc (45,3) | Investigational   |                                 45 |                        29.25 |                          1.5 |                       100 |
| H       | QIVc         | Comparator        |                                 15 |                            0 |                          0.5 |                       100 |

The treatments for Groups B, C, D, E, F and G were obtained by 'bed-side' mixing of QIVc, standard dose of aQIVc (formulation aQIVc (15,1)) and MF59 solution. Mixing was not required for Groups A and H.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Study V201\\_01 aimed to assess immunogenicity and safety of different formulations of the adjuvanted quadrivalent cell-based (aQIVc) vaccine versus the non-adjuvanted quadrivalent cell-based influenza (QIVc) vaccine. Immunogenicity and safety were assessed in the overall study population (adults ≥50 years) and in the age subgroups 50 to 64 years and ≥65 years to determine the appropriate age population for this vaccine. Data from this study was used together with other data from Phase 2 studies to inform the selection of the optimal dose to be tested in the pivotal Phase 3 immunogenicity and safety study in older adults.

Study V201\\_01 was conducted in 838 male and female subjects. Subjects were randomized to receive study vaccines in equal ratio as planned, with 98 subjects randomized to QIVc (15,0) (97 subjects exposed), 107 subjects received aQIVc (15,1), 107 subjects received aQIVc (15,3), 103 subjects received aQIVc (30,2), 106 subjects received aQIVc (30,3), 108 subjects received aQIVc (45,1), 101 subjects received aQIVc (45,2), and 109 subjects received aQIVc (45,3). Stratification factors were applied for age group (50 to 64 and ≥65 years) and history of any influenza vaccination within 3 previous influenza seasons (yes/no).

## Study Objectives

The primary immunogenicity objective was to assess the immunogenicity of the different aQIVc formulations in comparison with the non-adjuvanted QIVc vaccine as measured by hemagglutination inhibition (HI) assay at Day 29. Due to lack of agglutination for A/H3N2 strain in HI assay, immunogenicity for that strain was assessed as measured by microneutralization (MN) assay as an acceptable alternative.

The secondary immunogenicity objectives were to assess the immunogenicity of the different aQIVc formulations in comparison with the QIVc vaccine as measured by HI assay at Day 181, and to assess the immunogenicity of the different aQIVc formulations in comparison with the QIVc vaccine as measured by MN assay at Day 29 and Day 181.

Additional exploratory immunogenicity analyses were conducted to assess the immunogenicity of the different aQIVc formulations in comparison with the QIVc vaccine as measured by MN assay using heterologous target viruses at Day 29. These comprised the geometric mean of MN antibodies at Days 1 and 29, the geometric mean of the fold increase in serum MN titre postvaccination (Day 29) compared to prevaccination (Day 1), and percentages of subjects with seroconversion at Day 29.

## Evaluation of Immunogenicity

The primary immunogenicity objective was assessed by HI assay for A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria strains using cell-derived target virus homologous to the seasonal vaccine. For homologous and heterologous A/H3N2 strains, where lack of red blood cell agglutination was observed due to changes in the HA protein of the virus, immunogenicity was measured by the MN assay. The assays were conducted on serum samples collected on Day 1 (before vaccination, baseline), and Day 29.

Serological specimens taken from subjects at baseline (Day 1) and after vaccination (Day 29 and Day 181) were used for measurement of immune responses using the HI (or MN) assay. The Per Protocol Set (PPS) was the main population for the primary, secondary, and subgroup immunogenicity analyses. No formal hypotheses were tested in the study and no thresholds for success were defined up front.

## Datasets Analysed

The All Enrolled Set consisted of 839 subjects who were enrolled in the study. The All Exposed set consisted of 838 subjects as one subject enrolled in the study was randomized to the QIVc (15,0) group but did not receive the study vaccine. There were no immunogenicity results on Day 29 in 4 subjects as blood samples could not be collected; therefore, the FAS consisted of 834 subjects. The PPS for Day 29 analysis consisted of 810 subjects from the FAS Immunogenicity who did not have any PDs medically assessed as having potential to impact the immunogenicity results.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There were no immunogenicity results on Day 181 in 18 subjects: 14 subjects due to premature study withdrawal (4 deaths, 6 lost to follow up, 3 withdrawal of consent, and 1 other) and 4 subjects were excluded due to protocol deviation (serum sample was not collected); therefore, the FAS consisted of 821 subjects at Day 181. The PPS for Day 181 analysis consisted of 799 subjects from the FAS Immunogenicity who did not have any protocol deviations medically assessed as having potential to impact the immunogenicity results.

## Results

Primary Immunogenicity Endpoints: The primary immunogenicity objective was to assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc formulations in comparison with the QIVc (15,0) vaccine as measured by HI assay (A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria strains) and MN assay (A/H3N2 strain) using cell-derived target virus at Day 29.

Postvaccination HI GMTs for A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria and MN GMTs for A/H3N2 on Day 29 in general showed a modest upward trend with increasing HA antigen and MF59 dose with the exception of aQIVc (45,3) formulation for A strains. Day 29 HI GMTs and MN GMTs for QIVc (15,0) compared to aQIVc (15,1) were higher for A/H1N1 strain and similar for A/H3N2, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria strains.

HI/MN Day 29 GMT ratios in general showed an upward trend with increasing HA antigen and MF59 dose for A/H1N1 (0.77-1.19), A/H3N2 (0.97-1.35), B/Yamagata (0.99-1.45), and B/Victoria (0.951.28), with a response decline or plateau when MF59 adjuvant level increases from 2x to 3x. Day 29 MN GMT ratios were above 1.0 for all aQIVc formulations for A/H3N2 strain. There were only two aQIVc formulations [aQIVc (45,1) and aQIVc (45,2)] that showed HI GMT ratio more than 1.0 for all 4 strains.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Percentages of subjects with a postvaccination HI titre of ≥1:40 were higher for the A/H1N1 (90.4% - 98.9%) and B/Victoria (92.4%-98.1%) strains, and comparatively lower for B/Yamagata strain (68.6%-84.8%) and similar across all vaccine groups. No notable differences in the percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40 at Day 29 were seen with increase in HA antigen and MF59 dose in aQIVc formulations compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains assessed for HI titre ≥1:40. In general, the highest HI titre ≥1:40 were observed for doses with the highest HA and/or MF59 adjuvant dose formulations.

The HI SCRs varied between strains and vaccine groups, with no clear upward trend with increasing antigen and adjuvant. In general, the highest HA antigen and/or MF59 adjuvant dose formulations had the largest SCR differences compared to QIVc (15,0) for each of the 4 strains, with the exception of aQIVc (45,3) for B/Victoria strains.

a A/H3N2 immunogenicity was assessed by MN assay

Abbreviations: aQIVc = MF59-adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Inactivated cell-derived Influenza Vaccine; CI = confidence interval; HI = hemagglutination inhibition; QIVc = Quadrivalent subunit inactivated cell-derived influenza vaccine. Each treatment arm is described by the name of the treatment and the content in parentheses, where the first figure represents the antigen content (μg per strain) and the second number represents the adjuvant content (1 means 1x MF59 [ie, standard dose], 2 means 2x MF59 etc.).

Note: HI Seroconversion = either a prevaccination (baseline) HI titre &lt;1:10 and postvaccination HI titre ≥ 1:40, or a prevaccination HI titre ≥ 1:10 and a ≥ 4-fold increase in postvaccination HI antibody. MN Seroconversion = either ≥ 4-fold increase for subjects with prevaccination MN titres ≥ Lower Limit of Quantitation (LLOQ) or as ≥ 4*LLOQ for subjects with prevaccination MN titre &lt;LLOQ.

Note: As the model did not converge, unadjusted values are presented for vaccine differences. For MN assay, adjusted values are presented.

The immunogenicity results obtained in the PPS for the primary objective were in line with those obtained in the FAS Immunogenicity.

Secondary Immunogenicity Endpoints: The secondary immunogenicity objectives were to assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc formulations in comparison with the QIVc comparator vaccine as measured by HI and MN assay using cell-derived target virus at Day 181 (HI) and Day 29 and Day 180 (MN), respectively.

Secondary immunogenicity endpoints of MN antibody titre at Day 29 were reported for the 3 strains, A/H1N1, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria, as the A/H3N2 strain measured using MN assay was part of the primary endpoint in place of the missing results for HI assay.

- Postvaccination MN GMTs at Day 29 in general showed an upward trend with increasing HA antigen and MF59 dose with exception of aQIVc (15,3) and aQIVc (45,3) formulations for A/H1N1 strain.
- The MN GMT ratio at Day 29 for aQIVc formulations ranged from 0.90 to 1.53 for A/H1N1, 0.99 to 1.35 for B/Yamagata, and 1.05 to 1.54 for B/Victoria. Similar to HI, MN GMT ratios in general showed an upward trend with increasing MN antigen and MF59 dose, with a response decline or plateau at 45 µg of HA when MF59 adjuvant level increased from 2x to 3x.
- The MN SCRs for aQIVc formulations at Day 29 were higher for the A strains compared with the B strains and varied across vaccine groups. In general, all aQIVc formulations had a positive difference as compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains with the exception of aQIVc (15,1) for B strains. In the A/H1N1 and to a lesser extent with B strains, the SCRs increased in particular with increasing dose of MF59 adjuvant. The maximum difference in SCRs were observed with the aQIVc formulations that contained high doses of either HA antigen, MF59 adjuvant, or both as compared to QIVc (15,0) in all 3 strains.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The Day 181 serum samples were tested in a different testing campaign as compared to the Day 1 and Day 29 serum samples. Therefore, comparison of GMTs at Day 181 with GMTs at Day 1 should be interpreted with caution.

- The HI GMTs for aQIVc formulations at Day 181 showed slightly higher immune response for B strains and lower immune response for A/H1N1 strain compared to QIVc (15,0). The Day 181 GMT ratios for aQIVc formulations were in the range of 0.71 to 0.86 for A/H1N1, 0.99 to 1.09 for B/Yamagata, and 0.96 to 1.23 for B/Victoria.
- Percentages of subjects with a postvaccination HI titre of ≥1:40 at Day 181 were higher for the A/H1N1 (80.0%-87.3%) and B/Victoria (71.3%-75.5%) strains, and comparatively lower for the B/Yamagata strain (40.2%-49.5%) for all vaccine groups. No notable differences in the percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40 at Day 181 were seen with increase in HA antigen and MF59 dose in aQIVc formulations compared to QIVc (15,0) for all 3 strains.
- The postvaccination MN GMTs for aQIVc formulations at Day 181 mostly showed higher immune response for all 4 strains compared to QIVc (15,0). The GMT ratios for aQIVc formulations were in the range of 0.83 to 1.25 for A/H1N1, 0.86 to 1.24 for A/H3N2, 1.01 to 1.23 for B/Yamagata, and 1.03 to 1.64 for B/Victoria.

Subgroup and Exploratory: Immunogenicity analyses at Days 1 and 29 were stratified by the variables of influenza vaccination history, age, gender, and comorbidity score. Immunogenicity analyses at Day 181 were stratified by influenza vaccination history and age group.

In the previously vaccinated subgroup, there were no notable differences in baseline titre (both HI and MN) as compared to not previously vaccinated subgroup on Day 1. The postvaccination titre on Day 29 results showed a pronounced immune response in terms of higher antibody titres in 'not previously vaccinated' subjects compared to 'previously vaccinated' subjects in all vaccine groups for all 4 strains. This effect, however, was not similar in all vaccine groups and often most pronounced in the QIVc (15,0) group. Postvaccination HI titres ≥1:40 on Day 29 for A/H1N1 and B/Victoria were high in both the previously vaccinated and the not previously vaccinated subgroup, but for B/Yamagata, higher percentages were seen in the not previously vaccinated group. Similar to HI and MN titre, the percentage of subjects with HI SCRs and MN SCRs at Day 29 was higher in subgroup of subjects 'not previously vaccinated' compared with subjects who were 'previously vaccinated'.

In the age subgroup of subjects 50 to 64 years of age and ≥65 years of age, no notable differences in baseline HI and MN titre at Day 29 and Day 181 were observed with the exception of MN titre at Day 29 for A/H1N1 strain where the immune response was higher in age group of 65 years and older than age group of 50 to 64 years. No notable difference in percentage of subjects with postvaccination HI titres ≥1:40 was observed between both age subgroups (50 to 64 years and ≥65 years) at Day 29 and Day 181. The percentage of subjects with HI SCRs was higher in 50 to 64 years age group compared to ≥65 years age group for B strains at Day 29, whereas no notable differences were observed in MN SCRs at Day 29 in subgroup of subjects 50 to 64 years of age and ≥65 years of age. The MN SCRs at Day 29 varied between strains but in general all aQIVc formulations had a positive difference as compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains for both age groups with the exception of aQIVc (15,1) for B strains and aQIVc (45,1) for B/Victoria strain in subjects aged 50 to 64 years and aQIVc (15,1) for B/Yamagata strain in subjects aged ≥65 years. The maximum difference in MN SCRs we re observed with the aQIVc formulations that contained the highest dose of either HA antigen, MF59 adjuvant, or both as compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 strains.

No notable differences in pre- or postvaccination titres were observed in comorbidity score variable. For gender variable, no notable differences were observed for 3 out of 4 strains (A/H3N2, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria) on Day 29. For A/H1N1 strain, the female subgroup showed slightly higher postvaccination HI and MN titre and SCRs with more pronounced response in QIVc (15,0) vaccine group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The exploratory objective was to describe immunogenicity of all vaccine groups to three A/H3N2 heterologous strains as measured by MN assay.

The postvaccination MN GMTs for aQIVc formulations at Day 29 showed higher immune response for aQIVc formulations for the 3 A/H3N2 heterologous strains compared to QIVc (15,0). The response for the A/Singapore and the A/Newcastle strains showed a modest upward trend with increasing in HA antigen and MF59 adjuvant dose with a response plateau when MF59 adjuvant level increased from 2x to 3x with exception of aQIVc with 30 µg antigen dose for A/Newcastle. The GMT ratio ranged from 1.09 to 1.59, 1.03 to 1.80, and 1.02 to 1.33 for A/Singapore/GP2050/2015, A/Indiana/08/2018, and A/Newcastle/82/2018, respectively.

The MN SCRs for aQIVc formulations were higher for the A/Singapore strain as compared to the two other heterologous strains. All aQIVc formulations had a positive difference as compared to QIVc (15,0) for the 3 A/H3N2 heterologous strains with the exception of aQIVc (45,3) for A/Indiana/08/2018 strain. For the A/Singapore and A/Newcastle strains, the maximum differences in SCRs were observed with the aQIVc formulations that contained the highest dose of either HA antigen, MF59 adjuvant, or both as compared to QIVc (15,0).

In summary, the immunogenicity results demonstrated that increasing doses of HA antigen (up to 45 µg of HA per strain) and MF59 adjuvant (up to 2x dose) in aQIVc formulations elicited higher immune response in subjects aged 50 years or older.

## 5.3.1.3. Study V201\\_07

## Overall Study Design and Plan

Study V201\\_07 was a Phase 2b, randomized, stratified, controlled, observer-blind, multi-center, antigen, and adjuvant dose-confirmation clinical study in approximately 1000 male and female adults aged 50 years and older who were healthy or had comorbidities which increased their risk of complications from influenza infection. An overview of the study vaccines is presented in Table 10.

Table 10. Overview of Vaccines Tested in Study V201\\_07

| Vaccine       | Type of vaccine   |   HA antigen content/ strain (µg ) |   MF59 squalene content (mg) |   Total injected volume (mL) | Presentation/ mixing          |
|---------------|-------------------|------------------------------------|------------------------------|------------------------------|-------------------------------|
| QIVc (45,0)*  | Investigational   |                                 45 |                            0 |                          1.5 | 3x QIVc                       |
| aQIVc (45,1)* | Investigational   |                                 45 |                         9.75 |                         1.25 | 0.5* (aQIVc (45,2) + 3x QIVc) |
| aQIVc (45,2)  | Investigational   |                                 45 |                        19.50 |                          1.0 | PFS                           |
| QIVc (15,0)   | Comparator        |                                 15 |                            0 |                          0.5 | PFS                           |

Study V201\\_07 aimed to confirm immunogenicity and safety of the adjuvanted quadrivalent cell-based influenza (aQIVc) vaccine containing 45 µg of antigen per strain and 2xMF59 (containing 19.5 mg of squalene). Two other formulations of (a)QIVc were also included to confirm the incremental benefit of additional antigen and adjuvant which was expected to lead to the optimal dose-response elicited by aQIVc. All investigational formulations were compared against one another as well as to the licensed non-adjuvanted quadrivalent cell-based influenza vaccine (QIVc, Flucelvax Quadrivalent). Immunogenicity and safety were assessed in the overall study population (adults ≥50 years and above) and in the age subgroups ≥50 to 64 years and ≥65 years. Data from this study was used to confirm the optimal dose to be tested in the pivotal Phase 3 immunogenicity and safety study in older adults.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Study V201\\_07 was conducted in 1051 male and female subjects, randomized to receive study vaccines in 1:1:1:1 ratio, with 263 subjects randomized to QIVc (15,0) (262 subjects exposed), 264 subjects receiving QIVc (45,0), 265 subjects randomized to aQIVc (45,1) (263 subjects exposed), and 264 subjects randomized to aQIVc (45,2) (262 subjects exposed). Stratification factors were applied for age (50 to 64 and ≥65 years) and history of any influenza vaccination within 3 previous influenza seasons (yes/no).

## Study Objectives

The primary immunogenicity objective was to assess the immunogenicity of the different (a)QIVc formulations through pairwise comparisons and trend analysis as measured by hemagglutination inhibition (HI) assay using cell-derived target viruses after vaccination, at Day 29.

The secondary immunogenicity objective was to assess the immunogenicity of the different (a)QIVc formulations through pairwise comparison and trend analysis, as measured by MN assay using cellderived target viruses at Day 29.

Additional exploratory immunogenicity analyses were conducted to characterize immune response of different (a)QIVc formulation in terms of HI titre at Day 29 and consisted of the percentage of subjects with HI titres ≥1:80, ≥1:160, and ≥1:320 at Day 1 and Day 29.

## Evaluation of Immunogenicity

The primary immunogenicity objective was assessed by HI assay for A/H1N1, A/H3N2, B/Yamagata, and B/Victoria strains using cell-derived target virus homologous to the seasonal vaccine. The assays were conducted on serum samples collected on Day 1 (before vaccination, baseline), and Day 29.

Serological specimens taken from subjects at baseline (Day 1) and after vaccination (Day 29) were used for measurement of immune responses using the HI (or MN) assay. The PPS was the main population for the primary, secondary, and subgroup immunogenicity analyses. No formal hypotheses were tested in the study and no thresholds for success were defined up front.

## Datasets Analysed

The All Enrolled Set consisted of 1056 subjects who were enrolled in the study. The All Exposed Set consisted of 1051 subjects as five subjects enrolled in the study were randomized [one subject in QIVc (15,0) and two subjects each in aQIVc (45,1) and aQIVc (45,2)] but did not receive the study vaccine. In total, 25 subjects were excluded from immunogenicity analysis (due to early termination or protocol deviation); therefore, the FAS consisted of 1031 subjects. All decisions for exclusion were taken before unblinding.

The PPS consisted of 1022 subjects from the FAS Immunogenicity Set who did not have any protocol deviations medically assessed as having potential to impact the immunogenicity results The PPS was the main population for the primary, secondary, and subgroup immunogenicity analyses. The most common reason for exclusion from the PPS after withdrawal of consent and lost to follow-up was for subjects not meeting the eligibility criteria (i.e., did not meet at least 1 inclusion or met at least one exclusion criterion [0.7%]).

## Results

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Primary Immunogenicity Endpoints: The primary immunogenicity objective was to assess the immunogenicity of the different (a)QIVc formulations through pairwise comparisons and trend analysis as measured by hemagglutination inhibition (HI) assay using cell-derived target viruses at Day 29.

Postvaccination HI GMTs for A/H1N1, A/H3N2, B/Victoria, and B/Yamagata on Day 29 in general showed an upward trend with higher HA antigen and increasing MF59 dose for all 4 strains with the exception of aQIVc (45,1) formulation for B/Victoria strain. Increasing MF59 adjuvant to 1x in higher HA antigen formulation [aQIVc (45,1)] slightly increased GMTs for both A and B/Yamagata strains, whereas increasing MF59 adjuvant to 2x [aQIVc (45,2)] substantially increased GMTs for all 4 strains. Similar trends were seen in GMT ratio and GMFI.

Pairwise comparisons showed that the GMT ratios of aQIVc (45,2) over QIVc (15,0) were statistically superior (lower bound of 95% Confidence Interval &gt;1.0) for all 4 strains. Pairwise comparisons showed that the GMT ratios of aQIVc (45,2) over QIVc (45,0) were statistically superior (lower bound of 95% Confidence Interval &gt;1.0) for both A strains and B/Yamagata, whereas aQIVc (45,1) did not demonstrate statistical  superior  responses  compared  to  QIVc  (45,0)  for  any  strain.  aQIVc  (45,2)  also  showed statistically significant GMT ratios (lower bound of 95% Confidence Interval &gt;1.0) over aQIVc (45,1) for A/H1N1 and B/Yamagata strains.

The  immunogenicity  results  obtained  in  the  PPS  for  the  primary  objective  were  supported  by  those obtained in the FAS.

Secondary  Immunogenicity  Endpoints:  The  secondary  immunogenicity  objective  was  to  assess  the immunogenicity of the different (a)QIVc formulations through pairwise comparisons and trend analysis as measured by MN assay using cell-derived target viruses at Day 29. MN analysis showed a similar trend as analysis using the HI assay and conclusions drawn from HI results were confirmed.

Postvaccination MN GMTs in general showed an upward trend with higher HA antigen and increasing MF59 dose. Increasing MF59 adjuvant to 1x in higher HA antigen formulation [aQIVc (45,1)] slightly increased GMTs for both A and B/Victoria strains, whereas increasing MF59 adjuvant to 2x [aQIVc (45,2)] substantially increased GMTs for all 4 strains.

In general, MN GMT ratios and MN GMFI showed an upward trend with higher HA antigen and increasing MF59 dose for all 4 strains. The increase was statistically significant in aQIVc (45,2) compared to both QIVc (15,0) and QIVc (45,0) for all 4 strains.

Pairwise comparisons showed that the MN GMT ratios of aQIVc (45,2) over QIVc (45,0) were statistically significantly higher for all 4 strains, whereas aQIVc (45,1) did not elicit statistically significant differences
