Abbreviations: ANCOVA = analysis of covariance; aQIV = MF59-Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Inactivated Egg-derived Influenza  Vaccine;  aQIVc HD  =  High-Dose  MF59-Adjuvanted  Quadrivalent  Subunit  Inactivated  Cell-derived  Influenza Vaccine; CI = confidence interval; GMFI = geometric mean fold increase; GMT = geometric mean titre; HI = hemagglutination inhibition; N/A = not applicable; PPS = Per Protocol Set; SCR = seroconversion rate.

Note 1: Immunogenicity was measured by HI assay using the following egg-derived target viruses: A/Victoria/4897/2022 IVR238 (A/H1N1), A/Darwin/6/2021 IVR-227 (A/H3N2), B/Phuket/3073/2013 BVR-18 (B/Yamagata), B/Austria/1359417/2021 BVR-26 (B/Victoria).

Note 2: Day 1 GMTs are unadjusted values. Day 29 GMTs and GMT ratios are adjusted values, calculated via ANCOVA with vaccine  group,  prevaccination  titre  (log10  transformed),  age  subgroup,  sex,  country,  and  influenza  vaccination  history  as covariates. GMFIs are adjusted values, calculated via ANCOVA with vaccine group, age subgroup, sex, country, and influenza vaccination history as covariates.

Note 3: The Day 29 GMT ratios, GMFI ratios, and Day 29 SCR differences are calculated for aQIV with reference to aQIVc HD and, therefore, these parameters are denoted 'N/A' for the aQIVc HD group.

## Sex

For both the male and female subgroups, the results based on the HI assay using cell-derived target viruses were consistent with the overall study results. The higher immunogenicity of aQIVc HD compared to aQIV at Day 29, as evidenced by GMTs, GMFIs, percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40, and SCRs, was  observed  in  both  the  male  and  female  subgroups  for  all  strains.  The  extent  of  this  increased immunogenicity was generally comparable in male and female subjects.

The results observed in the male and female subgroups based on the HI assay using egg-derived target viruses were consistent with the corresponding results in the overall study population. For 3 strains (A/H3N2 and the two B strains), immunogenicity was higher or comparable for aQIVc HD compared with aQIV  in  both  the  male  and  female  subgroups;  values  were  comparable  in  both  sex  subgroups.  For A/H1N1, values for the HI assay using egg-derived target viruses were lower for aQIVc HD compared with aQIV in both sex subgroups, with comparable values in both subgroups.

## Vaccination History

For both not previously vaccinated subjects and previously vaccinated subjects, the results based on the HI assay using cell-derived target viruses were consistent with the overall study results. The higher immunogenicity  of  aQIVc  HD  at  Day  29  compared  to  aQIV,  as  evidenced  by  GMFIs,  percentage  of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40, and SCRs, was observed in both the not previously vaccinated subjects and previously vaccinated subjects for all strains. The extent of this increased immunogenicity was generally comparable in both subgroups.

The results observed in not previously vaccinated subjects and previously vaccinated subjects based on the HI assay using egg-derived target viruses were consistent with the corresponding results in the overall study population. For 3 strains (A/H3N2 and the two B strains), immunogenicity was higher or comparable for aQIVc HD compared with aQIV in both the not previously vaccinated and previously vaccinated subgroups; values were comparable in both subgroups. For A/H1N1, values for the HI assay using egg-derived target viruses were lower for aQIVc HD compared with aQIV in both the not previously vaccinated and previously vaccinated subgroups, with comparable values in both subgroups.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Prevaccination Titre

For both subjects with a prevaccination titre of &lt;1:10 and subjects with a prevaccination titre of ≥1:10, the results based on the HI assay using cell-derived target viruses were consistent with the overall study results. The higher immunogenicity of aQIVc HD at Day 29 compared to aQIV, as evidenced by GMTs, GMFIs, percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40, and SCRs, was observed in both subjects with a prevaccination titre &lt;1:10 and subjects with a prevaccination titre ≥1:10 for all strains. The extent of this increased immunogenicity was higher in subjects with a prevaccination titre &lt;1:10.

The results observed in subjects with a prevaccination titre of &lt;1:10 and subjects with a prevaccination titre of ≥1:10 based on the HI assay using egg -derived target viruses were generally consistent with the corresponding results in the overall study population. For 3 strains (A/H3N2 and the two B strains), immunogenicity was higher or comparable for aQIVc HD compared with aQIV in both subjects with a prevaccination titre of &lt;1:10 and subjects with a titre of ≥1:10; values were generally comparable in both subgroups. For A/H1N1, values for the HI assay using egg-derived target viruses were lower for aQIVc HD compared with aQIV in both subgroups, with comparable values in both subgroups.

## Country

Immunogenicity analyses by country showed high variability in results, especially for countries with fewer subjects thus analyses should be approached with caution. In the EU countries, Germany (aQIVc HD: N=811; aQIV: N=540), Estonia (aQIVc HD: N=585; aQIV: N=390), and Denmark (aQIVc HD: N=25; aQIV:  N=18),  higher  immunogenicity  of  aQIVc  HD  at  Day  29  was  observed  compared  to  aQIV,  as evidenced by GMFI ratios for all four strains

## 5.3.2.1.4.6. Ancillary analyses

## Post Hoc Exploratory Subgroup Analyses, Comparing aQIVc HD vs QIVr and aQIV

Post hoc immunogenicity analyses were performed in V201\\_03 study with the purpose to explore the immune response of subjects in different subgroups, i.e. age group, race/ethnicity, comorbidity risk score. The purpose of this post hoc analysis it to provide information on subgroups that could be relevant for regulatory submissions and for discussions with national immunization groups.

The objectives of this exploratory analysis were:

1. To assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV for each homologous vaccine strain, in all subjects by age group (Age Group 1: 50-59 years and 60 years and older; Age Group 2: 50-64 years, 65-74 years, 75-84 years, and 85 years and older), as measured by hemagglutination inhibition (HI) assay using cell-derived target viruses, at Day 1 and Day 29.

2. To assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV for each homologous vaccine strain, in all subjects and in subjects enrolled in the United States/Canada/Pakistan by race/ethnicity, overall and by age group (50-64 years and 65 years and older), as measured by HI assay using cell-derived target viruses, at Day 1 and Day 29.

3. To assess the immunogenicity of aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV for each homologous vaccine strain, in all subjects by comorbidity risk score, overall and by age group (50-64 years and 65 years and older), as measured by HI assay using cell-derived target viruses, at Day 1 and Day 29.

The objectives of this analysis are exploratory and descriptive in nature to provide estimates including 95% CIs for the GMT ratio, GMFI ratio, and for the SCR difference between vaccine groups (ie, aQIVc HD vs QIVr, aQIVc HD vs aQIV).

## Results

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The results obtained from the post hoc analysis in the different subgroups and combination of subgroups (race and age, ethnicity and age, comorbidity risk score and age) are similar as those observed in the overall study population. Some of these results should be taken with caution due to the low number of subjects in some subgroups.

The complete set of data for this post hoc exploratory subgroup analysis can be found in the document V201\\_03 CSR Addendum 1.

## 5.3.3. Clinical studies in special populations

No  special  studies  have  been  carried  out  in  special  populations.  The  elderly  are  part  of  the  main population since the sought indication is in adults 50 years and alder.

## 5.3.4. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.4.1. Discussion

The product indication is prophylaxis of influenza in adults 50 years of age and older.

The aTIVc (MF59-adjuvanted trivalent subunit inactivated cell-derived influenza vaccine) is a seasonal, cell  culture-derived,  inactivated,  subunit,  trivalent  influenza  vaccine,  adjuvanted  with  MF59.  The vaccine  contains  purified  surface  antigens  from  3  strains  of  influenza  virus,  A/H1N1,  A/H3N2,  and B/Victoria lineage, which is in line with current recommendations.

aTIVc is proposed to offer important advantages over existing influenza vaccines by combining the benefits  of  a  high  dose  of  hemagglutinin  (HA)  antigens,  a  cell-based  manufacturing  platform,  and adjuvanted  technology.  Cell  culture-derived  antigens  may  provide  a  better  match  to  circulating influenza  strains,  addressing  the  issue  of  potential  mismatch  due  to  egg  adaptation,  which  could improve vaccine effectiveness. The aTIVc formulation contains 3 times the standard HA antigen dose (45  µg  HA  of  each  strain  vs  15  µg  as  standard  in  aQIV  [MF59-adjuvanted  quadrivalent  subunit inactivated egg-derived influenza vaccine]) and 2 times the standard dose of MF59 in aQIV (containing 19.5 mg of squalene vs 9.75 mg as standard).

aTIVc is expected to provide benefits in adults aged 50 years and older by enhancing the immune response via inclusion of the MF59 adjuvant (at a higher dose) and use of a higher dose of antigen. MF59 is approved for use in seasonal and pandemic influenza vaccines across many countries and has a well-established safety profile.

The clinical efficacy is inferred from the submitted immunogenicity studies. This approach is in general in agreement with the recommendations of the Guideline on Influenza Vaccines (Non-clinical and clinical module) (EMA/CHMP/VWP/457259/2014). The clinical development was conducted with quadrivalent vaccines (QIV), which was the composition recommended at the time the studies were conducted. Since 2023, WHO and regional authorities have recommended trivalent (TIV) composition by suppression of the strain B Yamagata, which is no longer in circulation. From a clinical perspective, since QIV and TIV are manufactured using the same manufacturing process, QIV and TIV are equivalent for the shared antigens (A/H1N1, A/H3N2, and B Victoria strain). This approach has been endorsed by ETF and CHMP in previous submissions. For the actual commercial product, the applicant plans to provide the trivalent formulation with the composition as recommended for the respective season.

The clinical program in support of the Flucelvax QIV, high dose, adjuvanted influenza vaccine (referred to as aQIVc HD) consists of 3 phase 2 supportive studies (V201\\_10, V201\\_01, and V201\\_07) and one phase  3  pivotal  study  (V201\\_03).  The  applicant  stated  that  stepwise  clinical  development  was undertaken,  first  to  show  that  addition  of  adjuvant  improved  the  immune  response,  secondly  to investigate the range of antigen and adjuvant dose and third to confirm the magnitude of effect with the selected dose, prior to proceeding to the phase 3 clinical study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Dose-response studies

## Study V200\\_10: Proof of Concept

Study V200\\_10 was a proof-of-concept Phase 2 study which compared the immunogenicity of aQIV (Fluad), QIVc (Flucelvax) and QIVr (Flublok/Supemtek) to the investigational vaccine aQIVc containing 15 μg of each HA antigen and MF59 adjuvant (the standard dose used in Fluad).

The primary immunogenicity objective, of descriptive nature, was the comparison of immunogenicity measured by cell-derived HI assay at day 29. Secondary objectives included cell-derived HI comparison at Day 181, and MN immunogenicity at days 29 and 181. Exploratory objectives included egg-derived HI evaluation, MN analysis of H3N2 heterologous strains and NA immunogenicity. All endpoints were determined in subjects 50-64 and ≥ 65 years of age and overall.

The study was conducted in the US and it enrolled 471 subjects aged 50 to 87 years of age. 48.2% of subjects were aged 50-64 years of age, and 51.8% were 65 years of age or older, meeting the initial plan of enrolling 50% of subjects in each group. 60.1% of the subjects were female, and 70% of the subjects had been vaccinated for influenza within the 3 previous seasons although its exact date was mostly incomplete. Demographic characteristics were well balanced between the different intervention groups, which were randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio. The primary objective was assessed in the PPS Day 29 set, which consisted of 449 subjects.

The primary immunogenicity analysis compared the four vaccines at Day 29 in terms of GMTs, HI antibody titres ≥ 1:40 and seroconversion rates, using the cell-derived HI assay. In the case of H3N2, due to lack of agglutination, this strain was assessed using the MN assay.

In terms of GMT, the immune response was higher in subjects receiving aQIVc compared to those receiving aQIV or QIVc. The effect was more pronounced for B strains (GMT ratio 95% CI LL always higher than 1) than for A strains. The immune response was also higher against B strains in aQIVc subjects compared to QIVr individuals. Nevertheless, QIVr vaccine was more immunogenic than aQIVc for A strains.

The percentage of subjects with HI titre against H1N1 ≥ 1:40 was similar across the four vaccines tested. In contrast, this percentage for both B strains was higher in subjects receiving aQIVc than with any of the other three vaccines (showing an improvement of 15.2-27.8% for B/Yamagata, and 8.5-18.6% for B/Victoria).

The seroconversion rates were higher for aQIVc subjects than for aQIV or QIVc, with differences in the range of 4.1-14.0% (vs QIVc) or 10.2-22.9% (vs aQIV). SCR were mostly comparable between aQIVc and QIVr (differences in the range of -9.2 to 6.0%) with 95% CI always overlapping 1.

In each arm, about half of subjects were ≥ 65 years of age. It is interesting to note that though the GMT was lower, the GMT ratio to the comparator was slightly higher in this age group than in the 5064 years group. The % differences in subjects with HI titres ≥ 1:40 and in SCR (seroconversion rate) were comparable between age groups. This was consistent between HI and MN assay. The superior immunogenicity of aQIVc over aQIV faded when an egg-derived HI assay was used for exploratory objectives, and aQIV was even superior in some parameters like H1N1 GMT (ratio 0.81), HI titre ≥ 1:40 (difference -4.4%) and SCR (difference -5%). This is not unexpected and confirms that the egg-derived assay favours an egg-derived vaccine, while the cell-derived assay might favour the cellderived vaccine.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

At D181, the GMTs and GMTR were lower than at D29 following the same trends in all vaccine arms, suggesting that additional dose of adjuvant does not extend the antibody persistence.

In conclusion, within the same antigen dose (15µg/strain), the addition of adjuvant seems to improve the immune response as compared to QIVc and is comparable to the high antigen dose (45µg/strain) vaccine. Also, the adjuvant does not seem to result in a longer duration of response, irrespective of antigen dose.

## Study V201\\_01: Dose finding

Study V201\\_01 was an antigen and adjuvant dose ranging Phase 2 study which compared the immunogenicity of different formulations of aQIVc to QIVc (Flucelvax). A total of 7 aQIVc formulations were tested with antigen dose ranging from 15 to 45 μg of each HA antigen; and adjuvant dose ranging from 9.75 to 29.25 mg of squalene (1 to 3 times the adjuvant dose in Fluad). Six of these formulations were obtained by bed-side mixing.

The primary immunogenicity objective was to compare the immunogenicity of the different formulations of aQIVc to the non-adjuvanted QIVc, measured by cell-derived HI assay at Day 29. The secondary objectives included HI comparison at Day 181 and MN immunogenicity at Days 29 and 181. As an exploratory objective, MN immunogenicity was assessed for H3N2 heterologous strains. These objectives were consistent with the POC study V201\\_10 described above.

The study was conducted in Australia, New Zealand, South Africa and Philippines; and it enrolled 839 subjects, most of them from Australia and New Zealand (36.1 and 29.3%). They were randomized to receive any of the 8 vaccines in equal ratio. All subjects were between 50 and 89 years of age, with 52.7% being 50-64 years of age, thus meeting the plan to enrol 50% of subjects in each stratum (50-64 and ≥ 65). Females represented 56.6% of participants, and 60.3% of the subjects had received an influenza vaccine within the 3 previous seasons. The demographic characteristics were generally well balanced between the 8 groups, except for sex, which showed big variations (from as low as 29.6% of males in QIVc to 54.6% of males in aQIVc3). This is not considered relevant considering the total evidence generated in other well-balanced studies.

The primary objective was assessed in the PPS Day 29 set, which consisted of 810 subjects. A total of 17 subjects were excluded from PPS Immunogenicity Day 29 due to not meeting at least 1 inclusion/exclusion criteria, despite having been enrolled in the study and administered the vaccine. The applicant provided further information and explained that 8 of them had previous history of AESI and 9 of them had been prescribed monoclonal antibodies, and this was discovered later. Their exclusion did not have any significant effect on the immunogenicity results, as shown by sensitivity analysis in the FAS.

The primary immunogenicity analysis compared the four vaccines at Day 29 in terms of GMTs, HI antibody titres ≥1:40 and seroconversion rates, using the cell -derived HI assay. In the case of H3N2, due to lack of agglutination, this strain was assessed using the MN assay.

An SAP amendment (dated August 2021) allowed a wider time window for Day 29 results (-7 to +21, that is, 22 to 51 days) to be included in the PP analysis. The applicant provided an analysis of mean (and SD) of the time period between Day 1 and Day 29 samples for the different groups, and this showed no imbalance between groups. It is therefore not considered to have an impact on the results.

GMTs showed an upward trend with increasing antigen and adjuvant dosage. aQIVc7 (45,2) showed the higher titres for H1N1 and H3N2, while for B strains these were surpassed by aQIVc11 (45,3) although by a small margin (GMTR 1.43 vs 1.45 for B/Yam; 1.25 vs 1.28 for B/Vic). There were only two aQIVc formulations [aQIVc (45,1) and aQIVc (45,2)] that showed HI GMT ratio more than 1.0 for all 4 strains. Of note, the formulations with the lesser amount of antigen and adjuvant did not show any improvement over the unadjuvanted QIVc.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

aQIVc 15,1 showed higher titres than QIVc 15,0 in study 200\\_10 but on the contrary lower titres in 201\\_01. The applicant acknowledged the lower GMTR in aQIVc versus QIVc in study V201\\_01 and stated that it did not find any plausible explanation. A confirmatory study was initiated (V201\\_07) to address this issue.

In terms of percentage of subjects with HI titre ≥1:40, the numbers were generally comparable between the different formulations.

Seroconversion rates (SCR, defined as a ≥4 -fold increase in titre or a postvaccination titre ≥1:40) were similar for all vaccines for H1N1 and B/Victoria strains (with the exception of lower seroconversion for aQIVc9(15,3) compared to QIVc). aQIVc7(45,2) showed the higher SCR for H3N2 (17.9% more than QIVc). While it also showed superior SCR than QIVc for B/Yamagata (9.6% difference), the highest SCR was seen in subjects vaccinated with aQIVc11(45,3) (11.7% difference with QIVc).

## Secondary analysis:

Immunogenicity at D29 by the MN assay, the GMT of MN titres for all aQIVc formulations and for the QIVc comparator were considerably higher than for the HI titres. The GMTR was above 1 for all but the aQIVc (15,1 and 15,3) formulations. The seroconversion rates were higher than for HI titres and in general the SCR were higher for the aQIVc than the QIVc. For the MN assay, the GMT at D181 were higher than at D29, except for A/H3N2 strain.

HI titres at Day 181 showed reduced GMTs against H1N1 for all aQIVc formulations and comparable titres against B strains with aQIVc11(45,3) and aQIVc7(45,2) showing the highest GMT ratio for B/Yamagata (1.09) and B/Victoria (1.23), respectively.

The applicant states that Day 181 samples were tested in a different testing campaign as compared to the Day 1 and Day 29 serum samples, and explained that this was done to obtain Day 29 results soon to proceed with the dose selection for further studies. The applicant acknowledged that this makes comparison between Day 181 and Day 1/29 difficult, and stated that this problem is exclusive to Study V201\\_01, and not a concern for other studies. Subgroup analyses showed similar results with one exception. Analysis by gender showed that only in females, unadjuvanted QIVc showed superior immunogenicity to the other vaccines for H1N1 (HI and MN, GMTs and SCR).

In conclusion, V201\\_01 dose finding study showed that increasing the dose of antigen from 15µg to 45µg improved the immune response. At D29, the aQIVc7 formulation with 45 μg of each HA antigen and twice the adjuvant dose in aQIV elicited an immune response marginally higher than the formulation aQIVc(45,1) containing 45 μg of each HA antigen and same adjuvant dose as aQIV. Both aQIVc(45,2) and aQIVc(45,1) elicited overall a higher immune response than unadjuvanted QIVc and other formulations. Study 201\\_01 supports the selected formulation.

At D181, the immune response was higher in the QIVc than in any of the adjuvanted formulations.  It is not clear to what extent this modest improvement in immunogenicity will translate in clinical efficacy.

## Study V201\\_07: Dose confirmation

Study V201\\_07 was an adjuvant dose-confirmation Phase 2b study which assessed the immunogenicity of three investigational vaccines with 45 μg of each HA antigen and three different adjuvant doses (no adjuvant,  the  standard  dose  in  Fluad  and  a  double  dose  equivalent  to  19.50  mg  of  squalene)  in comparison to unadjuvanted QIVc (Flucelvax,15 μg of each HA antigen).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The primary immunogenicity objective was to analyse the different formulations through pairwise comparisons and trend analysis of HI immunogenicity at Day 29 using a cell-derived assay. The secondary objective used the same type of analysis but with cell-derived MN results at Day 29. Exploratory analysis assessed the percentage of subjects with a cell-derived HI titre higher than preestablished levels (1:80, 1:160, 1:320) at Day 29.

The study was conducted in the US, and it enrolled 1056 subjects aged 50 to 93 years of age. 52.1% of subjects were aged 50-64 years of age, meeting the initial plan of enrolling 50% of subjects in each age group. 58% of the subjects were female, and 72% of the subjects had been vaccinated for influenza within the 3 previous seasons. Demographic characteristics were well balanced between the different intervention groups, which were randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio. The primary objective was assessed in the PPS Immunogenicity set, which consisted of 1022 subjects.

According to the CSR, a total of 31 major deviations from GCP were recorded. 4 of them involved the mishandling of samples and required additional blood draws (these are excluded from PPS) and the rest of them involve the inclusion of the subject name in the ERT. The applicant justified that these deviations are not expected to have a significant impact on the results, and it is agreed.

The primary immunogenicity analysis compared the four vaccines at Day 29 in terms of GMTs, HI antibody titres ≥ 1:40 and seroconversion rates, using the cell-derived HI assay. The GMT ratio versus QIVc of HI titres was &gt;1 for all doses, and slightly increased with the increased dose of adjuvant, except for the aQIVc 45,1 for the B/Victoria strain. Pairwise comparison of the 45µg/strain doses showed that the ratio of GMTR for the aQIVc 45,2 was statistically significant (the lower bound of the 95% CI was &gt;1) versus aQIVc 45,1 and aQIVc 45,0 for all strains, whereas the ratio of GMTR for aQIVc 45.1 versus aQIVc 45,0 was not statistically significant (the lower bound of the 95% CI was not &gt;1) for any strain. aQIVc (45,2) was statistically superior (lower bound of 95% Confidence Interval &gt;1.0) to licensed QIVc as assessed by GMT ratio for all four strains.

In all these parameters and for all strains, aQIVc (45,2) showed always the superior immune response. Additionally, trend analysis showed a positive correlation between higher adjuvant dose and higher GMT titres.

These results were confirmed in the secondary analysis using MN assay, and in the exploratory analysis with different pre-established HI titres.

In  all  these  analyses  aQIVc  (45,2)  was  statistically  superior  (lower  bound  of  95%  Confidence Interval &gt;1.0) to licensed QIVc as assessed by GMT ratio for all four strains. The percent of subjects with HI titres ≥ 1:40 is high (above 90%) for all formulations and strains and is higher for the aQIVc than QIVc for all doses, except aQIVc 45,0 for B/Victoria strain.

The percentage difference in HI SCR was positive for all aQIVc dose groups compared with QIVc, across all strains. In addition, the percentage differences for aQIVc 45,2 versus both aQIVc 45,1 and aQIVc 45,0 were positive for all strains.

The secondary endpoint explored the immunogenicity by MN assay. The GMTR of MN titres aQIVc versus the QIVc was &gt;1 for all doses and for all strains, with a slight increase with the increased adjuvant dose. Pairwise comparison of the ratio of GMTR for different aQIVc doses followed the same pattern as for the HI titres. The % difference in HI seroconversion rate (SCR, defined as a ≥4 -fold increase in titre or a postvaccination titre ≥1:40) was positive for all doses of aQIVc versus QIVc, for all strains.

Subgroup analyses showed similar results with the exception that aQIVc (45,1) elicited the higher HI GMT titres for B/Victoria only in individuals aged 65 years of age or older. No subgroup analysis by gender was performed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In conclusion, V201\\_07 demonstrated that aQIVc formulation with 45 μg of each HA antigen and an adjuvant dose equivalent to 19.50 mg of squalene (twice the adjuvant dose in Fluad) was statistically superior (lower bound of 95% Confidence Interval &gt;1.0) to licensed QIVc as assessed by GMT ratio for all four strains and supports this as the selected dose for Phase 3 studies.

## Pivotal Study V210\\_3

## Study design

The V201\\_03 pivotal study is a phase 3, randomized, observer-blind, controlled, multicentre, clinical study to evaluate immunogenicity and safety of a high-dose MF59-Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Cell-derived Influenza Vaccine (aQIVc HD) in comparison with a Non-adjuvanted Quadrivalent Recombinant Influenza Vaccine (QIVr) and an MF59- Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Egg-derived Influenza Vaccine(aQIV), in adults aged 50 years and older who were healthy or had stable comorbidities that increased their risk of complications from influenza infection.

The trial design had the following aims: to demonstrate the lot-to-lot immunological equivalence between three consecutive production lots and to compare the immunogenicity and safety of aQIVc HD compared to two comparator vaccines: QIVr and aQIV, up to 12 months after vaccination. The main study objectives are acceptable, although the Guideline on clinical evaluation of vaccines (EMEA/CHMP/VWP/164653/05 Rev. 1) does not require the demonstration of lot consistency in the clinical studies.

The primary objective was analysed in the overall population of participants aged ≥50 years and in two predefined age subgroups: 5064 years and ≥65 years. The approximately equal allocation of participants across these two age strata is considered appropriate, as it ensured adequate representation of both subgroups and supported reliable conclusions across the age range studied.

The number of participants, 7699 male and female subjects, is considered adequate for a pivotal Phase 3 study designed to address the above-mentioned objectives. Other subpopulations are considered to analyse specific questions (HI-Egg subset, Long-Term immunogenicity subset and CMI subset). The number of participants (3803 subjects, 774 subjects and 100 subjects, respectively) is adequate.

Immunogenicity was measured by the HI (Hemagglutination Inhibition) and Microneutralization (MN) assay by titrating antibodies against the homologous influenza strains. For the primary immunogenicity objectives, HI antibody responses using a cell-derived target virus were evaluated for all strains 28 days after vaccination at Day 29. The use of the HI assay as the primary assay to assess vaccine immunogenicity is consistent with the recommendations of the Guideline on Influenza Vaccines (Non-clinical and clinical module) (EMA/CHMP/VWP/457259/2014).

The applicant plans to conduct an efficacy study with relative efficacy of aTIVc vs Fluad (aTIV) as the primary objective. The applicant provided a summary of the study plan and informed that, although originally planned for 2027, its timing is being reassessed.

## Inclusion and exclusion criteria

The  main  inclusion  criteria  (age  ≥50  years  at  enrolment  and  being  healthy  or  having  stable comorbidities) are considered appropriate, as they reflect the population for whom the indication is sought while excluding participants with unstable or complex comorbidities that could confound the study results. Regarding exclusion criteria, they are considered adequate.

## Randomization and blinding

For the overall study population, subjects were randomized to one of three lots of aQIVc HD or one of the comparator vaccines (aQIV or QIVr), in a 1:1:1:2:2 ratio (for a 3:2:2 ratio for aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV), so that they were evenly distributed taking into consideration the following aspects: age group, influenza vaccination history and site. This is acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In addition, there was a subpopulation that was randomized, the HI-Egg subset (including 70% of the subjects that received aQIVc HD or aQIV). The other two subpopulations (Long-term subset and CMI subset) were selected via convenience sampling. The convenience sampling selection is not the ideal way to make up a subset of subjects that undergo a specific analysis but considering that they are very small subpopulations (770 subjects for the long-term and 100 subjects for the CMI), the impact of not being randomized is considered negligible.

The V201\\_03 study is observer-blind, which is acceptable.

## Trial intervention

The investigational vaccine is aQIVc HD, and there are two comparator vaccines, which are aQIV and QIVr.

The aQIV (Fluad) is an inactivated, egg-based, adjuvanted vaccine, containing 15 μ g HA/strain and MF59 adjuvant containing 9.75 mg squalene, in a total volume of 0.5 mL. Fluad is authorised in the EU in adults from 50 years of age and older. Efficacy of Fluad in adults &gt; 65 years of age and respectively in  adults  50-64  years  of  age  was  inferred  from  two  comparative  immunogenicity  studies  which demonstrated non-inferiority relative to a non-adjuvanted vaccine.

The choice of aQIV as a comparator was questioned in Scientific Advice (SA) (EMA/SA/0000077078) for several reasons:

- at the time of seeking advice, aQIV was not licensed in the EU for 50 years of age and older, but at the time of this MAA submission this issue is solved because in 2024 the indication of aQIV was extended to 50 years and above.
- CHMP considered that QIVc would be a more suitable comparator, since it is manufactured in the same platform (cell culture) and the comparison with aQIVc would inform the effect of both increasing the antigen dose and adding the adjuvant. The issues regarding the eggadaptation that occur with egg-based vaccines (such as aQIV) would not be present if the comparator was QIVc.
- The rational for the selection of aQIV as the comparator had not been provided by the applicant at the time of the SA. Now the applicant has provided an explanation which is acceptable. They explain that aQIV is an enhanced vaccine (indicated for older adults who are at increased risk and with immunosenescence) as is aQIVc HD, and for this reason it can be a better comparator than QIVc. Regarding the different response that aQIVc HD and aQIV may have, depending on if the vaccine is manufactured in a cell-based or egg-based platform, it is known that aQIV and aQIVc can perform differently for this reason. This issue has been tackled by introducing the analysis using both cell-target viruses and egg-target viruses. In conclusion, aQIV can be considered a valid comparator. Moreover, a direct comparison to QIVc was evaluated in supportive study V201\\_07.

The other comparator, QIVr (Flublok/Supemtek), is a recombinant, prepared in cell culture vaccine also an enhanced influenza vaccine due to its high dose of recombinant HA antigen content (45 μ g HA/strain (non-adjuvanted, in a total volume of 0.5 mL). Supemtek is authorised in EU for prevention of influenza disease in adults and children from 9 years of age and older. Supemtek Tetra relative efficacy was demonstrated in adults ≥ 50 years of age comparatively to an egg -based quadrivalent inactivated influenza vaccine. Inclusion of this vaccine as a second comparator in pivotal Study V201\\_03 is intended to  provide  further  regulatory  flexibility,  allowing  adaptation  to  the  preferences  of  national  advisory boards. QIVr (Flublok/Supemtek) is authorised in the EU but is not widely used in Europe. Therefore, it is accepted that the applicant uses this additional comparator, but this assessment will mainly be based on the results from the comparison between aQIVc HD and aQIV.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Considering there are several vaccines specifically intended for older subjects, either adjuvanted or high-dose of antigen, the proposed vaccine does not address an unmet medical need.

## Primary objectives

There were three primary objectives in the V201\\_03 study. One of them was to demonstrate lot-to-lot consistency of 3 consecutive lots of aQIVc HD in terms of immunogenicity. In the EU, the demonstration of lot-to-lot consistency is not a regulatory requirement, but the objective is acceptable since it has been included to comply with other regulatory authorities.

The other two objectives were to demonstrate immunological non-inferiority of aQIVc HD vs the two comparator vaccines (aQIV or QIVr) for each vaccine strain in subjects 50 years of age and older, which is the age for which the indication is sought, using cell-derived target viruses, at day 29.

These two primary objectives are adequate although redundant, since they both aim to demonstrate non-inferiority in the same terms (immunogenicity endpoints, age and assay), but with two different comparators. It is understood that the use of aQIV as comparator is intended to inform EU regulatory authorities,  since  in  older  adults  aQIV  is  widely  used  in  this  territory,  whereas  the  use  of  QIVr  as comparator is intended to inform other regulatory authorities in whose territories QIVr is the mainly used vaccine in the older adult population.

Although all submitted data (coming from the aQIV arm or the QIVr arm of the study) have been assessed and discussed, it is considered that only the aQIV arm would have been sufficient for a MAA for the EU.

The success criteria for all three objectives are acceptable.

## Primary endpoints

All primary endpoints, i.e. the lot-to-lot consistency and the non-inferiority, are considered adequate. The immunogenicity primary endpoints were agreed with in the CHMP Scientific advice.

The use of cell-derived target viruses for the HI assay in the analysis of the primary endpoint is suitable since aQIVc HD is a vaccine prepared in cell culture and one of the advantages that it aims to provide is  an  improved strain match as compared to vaccines prepared in eggs. Nevertheless, as it will be commented in the secondary objectives, the performance of aQIVc HD has also been tested using eggderived target viruses, and this is acknowledged for completeness of the data.

As two comparators would be analysed in parallel, an α -adjustment using the Bonferroni method was applied in the evaluation of non-inferiority (primary objective) and each test was performed with 0.025 (2-sided) significance level. A gatekeeping procedure with Bonferroni-Holm adjustment was applied to test the key secondary objectives.

All primary immunogenicity analyses were conducted in the PPS Immunogenicity in a sequential manner on  the  immunogenicity  data  collected  up  to  Day  29.  The  FAS  Immunogenicity  was  used  for  key secondary immunogenicity analyses of statistical superiority. The PPS Immunogenicity was used for key secondary immunogenicity analysis of noninferiority.

The applicant was asked to provide the estimand table with explanation of strategies to handle the intercurrent events. The provided information did not fully follow the estimand framework as defined in ICH E9 R1 (population and strategy to handle the intercurrent events are not defined, a clear estimand statement is not specified). Nevertheless, considering the low number of subjects excluded from the FAS, the issue was not further pursued.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Secondary objectives

There were four key secondary objectives: non-inferiority vs aQIV in 65 years and older, superiority vs aQIV in 50 years and older, superiority vs QIVr in 50 years and older and superiority vs aQIV in 65 years and older, in all cases using cell-derived target viruses. These objectives are all relevant.

The demonstration that the vaccine is non-inferior in people aged 65 years and above is important since in this age there is an increased risk of complications after an influenza infection, and this age group usually presents a weakened immune response.

The demonstration of superiority is pertinent, because aQIVc HD has been formulated with the intention to  be  a  vaccine  with  an  improved  performance,  and  therefore  the  analysis  of  superiority  is  very important. This objective is analysed in the 50 years and older population (for which the indication is sought) and in the 65 years and older population, which are in an increased risk of influenza infection complications and frequently present a weakened  response.  Also, it  was  mentioned  in  SA (EMA/SA/0000077078) that superiority should be an objective.

There  were  seven  other  secondary  objectives:  superiority  by  margin  vs  QIVr  in  50  and  above, superiority  by  margin  vs  aQIV  in  65  and  above,  homologous  strains  overall  and  by  age  subgroup, homologous strains overall and by age subgroup using egg-derived target viruses, long-term vs aQIV and QIVr overall and by age subgroup, comparison vs aQIV and QIVr by MN assay, long-term vs aQIV and QIVr by MN assay.

The other secondary objectives are all relevant. The objective to compare aQIVc HD vaccine in terms of superiority by margin is not frequently seen in vaccine assessments but, given that aQIVc HD was expected to induce a higher immune response than the comparators, the applicant has decided to explore this objective, and this is endorsed.

As mentioned above, the comparison of the response induced by aQIVc vs aQIV using egg-derived target viruses for the HI assay is especially relevant, since aQIV is an egg-based vaccine and it is important to have the HI data using both cell and egg-derived viruses in order to see the impact that the origin of the virus has in the results obtained from the HI assay.

The analysis of the long-term immunogenicity is pertinent because influenza vaccines are recommended to be administered annually and therefore, they are expected to protect for at least one season. The objective to collect data up to 12 months is endorsed. The assessment of long-term immunogenicity is in line with the above-mentioned Influenza Vaccine Guideline.

## Secondary endpoints

The endpoints for the key secondary objectives are acceptable and suitable for their purpose.

The endpoints for the other secondary objectives are also endorsed. The HI assay using egg-derived target viruses was carried out in a subset (approximately 70% of the total enrolled population), which is  acceptable.  Long-term  immune  responses  were  analysed  at  different  time  points,  which  are agreeable. For two of the secondary endpoints, the MN assay was used instead of the HI assay. It has been described that H3N2 viruses tend to agglutinate less and sometimes present problems in the HI assay, therefore MN assay is typically used in the analysis of influenza vaccines for the H3N2 viruses. Consequently, the use of this assay in two of the secondary endpoints is endorsed.

Also, some of the endpoints aimed to analyse the objectives in age subgroups (50-64 years of age and 65 years and above). As commented above for the key secondary endpoints, this separate analysis is pertinent since older adults (65 years and above) are in higher risk of influenza-related complications and usually present a diminished response compared to younger adults.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Exploratory objectives

Two of the exploratory objectives (to characterize the CMI response and the anti-NA immunity in aQIVc HD and the comparators) are frequently studied in the analysis of influenza vaccines and in line with the Guideline on Influenza Vaccines Non-clinical and Clinical Module (EMA/CHMP/VWP/457259/2014) recommendations. The results coming from these objectives contribute to the better understanding of how  influenza  vaccines  work  and  provide  information  regarding  the  immune  response  induced  by influenza vaccines in additional aspects than only HA-neutralizing antibodies. It will be interesting to compare  the  CMI  and  the  anti-NA  immunity  induced  QIVr,  that  only  contains  HA  as  an  antigen. Therefore, these two objectives are acknowledged.

The study of the long-term immunity of aQIVc HD vs aQIV by age subgroup and using egg-derived target viruses is an adequate objective that will complete the set of data coming from assays that use egg-derived viruses.

The assessment of the immunogenicity for H3N2 heterologous vaccine strains measured by MN assay using  cell-derived  target  viruses  is  considered  adequate,  since  it  will  inform  on  the  breadth  of  the response in the assay (MN) that is more suitable for H3N2 strains, that tend to not agglutinate red blood cells in the HI assay.

The assays and endpoints chosen are suitable and adequate for the analysis and assessment of the exploratory endpoints.

## Populations analysed

The different analysis populations are well defined and described.

## Statistical methodology for the primary endpoint analysis

All primary immunogenicity analyses were conducted in the PPS Immunogenicity population, which is the preferred population for this analysis, so this is endorsed. The primary analysis was done in a sequential  manner,  with  the  analysis  of  the  lot-to-lot  consistency  endpoint  first  and  after  its confirmation,  the  non-inferiority  was  analysed.  This  is  accepted,  although  in  the  Scientific  Advice (EMA/SA/0000077078) the CHMP gave the opinion that the comparison between vaccines should be done regardless of whether the lot-to-lot consistency analysis was successful.

The statistical hypotheses for the primary analyses are endorsed.

## Statistical methodology for the secondary endpoint analysis

The FAS Immunogenicity population was used for key secondary immunogenicity analyses of statistical superiority. The PPS Immunogenicity population was used for key secondary immunogenicity analysis of noninferiority. The reason for using different analysis populations (FAS for superiority and PPS for non-inferiority) is not understood and no rationale is provided by the applicant. In the above-mentioned Scientific Advice, the CHMP gave the opinion that it was preferable that the superiority tests would be performed in the same population as the primary analysis (PPS). No justification was requested from the applicant, so this issue is not further pursued.

Superiority by margin would be tested if statistical superiority for the same comparator and age group was confirmed. This is endorsed.

For  the  other  immunogenicity  endpoints,  the  same  methodology  was  used  as  for  the  primary  and secondary endpoints. This is acceptable.

## Subgroup Analyses

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Several  subgroup  analyses  were  carried  out,  in  the  primary  and  in  the  secondary  and  exploratory endpoints.

In the primary endpoint, non-inferiority of aQIVc HD vs QIVr was additionally evaluated in the subset of  subjects  randomized  in  countries  where  US-licensed  Flublok  was  used  as  the  QIVr  comparator vaccine (US, Canada, and Pakistan). This analysis is not considered very informative for the EU MAA, but is accepted as it is intended to be useful for other regulatory authorities.

In the secondary and exploratory endpoints, the population was analysed in age subgroups and also by sex, influenza vaccination history, pre-vaccination titre and country. All these subgroup analyses are acknowledged, since they will render interesting information.

## Sample Size Determination, Multiplicity adjustment and Interim Analyses

The calculations for the sample size are endorsed. More detail can be found in the Statistical Analysis Plan.

Because of the two comparators being analysed in parallel, an alpha adjustment using the Bonferroni method was applied to the evaluation of non-inferiority (primary objective). Each test was performed with 0.025 (2sided) significance level (ie, α/2=0.0125, 1 -sided). This approach is endorsed.

The applicant states that no formal interim analysis was performed. Nevertheless, the study analysis was performed in a stepwise fashion. This is acceptable.

## Amendments in the SAP and other changes

The SAP was amended four times during the study. The amendments are not considered to affect the integrity of the data from the study.

After the study was unblinded, two corrections were made in the clinical database, which do not affect the study results.

The applicant states that 15 site audits have been performed by the Sponsor and that the sites were selected based on the study's risk-based audit plan. No GCP inspections have been carried out or are planned by regulatory authorities. This is acceptable.

## V201\\_03 Study Results

The current protocol version is 4.0. There were 3 Protocol amendments during the study. In the last protocol amendment (to version 4.0), the main changes were: the safety follow-up to 12 months will include all enrolled subjects and not only a 10% subset and the dataset for subjects &gt;65 years of age included in the egg-based immunogenicity analyses is increased from 2,750 (50%) to 3,850 (70%) to increase  statistical  power.  In  the  previous  amendment  (to  version  3.0),  study  objectives  were substantially rearranged, at the CBER's request. The non-inferiority vs QIVr and superiority vs aQIV in  &gt;65 years old was added to key secondary endpoints. The key secondary superiority by margin endpoints in &gt;50 years old vs QIVr became 'other secondary' and superiority by margin in &lt;65 years old vs aQIV was added to 'other secondary endpoints'.  Finally, in the 1 st  amendment (to version 2.0), the  difference  in  SCR  was  deleted  from  the  key  secondary  objectives  and  long  term  (12  months) immunogenicity assessment was introduced. Overall, the amendments led to a reinforced assessment of immunogenicity.

The primary immunogenicity analyses and key secondary immunogenicity analyses were conducted in both the PPS Immunogenicity and FAS Immunogenicity. Sensitivity analyses for these primary and key secondary immunogenicity analyses were conducted in the FAS Immunogenicity, which confirmed the results observed in the PPS Immunogenicity. All descriptive secondary immunogenicity analyses were conducted in both the PPS Immunogenicity and FAS Immunogenicity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A total of 7699 subjects 50 years of age and older were randomized in the study, with 99.7% of them receiving the study vaccine. The majority of the subjects (97.6%) completed the study up to day 181 and most of them (80.2%) completed it up to day 365. The proportion of subjects receiving the vaccine and finalising the study is considered sufficient and adequate for the analysis of the data.

There were 505 major protocol deviations (PD), a 6.6% of the overall population. The proportion of subjects with major PD is very low and it is not considered to affect the results of the study.

## Demographic and baseline characteristics

The study was conducted at 96 centres in 8 countries (Canada, Denmark, Estonia, Germany, Pakistan, Philippines, United Kingdom, and United States). The long-term subset was conducted only in the US. The mean (SD) age of the study population was 64.3 (8.28) years, and the range was 50 to 95 years, which was consistent with the intended study population. The age distribution was 49.8% of 50-64 years of age and 50.2% of 65 years of age or older (about half were &gt; 65 years old). In the exposed set, a higher proportion of females than males was enrolled: 4,308/7,677 subjects (56.1%) were female and 3,369/7,677 subjects (43.9%) were male. A previous vaccination history was reported as yes for 60.5% of subjects overall and for 76.7% of subjects in the long-term subset. In the exposed set, 81.5% of subjects had a medical history of at least one medical disorder. Overall, demographic characteristics are considered well balanced across study arms.

## Primary Immunogenicity Results

For  lot-to-lot  consistency,  the  prespecified  success  criteria  for  demonstrating  consistency  of  3 consecutive aQIVc HD lots were met in subjects aged 50 years and older.

Immunological noninferiority of aQIVc HD in subjects aged 50 years and older, as measured by HI assay using cell-derived target viruses. The criteria were: the lower limit (LL) of the 2-sided 97.5% CI for the adjusted Day 29 GMT ratio (aQIVc HD/comparator) ≥ 0.67 and the LL of the 2-sided 97.5% CI for the Day 29 SCR difference (aQIVc HD minus comparator) was ≥ -10%.

The non-inferiority results for the comparison of aQIVc HD vs QIVr in subjects 50 years and older showed that aQIVc HD was non-inferior to QIVr in three of the four strains, but the non-inferiority was not demonstrated for the H3N2 strain, this was shown both with the GMT and the SCR data. Therefore, the prespecified success criteria for demonstrating immunological noninferiority of aQIVc HD vs QIVr were not met in subjects aged 50 years and older.

For the purpose of US licensure, the non-inferiority of aQIVc HD was also analysed vs QIVr using as comparator only the subjects enrolled in countries where QIVr Flublok Quadrivalent was used (US, Canada and Pakistan). In this case, the non-inferiority criteria were met for the four strains in terms of SCR difference but for the GMT ratio, the H3N2 did not meet the prespecified criteria. This result is consistent  with  that  obtained  using  the  complete  population  that  had  received  QIVr  in  the  other participating countries. The primary endpoint of demonstating non-inferiority of aQIVc HD vs QIVr was not met.

When comparing aQIVc HD with aQIV it was shown that for each of the 4 vaccine strains, for both the GMT ratio and the SCR difference, the prespecified success criteria for demonstrating immunological noninferiority of aQIVc HD vs aQIV were met in subjects aged 50 years and older.

Taken together, the primary analyses showed that the lot-to-lot consistency endpoint was met and that non-inferiority of aQIVc HD was demonstrated versus aQIV, but not versus QIVr. The applicant was asked to clarify the significance of this finding and provided additional immunobridging results for other variables. It is noted that non-inferiority was demonstrated for 3 of the 4 vaccine strains, while for the fourth strain, A/H3N2, the non-inferiority criterion was missed by a very narrow margin. This finding is unlikely  to  have  a  clinically  relevant  impact,  as  other  immunobridging  criteria  were  met  and  were indicative of protection, including SCR and the percentage of subjects with HI antibody titres ≥1:40. The applicant also provided a descriptive analysis showing that non-inferiority would have been met in subjects aged 65 years and older. Overall, the applicant has provided sufficient arguments to support that aQIVc is expected to elicit at least comparable immunogenicity to QIVr, despite narrowly missing one co-primary non-inferiority endpoint. The applicant agreed  to include  a  statement in the SmPC describing the results of the comparison versus QIVr.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

From an EU regulatory perspective, neither the lot-to-lot consistency assessment nor the comparison with QIVr would have been required for the study design to comply with EU requirements. Therefore, the data most relevant to the assessment of the aQIVc HD marketing authorisation application are those from the comparison between the study vaccine and aQIV. On this basis, the primary analysis of study V201\\_03 can be considered successful.

## Key Secondary Results

Key secondary objectives were aimed at characterizing further the comparative between aQIVc HD and the comparators, in the age range of 65 years and above and also, in the case that non-inferiority had been demonstrated, to explore the superiority of aQIVc HD vs the comparator vaccines in the overall age group or in 65 years and above.

Non-inferiority between aQIVc HD and aQIV was demonstrated in subjects 65 years of age and older, as was measured both by GMT ratio and SCR difference, therefore this key secondary endpoint was met.

Superiority in subjects 50 years of age and older was only assessed between aQIVc HD and aQIV, because  this  is  the  only  comparator  for  which  non-inferiority  had  been  demonstrated.  In  the comparative of aQIVc HD vs aQIV superiority criteria were met, so this key secondary endpoint was demonstrated. Moreover, this same endpoint was assessed in older subjects (65 years and above) and superiority of aQIVc HD vs aQIV was also demonstrated in this age group.

The results from the key secondary objectives and endpoints continue with the characterization of the immune response to aQIVc HD as compared to aQIV in the older adult group (65 years and above) and in terms of superiority. aQIVc HD shows an improved immunogenicity compared to aQIV in the overall population (50 years and above) for which the indication is sought and in the older population (65 years and above) that present a higher risk of influenza-derived complications.

## Other Secondary Results

Several other secondary objectives were assessed in the V201\\_03 pivotal trial, most of which were descriptive, except the superiority by margin analysis, that had a statistical hypothesis and predefined statistical criteria.

Superiority by margin (if the LL of the CI for the GMT ratio comparing aQIVc HD vs aQIV was &gt;1.5 for at least one A strain) was demonstrated. The H1N1 met the predefined criteria The two B strains also met  the  criteria,  although  they  were  not  included  in  the  predefined  criteria.  This  statistical  term 'superiority  by  margin'  is  not  frequently  used  in  vaccine  assessments  and  its  clinical  relevance  is unknown, but nevertheless it underlines the clear superiority of aQIVc HD as compared to aQIV in the antibody GMT measurement.

The remaining secondary endpoints were descriptive and are considered relevant.

In all the comparisons and age groups, the performance of aQIVc HD was at least non-inferior to the aQIV comparator. In some endpoints and for some strains the point estimate for aQIVc HD was lower than aQIV but always inside the non-inferiority margin.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In  terms  of  long-term  immunogenicity,  at  D181,  D271  and  D365,  the  GMT  of  HI  titres  decreased considerably and to the same extent for the aQIVc HD and aQIV vaccines.

The results from these descriptive endpoints are very valuable and informative. For example, the longterm immunogenicity data provide reassurance that aQIVc HD is expected to confer protection for at least as long as aQIV, which is one of the main vaccine options available for older adults in the EU. Also, RCD curves contribute with information of how fast the immune response reaches its peak, and it is reassuring that aQIVc HD is at least as fast (or faster) than aQIV. By age group, with the limitation of very small sample size, it seems that the HI titres are decreasing to a lower extent in subjects &gt;65 years of age than in 50-64 years group, for all vaccines and all strains.

The information from the egg-derived target virus assay shows that, even though aQIVc HD vaccine is manufactured in cell culture and not in embryonated eggs, as is aQIV, it achieved comparable antibody measurements even when analysed in an assay that is more favourable for the comparator vaccine. This gives an idea of the breadth of the immune response induced by the aQIVc HD.

Results  from  microneutralization  (MN)  analysis  using  cell-derived  target  viruses  followed  the  same pattern as the HI titres, GMT and SCR were higher for aQIVc HD for all strains as compared to aQIV.

## Exploratory results

No results are presented for the exploratory objectives and endpoints; the applicant states that they will be submitted in a subsequent version of the CSR. This is acceptable, since the data from these exploratory analyses will not change the overall benefit-risk balance of the aQIVc HD.

## Subgroup analysis

Many subgroup analyses were performed, by age, sex, vaccination history, pre-vaccination titre and country.  Also,  the  analyses  were  carried  out  both  in  the  PPS  immunogenicity  and  in  the  FAS immunogenicity populations and they were done using cell-derived and egg-derived target viruses for the HI assay and using cell-derived target viruses for the MN assay.

In summary, subgroup analysis showed results that were in agreement with the results coming from the overall population. In all subgroups analysis there is a repeated observation: in the HI assay using cell-derived target viruses, aQIVc HD induced a higher response than aQIV in all groups and strains, but in the HI assay using egg-derived target viruses, values for the H1N1 strain were higher for aQIV than for aQIVc HD. Cell-based HI assays identically match the cell-based vaccine virus strains, while egg-based HI assays identically match the egg-based vaccine virus strains. Therefore, it is expected that each vaccine will have higher immunogenicity results with each corresponding assay. Cell-derived viruses are expected to be more similar to circulating strains due to the lack of eggadaptive mutations, and thus the results from the cell-based HI assay could be considered more relevant.

In the analysis of immunogenicity by sex, the D29 GMT of HI titres using the cell derived target virus assay were higher in female than male subjects for aQIVc HD. When using the egg derived target virus assay, the GMT were higher in males for aQIVc HD and aQIV.

When  analysing  the  immunogenicity  by  influenza  vaccination  history,  2,938  subjects  were  not previously vaccinated and 4,481 were previously vaccinated. At D29 the HI titres were higher in those not previously vaccinated. Long-term immunogenicity was evaluated in a subset (144 not previously vaccinated and 585 previously vaccinated). For subjects in the aQIVc HD group, the long-term HI titres were higher in the not previously vaccinated and HI titres ≥ 1:40 were higher for A/H1N1 in previously vaccinated and for B/Victoria were higher in not previously vaccinated.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Immunogenicity analyses by country showed high variability in results, especially for countries with fewer  subjects  thus  analyses  should  be  approached  with  caution.  In  the  EU  countries,  higher immunogenicity of aQIVc HD was observed compared to aQIV, as evidenced by GMFI ratios for all four strains.

## Ancillary analysis

Post hoc immunogenicity analyses were performed in different subgroups, i.e. age, race/ethnicity, comorbidity risk score and combinations of these (race and age, ethnicity and age, comorbidity risk score and age) with the purpose to inform regulatory authorities and national immunization advisory groups.

The results obtained from this post hoc analysis are in line with those observed in the overall study population. Conclusions from this analysis should be taken with caution due to the low number of subjects in some subgroups and to the post hoc nature of the analysis itself.

## 5.3.4.2. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

The aQIVc HD is the first influenza vaccine combining a high dose of antigen (45µg/strain) and a high dose of adjuvant MF-59 (19.5mg, 2 times the dose currently authorised for aQIV). This combination is intended to improve the immune response in subjects 50 years and above.

The efficacy of aQIVc HD is inferred from comparative immunogenicity studies. All studies were conducted in the target population, i.e. subjects 50 years of age and above. The applicant submitted a comprehensive set of data showing that aQIVc HD vaccine presents a statistically superior immunogenicity in GMTs and SCR as compared to aQIV. This superior response was demonstrated in the overall population (50 years and above) for which the indication is sought. It was also observed in participants aged ≥65 years, a subgroup that commonly shows reduced immune responsiveness and is at increased risk of influenza-related complications. Other subgroup analyses were in line with the observations made in the overall population.

In conclusion, the aQIVc HD is able to elicit an immune response at D29, non-inferior to QIVr (except for the A/H3N2 strain, for which the NI criterion was not met) and superior to aQIV. Persistence of immune response up to 12 months is of the same order of magnitude as the comparators. The efficacy results presented by the applicant support MA for aQIVc.

## 5.4. Clinical safety

Adjuvanted trivalent subunit inactivated cell-derived influenza vaccine (aTIVc) is a seasonal, surface antigen, inactivated influenza vaccine, adjuvanted with MF59C. The clinical development program to support licensure of aTIVc in adults 50 years of age and older is supported with safety data from aQIVc.

The aQIVc/aTIVc formulation contains 3 times the standard HA antigen dose (45 µg HA of each strain vs 15 µg in aQIV/aTIV) and 2 times the standard dose of MF59 in aQIV/aTIV (containing 19.5 mg of squalene vs 9.75 mg as standard). The manufacturing process and formulation of aTIVc are the same as that of aQIVc, with the exception of the exclusion of the B/Yamagata viral antigens based on WHO recommendations. Therefore, clinical study experience with aQIVc provides data for assessing the overall safety of aTIVc.

Therefore, assessment of the aTIVc safety profile in this application is based on data from the pivotal aQIVc HD Study V201\\_03, supported with data from supportive studies (V201\\_07 and V201\\_01).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Safety data from supportive Study V200\\_10 are also included as this study investigated addition of adjuvant to QIVc.

## 5.4.1. Safety data collection

The clinical safety database to support the licensure of aTIVc in adults ≥ 50 years of age includes 3645 subjects treated with the aQIVc [aQIVc (45,2)] formulation:

- Study V201\\_03: 3282 subjects (2657 in the 12 Months Safety Set)
- Study V201\\_07: 262 subjects
- Study V201\\_01: 101 subjects

Safety assessments from all studies are presented independently and provide a comparison of the safety profile of the aQIVc HD formulation against other aQIVc formulations and comparator vaccines. No pooling of safety data was performed.

For all studies, safety and tolerability were determined by collection of solicited local and systemic adverse events (AE) for 7 days after vaccination (Day 1 to Day 7) and any unsolicited AEs during the treatment period (Day 1 to Day 29). Serious AEs (SAE), AEs leading to withdrawal, AEs of special interest (AESI) (in Study V201\\_03) and medically-attended adverse events (MAAEs) were collected throughout the entire study duration (i.e., from Day 1 to Day 181).

For pivotal Study V201\\_03, subjects were invited to extend their participation up to the 12 months safety follow-up, thereby providing a subset of subjects with up to 12 months safety data following vaccination. The 12 Months Safety Set consisted of 6246 subjects who were assigned to the Long-Term subset or who consented to 12 months safety follow-up and had post Day 181 unsolicited AE data (ie, attended at least 1 post Day 181 safety assessment).

In the Pivotal study V201\\_03, the demographic characteristics were well balanced among the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV vaccine groups. More female subjects (56.1%) than male subjects (43.9%) were exposed in the study. The majority of subjects were White (74.2%) followed by Asian (19.3%). The study population was predominantly of 'Not Hispanic or Latino' origin (94.3%), with 5.1% of subjects identified as Hispanic or Latino. The mean (SD) body mass index was 28.4 (6.13) kg/m2. Overall, 60.5% of subjects were previously vaccinated against influenza and 39.5% of subjects were not vaccinated against influenza within the 3 previous influenza seasons. 81.5% of participants reported at least 1 medical disorder and 80.1% reported at least 1 concomitant medication.

In the studies V201\\_07 and V201\\_01 demographic characteristics generally were well balanced among the study vaccine groups. In V201\\_07, 52.0% of subjects were 50 to 64 years of age, and 48.0% of subjects were ≥65 years of age, 58.0% of subjects were female and the majority of subjects were White (83.5%). In V201\\_01, 52.6% of subjects were 50 to 64 years of age and 47.4% of subjects were ≥65 years of age, 56.6% of subjects were female (56.6%) and the majority of subjects were White 56.0%).

The study V200\\_10 was the initial proof-of-concept clinical trial and did not evaluate the final formulation of aQIVc (45 µg HA of each strain and 19.5 mg of MF59). Therefore, the safety data from V200\\_10 was not include in the assessment.

## 5.4.3.1.1. Solicited Adverse Events

All subjects exposed to study vaccination and providing solicited safety data were included in the Solicited Safety Set.

The percentage of subjects reporting any solicited AE was highest in the aQIVc HD group, followed by the aQIV group, and lowest in the QIVr group (60.2%, 51.4%, and 41.9%, respectively).

Similar pattern was observed for solicited local and systemic AEs, as well, as for analgesic/antipyretic use.

The percentage of subjects reporting solicited local AEs after vaccination (Table 26) was higher in the aQIVc HD group compared with aQIV and QIVr (47.4%, 36.6%, and 22.4% respectively). The most frequently reported solicited local AE was injection site pain in each of the three groups (45.6%, 34.3%, and 21.3%, respectively). The majority of solicited local AEs were mild or moderate in severity. The percentage of subjects reporting severe events was very low in each of the 3 vaccine groups, and slightly higher in the aQIVc HD group (1.8% of subjects) compared with the aQIV and QIVr groups (0.8% and 0.7%, respectively). The majority of solicited local AEs were of short duration ( ≤ 3 days) and resolved during the 7-day period following vaccination.

The percentage of subjects reporting solicited systemic AEs after vaccination (Table 27) was higher in the aQIVc HD group compared with aQIV and QIVr (43.6%, 36.1%, and 32.7%, respectively). The most frequently reported solicited systemic AEs in the aQIVc HD, aQIV, and QIVr groups were fatigue (32.8%, 23.7%, and 23.1%, respectively) and headache (22.0%, 17.7%, and 16.0%, respectively). Fever was reported in 3.5%, 1.9%, and 2.0% of subjects in the aQIVc HD, aQIV, and QIVr groups, respectively, with severe fever reported in 0.2%, 0.4%, and &lt; 0.1% of subjects, respectively.

The majority of solicited systemic AEs were mild or moderate in severity. The percentage of subjects reporting severe events was very low in each of the 3 vaccine groups, and slightly higher in the aQIVc HD group (3.3% of subjects) compared with the aQIV a nd QIVr groups (≤2.0% of subjects in each group). The majority of solicited systemic AEs were of short duration ( ≤ 3 days) and resolved during the 7-day period following vaccination.

## 5.4.3.1.2. Unsolicited Adverse Events

The percentage of subjects with at least 1 unsolicited AE during the treatment period was similar between the 3 vaccine groups (aQIVc HD: 11.9%; QIVr: 11.8%; aQIV: 11.1%) (Table 28). The most commonly reported unsolicited AEs in the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV groups were COVID-19 (2.1%, 1.9%, and 1.9%, respectively), nasopharyngitis (1.1%, 1.6%, and 1.4%, respectively), and upper respiratory tract infection (0.9%, 1.1%, and 1.0%, respectively). There were no notable differences in the proportion of subjects with any unsolicited AEs during the treatment period across the 3 vaccine groups. The majority of unsolicited AEs were mild or moderate in severity in each vaccine group.

## 5.4.3.3. Adverse event of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events

## Deaths:

From Day 1 to Day 181, 19 deaths were reported and from Day 182 to Day 365 (in the 12-month safety Set), 8 deaths were reported.

Of these 27 deaths, 12 occurred in aQIVc (8 From Day 1 to Day 181 and 4 from Day 182 to Day 365), 6 in aQIV (4 From Day 1 to Day 181 and 2 from Day 182 to Day 365) and 9 in QIVr (7 From Day 1 to Day 181 and 2 from Day 182 to Day 365).

The AEs leading to death were reported across multiple SOCs, most commonly in the SOCs of 'Cardiac Disorders' and 'General disorders and administration site conditions'. None of the AEs leading to death were assessed as related to the study vaccine.

## Serious adverse events

From Day 1 to Day 181, SAEs were reported in:

- 91 of 3282 subjects (2.8%) in aQIVc HD group; of which 15 SAEs were reported from Day 1 to Day 29
- 64 of 2191 subjects (2.9%) in QIVr; of which 13 SAEs were reported from Day 1 to Day 29
- 78 of 2204 subjects (3.5%) in aQIV group; of which 11 SAEs SAEs were reported from Day 1 to Day 29

The most commonly reported SAEs in any vaccine group by Preferred Term were pneumonia (reported in 5, 3, and 4 subjects in the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV groups, respectively) and osteoarthritis

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(reported in 2, 5, and 3 subjects, respectively). All other SAEs were reported in 3 subjects or fewer in each vaccine group.

Abbreviations: AE = adverse event; aQIV = MF59-Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Inactivated Egg-derived Influenza Vaccine; aQIVc HD = High-Dose MF59-Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Inactivated Cell-derived Influenza Vaccine; MedDRA = Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities; PT = Preferred Term; QIVr = Non-adjuvanted Quadrivalent Recombinant Influenza Vaccine; SAE = serious adverse event; SOC = System Organ Class.

Note 1: Subjects are counted once per unique SOC and once per unique PT within the same SOC.

Four SAEs reported by 4 subjects were assessed as related to the study vaccine. All of them reported from Day 1 to Day 181:

- One subject in the aQIVc HD group experienced an SAE of giant cell arteritis (Start date: Study Day 5), which was classified as possibly related to the study vaccine based on a temporal relationship and exclusion of all other potential causes.
- One subject in the aQIVc HD group experienced an SAE of syncope (Study Day 2), which was classified as probably related to the study vaccine based on a temporal relationship.
- One subject in the aQIV group experienced an SAE of ischaemic stroke (Study Day 9), which was classified as possibly related to the study vaccine by the Investigator, based on a temporal relationship. The Sponsor assessed the event of ischemic stroke as not related to the study vaccine because of the biological implausibility for the study vaccine to cause or to contribute to the event. Contributory factors may have been advanced age (elderly).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- One subject in the aQIV group experienced an SAE of diabetic ketoacidosis (Study Day 75), which was classified as possibly related to the study vaccine by the Investigator, based on pharmacological/biological plausibility. The Sponsor assessed this event as not related and more plausibly linked to the underlying condition of Type 2 diabetes mellitus.

## AE of special interest

From Day 1 to Day 181, 16 of 7677 subjects (0.2%) experienced AESIs (aQIVc HD: 9/3282 subjects, 0.3%; QIVr: 2/2191 subjects, &lt;0.1%; aQIV: 5/2204 subjects, 0.2%).

Of the 16 AESIs reported from Day 1 to Day 181:

- 2 AESIs were classified as SAEs, one of which was assessed as possibly related to the study vaccine.
- o The serious, related AESI was an AESI of giant cell arteritis in one subject in the aQIVc HD group, that was classified as an SAE, assessed as possibly related to the study vaccine, and classified as a SUSAR.
- o One subject in the aQIV group experienced an AESI of coeliac disease that was classified as an (unrelated) SAE.
- The remaining 14 subjects experienced AESIs that were classified as non-serious and assessed as not related to the study vaccine; these 14 AESIs were also classified as non-serious MAAEs.
- While the percentage of subjects reporting AESIs was similar for the adjuvanted vaccines aQIVc HD and aQIV, more AESIs were assessed as related to the study vaccine by the Sponsor or the Investigator in the aQIVc HD group.

From Day 182 to Day 365, in the 12 Months Safety Set, from Day 182 to Day 365, AESIs were reported in 3 subjects: 1 subject in the aQIVc HD group (immune thrombocytopenia, which was classified as an SAE); 1 subject in the QIVr group (polymyalgia rheumatica); and 1 subject in the aQIV group (autoimmune thyroiditis). None of the AESIs reported from Day 182 to Day 365 were assessed as related to the study vaccine.

## Medically-Attended Adverse Events

From Day 1 to Day 181, 15.6% in aQIVc, 14.1% in QIVr and 15.1% in aQIV of subjects experienced non-serious MAAEs.

Non-serious MAAEs were most commonly reported in the SOC of 'Infections and infestations'. The most commonly reported non-serious MAAEs in the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV groups were COVID-19 (1.4%, 1.1%, and 0.9%, respectively), upper respiratory tract infection (0.7%, 1.1%, and 0.8%, respectively), and hypertension (0.8%, 0.5%, and 0.6%, respectively).

From Day 182 to Day 365, 7.9% in the aQIVc HD group, 7.4% in the QIVr group, and 7.9% in the aQIV group of subjects reported non-serious MAAES. The most commonly MAAEs reported by SOC was 'Infections and infestations' and by PT, there were included: COVID-19 (0.6%, 0.9%, and 1.0%, respectively), hypertension (0.4%, 0.3%, and 0.3%, respectively), osteoarthritis (0.2%, 0.4%, and 0.4%, respectively), and back pain (0.3%, 0.1%, and 0.4%, respectively)

## 5.4.3.4. Discontinuation due to adverse events

From Day 1 to Day 365 all of the AEs leading to withdrawal from the study were AEs leading to death.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.3.5. Safety in special populations

## By age

In pivotal Study V201\\_03, evaluation of safety was performed by age subgroup (50-64 yoa and &gt;65 yoa).

In both the 5064 years and ≥65 years age subgroups, the percentage of subjects reporting any solicited local AE was the highest in the aQIVc HD group, followed by the aQIV group, and the lowest in the QIVr group (50-64 years: 54.9%, 43.1%, and 26.8%, resp ectively; ≥65 years: 39.9%, 30.0%, and 18.1%, respectively). The same pattern was observed in both age subgroups for induration, erythema, and injection site pain. Injection site pain was the most commonly reported solicited local AE in each of the 3 vaccine groups and the majority of solicited local AEs were mild or moderate in severity.

Comparing solicited local AEs by age, the percentage of subjects reporting any solicited local AE was higher in the 5064 years age subgroup than in the ≥65 years age subgroup (aQIVc HD: 54.9% vs 39.9%; aQIV: 43.1% vs 30.0%; QIVr: 26.8% vs 18.1%) (Table 34)

In both the 50-64 years and ≥ 65 years age subgroups, the percentage of subjects reporting any solicited systemic AE was higher in the aQIVc HD group than in the aQIV and QIVr groups (50-64 years: 50.6%, 40.4%, and 35.9%, respectively; ≥ 65 years: 36.7%, 31.7%, and 29.6%, respectively). For each of the individual solicited systemic AEs, the percentage of subjects reporting an event was higher in the aQIVc HD group, with lower percentages of subjects reporting these events in the QIVr and aQIV groups in both age subgroups. The majority of solicited systemic AEs were mild or moderate in severity. The percentage of subjects reporting severe solicited systemic AEs was slightly higher in the aQIVc HD group compared with the aQIV and QIVr groups (4.8%, 2.2%, and 2.3%, respectively). In the ≥ 65 years age subgroup, the percentage of subjects reporting severe solicited systemic AEs was similar between the 3 vaccine groups (1.8%, 1.8%, and 1.4%, respectively).

Comparing solicited systemic AEs by age, the percentage of subjects reporting any solicited systemic AE was higher in the 50-64 years age subgroup than in the ≥ 65 years age subgroups (aQIVc HD: 50.6% vs 36.7%; aQIV: 40.4% vs 31.7%; QIVr: 35.9% vs 29.6%) (Table 35).

= Non-adjuvanted Quadrivalent Recombinant Influenza Vaccine.
