<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In both the 50-64 years and ≥ 65 years of age subgroups, the percentage of subjects reporting with at least 1 unsolicited AE were similar between aQIVc HD, QIVr and aQIV groups (50-64 years: 12.8%, 11.6%, and 10.3%, respectively; ≥ 65 years: 10.9%, 12.0%, and 11.9%, respectively). The majority of unsolicited AEs in each vaccine group were mild or moderate in severity.

In 50-64 years of age group, the percentage of subjects with unsolicited AEs assessed as related to the study vaccine was very low in the 3 vaccine groups, with slightly higher rates in the aQIVc HD group compared with the QIVr and aQIV groups (1.8%, 0.6%, and 0.8%, respectively) and in &gt;65 age group, the percentage of subjects with unsolicited AEs assessed as related to the study vaccine was very low and similar in the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV groups (0.6%, 0.8%, and 0.6%, respectively)

Comparing age groups, the percentages of subjects with unsolicited AEs were similar in the 50-64 years and ≥65 years of age subgroups (aQIVc HD: 12.8% vs 10.9%; QIVr: 11.6% vs 12.0%; aQIV: 10.3% vs 11.9%). In addition, in the aQIVc HD group, the percentage of subjects with related unsolicited AEs was slightly higher in the 5064 years of age subgroup than the ≥65 years of age subgroup (1.8% vs 0.6%)

In pivotal Study V201\\_03, from Day 1 to Day 181, the percentage of SAE was low and similar between vaccine groups in both age groups.  In each of the 3 vaccine groups, the percentage of subjects with SAEs was slightly lower in the 50 64 years of age subgroup than in the ≥ 65 years of age subgroup (aQIVc HD: 2.5% vs 3.0%; QIVr: 1.9% vs 3.8%; aQIV: 2.3% vs 4.4%). In 50-64 years of age group, none of the SAEs were assessed as related to the study vaccine. However, in &gt;65 years of age group, 4 SAEs reported in 4 subjects were assessed as related to the study vaccine (2 subjects each in the aQIVc HD and aQIV groups).

AESIs were reported in 6 (0.4%), 0, and 1 (&lt;0.1%) subjects, respectively in 50-64 years age group and in 3 (0.2%), 2 (0.2%), and 4 (0.4%) subjects respectively in &gt;65 years age group.

From Day 182 to Day 365, in the aQIVc HD and aQIV groups, the percentage of subjects with SAEs was slightly lower in the 50-64 years age subgroup than the ≥ 65 years age subgroups (CSR V201\\_03, aQIVc HD: 0.5% vs 1.7%; aQIV: 1.0% vs 1.8%). In the QIVr group, the percentage of subjects with SAEs was similar between the 50-64 years and ≥ 65 years age subgroups (1.5% vs 1.6%)

## 5.4.4. Supportive studies (V201\\_07 and V201\\_01)

## 5.4.4.1. Adverse events

## 5.4.4.2. Solicited Adverse Events

## Study V201\\_07

In Study V201\\_07, the rate of solicited local AEs was higher when increasing HA and/or MF59 adjuvant. The proportions of subjects reporting solicited local AEs were higher in QIVc (45,0), aQIVc (45,1), and aQIVc (45,2) (37.2%, 45.5%, and 58.6%, respectively) as compared to QIVc (15,0) (23.6%). The rates of systemic solicited AEs were mostly similar across the treatment groups (range: 36.5% to 49.8%) and no significant difference between treatment groups were observed. Table 36 and Table 37 provide an overview of the solicited local and systemic AEs in Study V201\\_07.

## Study V201\\_01

In Study V201\\_01, the rates of local solicited events were higher in subjects receiving higher amount of antigen and higher amount of adjuvant (2x or 3x) [aQIVc (30,3), 68.6%; aQIVc (45,3), 67.6%; and aQIVc (30,2), 66.0%], with the exception of aQIVc (45,2), 54.5%, as were the rates of systemic solicited events [aQIVc (30,3), 62.9%; aQIVc (45,3), 59.3%; and aQIVc (30,2), 57.3%], with the exception of aQIVc (45,2), 45.5%. ## 5.4.4.3. Unsolicited Adverse Events

## Study V201\\_07

There were no notable differences in the proportion of subjects reporting unsolicited AEs across vaccine groups. The proportion of subjects reporting related unsolicited AEs was low overall (diarrhoea in 6 subjects and blood pressure increased in 2 subjects were the only related unsolicited AEs reported in &gt;1 subject) and there was no significant difference across vaccine groups

## Study V201\\_01

There were no notable differences in the proportion of subjects reporting unsolicited AEs across the vaccine groups. The proportion of subjects reporting related unsolicited AEs was low overall. The proportion of related unsolicited AEs was slightly higher in subjects receiving 3x MF59 adjuvant compared to QIVc. The most common related unsolicited AEs were injection site pain (9 subjects in total) and arthralgia and fatigue (8 subjects each)

## 5.4.4.4. Adverse event of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events

## Deaths:

During Study V201\\_07, 1 death occurred (0.1%) in the aQIVc HD (45,2) group due to an acute cardiac event at Day 151. The fatal event was assessed as not related to the study vaccine.

During Study V201\\_01, 4 deaths occurred (0.5%); 1 subject each in QIVc, aQIVc (30,3), aQIVc (45,1), and aQIVc (45,2). The deaths were due to a concomitant disease or infection and assessed as unrelated to the study vaccine. The SAEs that resulted in death were sepsis, pyelonephritis, pneumonia and unknown cause of death, respectively.

## Serious adverse events

In Study V201\\_07 (from Day 1 to Day 181), 37 SAEs were reported in 27 subjects across all vaccine groups; the proportion of subjects reporting SAEs was comparable across vaccine groups. There was one SAE of presyncope reported on Study Day 7 in aQIVc (45,1) which it was considered as possibly related to the study vaccine by the investigator due to temporal relationship. The Sponsor assessed the event of presyncope to be not related to the study vaccine due significant contributory factors in the subject's medical history and concomitant medications.

In Study V201\\_01 (from Day 1 to Day 181), 28 SAEs were reported in 25 subjects across all vaccine groups; the proportion of subjects reporting SAEs was the highest in the QIVc (15,0) vaccine group (8.2%). Only the SAE of polymyalgia rheumatica in a subject receiving aQIVc (30,3) was considered as possibly related to the study vaccine. No other SAEs were considered related to the study vaccine.

## AE of special interest

No AESI was reported in Study V201\\_07

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

During Study V201\\_01, three AESIs were reported;

- one non-serious AESI of psoriasis in the QIVc (15,0) group during treatment period, considered as possibly related to study vaccine,
- one serious adverse event of polymyalgia rheumatica in the aQIVc (30,3) group during the follow-up period, considered as possibly related to study vaccine,
- one non-serious adverse event of rheumatoid arthritis in the aQIVc (30,3) group during the follow-up period, considered as unrelated to the study vaccine.

## Medically-Attended Adverse Events

Overall, during the entire study period in V201\\_07, non-serious medically-attended AEs were reported in comparable proportions across vaccine groups [from 10.2% in QIVc (45,0) to 16.0% in QIVc (15,0)]. The majority MAAE by SOC was 'Infections and infestations' in all groups.

## 5.4.4.5. Discontinuation due to adverse events

In Study V201\\_07, one subject experienced an AE leading to withdrawal from the study at Day 151.This subject, receiving aQIVc (45,2), had an SAE of acute cardiac event that led to withdrawal from the study due to death. The event was severe in intensity and serious in nature as it resulted in a fatal outcome.

In Study V201\\_01, 4 subjects [one subject each from QIVc (15,0), aQIVc (30,3), aQIVc (45,1), and aQIVc (45,2) groups] experienced an AE leading to withdrawal from study, with respectively preferred terms of death, pneumonia, pyelonephritis and sepsis.

## 5.4.4.6. Safety in special populations

In Study V201\\_07, in all vaccine groups, the rate of solicited reactions was higher in subjects aged 50 to 64 years compared to subjects ≥ 65 years, with higher rates of solicited local (28.1% to 63.8% vs 18.9% to 53.3%) and systemic (39.1% to 57.5% vs 33.6% to 41.8%) AEs observed in subjects aged 50 to 64 years compared to subjects aged ≥ 65 years.

The rates of use of antipyretic/analgesics in subjects aged 50 to 64 years were similar across the treatment groups; however, the rates were higher in aQIVc (45,2) group as compared to the other treatment groups in subjects ≥ 65 years. Unsolicited AEs including serious AEs and medically-attended AEs were reported at higher rate in subjects aged ≥ 65 years as compared to 50-64 years age group in QIVc (45,0) and aQIVc (45,1) vaccine groups, whereas the unsolicited AEs were comparable across both age subgroups in QIVc (15,0) and aQIVc (45,2) vaccine groups.

Each treatment arm is described by the name of the treatment and the content in parentheses, where the first figure represents the antigen content (μg per strain) and the second number represents the adjuvant content (1 means 1x MF59 [ie, standard dose], 2 means 2x MF59 etc.).

In Study V201\\_01, in all vaccine groups, the rate of solicited reactions (58.0% to 89.5% vs 32.6% to 70.8%) and use of antipyretic/analgesics (14.0% to 27.1% vs 0 to 17.6%) were higher in subjects aged 50 to 64 years compared to subjects ≥ 65 years. Higher rates of solicited local AEs were observed in subjects aged 50 to 64 years (34.0% to 73.7%) compared to subjects aged ≥ 65 years (26.1% to 62.5%). Higher rates of solicited systemic AEs were observed in subjects aged 50 to 64 years (50.0% to 75.4%) compared to subjects aged ≥ 65 years (19.6% to 53.1%). For the two age subgroups (50 to 64 years and ≥ 65 years), no notable difference in proportions of unsolicited AEs was observed across vaccine groups during the treatment period.

## 5.4.5. Post marketing experience

Not applicable

## 5.4.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.6.1. Discussion

## 5.4.6.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

The clinical safety database to support the MA of aTIVc is derived from the clinical development of aQIVc. The manufacturing process and formulation of aTIVc are the same as that of aQIVc, with the exception of the exclusion of the B/Yamagata viral antigens in accordance with on WHO recommendations.

The safety data are based on four trials (V201\\_03, V201\\_07, V201\\_01, and V200\\_10), involving a total of 10,037 subjects (7,677 in study V201\\_03; 1,051 in study V201\\_07; 838 in study V01-01 and 471 in study V200\\_10). For all studies, safety and tolerability were determined by collection of solicited local and systemic adverse events (AE) for 7 days after vaccination (Day 1 to Day 7) and any unsolicited AEs during the treatment period (Day 1 to Day 29). Serious AEs (SAE), AEs leading to withdrawal, AEs of special interest (AESI) (in Study V201\\_03) and medically attended adverse events (MAAEs) were collected throughout the entire study duration (i.e., from Day 1 to Day 181).

In the studies the safety assessments were conducted independently, and no pooling of safety data was performed.

The pivotal V201\\_03 was conducted in 7677 subjects (3282 subjects in aQIVc HD, 2204 subjects in aQIV and 2191 subjects in QIVr). The demographic characteristics were well balanced among the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV vaccine groups. More female subjects (56.1%) than male subjects (43.9%) were exposed in the study. The majority of subjects were White (74.2%) followed by Asian (19.3%). The study population was predominantly of 'not Hispanic or Latino' origin (94.3%), with 5.1% of subjects identified as Hispanic or Latino. The mean (SD) body mass index was 28.4 (6.13)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

kg/m2. Overall, 60.5% of subjects were previously vaccinated against influenza and 39.5% of subjects were not vaccinated against influenza within the 3 previous influenza seasons. 81.5% of participants reported at least 1 medical disorder and 80.1% reported at least 1 concomitant medication.

With regard to reactogenicity in trial V201\\_03, local AEs assessed included injection site pain, erythema and induration; and systemic AEs assessed included nausea, fatigue, myalgia, arthralgia, headache and fever. More AEs are observed with aQIVc HD vaccine (60.2 %) than with standard dose aQIV vaccine (51.4 %) and non-adjuvanted QIVr vaccine (41.9 %); this is to be expected given the increase in antigen and/or adjuvant concentrations. However, this increased proportion of AEs with aQIVc HD compared to aQIV and QIVr remains acceptable for local AEs (47.3% vs. 36.6%, and 22.4%, respectively), and systemic AEs (43.6% vs. 36.1%, and 32.7%, respectively. Indeed, the AEs observed are most often mild in intensity, appear within 3 days of vaccination and do not persist for more than 7 days. Severe solicited AEs are rare with aQIVc HD vaccine (1.8 % and 3.3 % for local and systemic solicited AEs respectively), they occur almost at twice lower frequency with QIVr and aQIV vaccines (0.7 % and 0.8 % for local solicited AEs with QIVr and aQIV respectively, and 1.8 % and 2 % for systemic solicited AEs with QIVr and aQIV respectively).

The main and most frequently reported solicited local AE was injection site pain in each of the three groups (45.6%, 34.3%, and 21.3%, respectively). The most frequently reported solicited systemic AEs in all groups were fatigue (32.8%, 23.7%, and 23.1%, respectively) and headache (22.0%, 17.7%, and 16.0%, respectively), followed by arthralgia (17.0%, 12.7%, 12.2%, respectively) and myalgia (16.8%, 10.4% and 9.0%, respectively). Fever was reported in 3.5%, 1.9%, and 2.0% of subjects in the aQIVc HD, aQIV, and QIVr groups, respectively, with severe fever reported in 0.2%, 0.4%, and &lt; 0.1% of subjects, respectively.

No notable difference was observed in the frequency of unsolicited AEs among the three groups (11.5% approx. in each group). Only COVID-19, nasopharyngitis and upper respiratory tract infection were reported with an incidence &gt;1% (COVID: 2.1% in aQIVc, 1.9% in aQIV and 1.9 in QIVr; Nasopharyngitis: 1.1%, 1.4% and 1.6%; upper respiratory tract infection: 0.9%, 1.0% and 1.1%, respectively). The percentage of subjects reporting related unsolicited AE in each vaccine group was very low overall, with slightly higher rates in the aQIVc HD group than in the QIVr or aQIV groups (aQIVc HD: 1.2%; QIVr: 0.7%; aQIV: 0.7%). No relevant imbalance in the proportion of any unsolicited related AEs was observed. Injection site pruritus, Chills and Dizziness were already included in section 4.8 of the SmPC.  During the procedure, the applicant also agreed to the inclusion of Injection site swelling as an ADR in the SmPC.

In Study V201\\_03, 27 deaths were reported, including 12 in the aQIVc HD group (0.4 %), 9 in the QIVr group (0.4 %), and 6 in the aQIV group (0.3 %). None of them were related to the study vaccine.

From Day 1 to Day 181, SAEs were reported by 2.8% of subjects in aQIVc, 3.5% of subjects in aQIV and 2.8% of subjects in QIVr. The most commonly reported SAEs in any group were pneumonia and osteoarthritis. From Day 182 to Day 365, SAEs were reported by 1.1%, 1.4% and 1.5% of subjects in aQIVc, aQIV and QIVr, respectively and no individual SAE was reported in more than 2 subjects.

No clinically relevant imbalance in the incidence of SAEs was observed between the groups; only two events were considered potentially related to aQIVc HD including a case of giant cell arteritis (see below) and syncope. Other 2 SAEs were reported in aQIV group (diabetic ketoacidosis and ischaemic stroke).

Initially, the applicant included the PT syncope in table 2 (Post-marketing experience reported after use of similar influenza vaccines) in the section 4.8 of the SmPC. However, after the RSI evaluation, the applicant accepted to include it in the table 1, with 'rare' frequency. Overall, 16 AESIS from Day 1 to Day 181 and 3 AESIs from Day 182 to Day 365 were reported.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

From Day 1 to Day 181, the incidence of AESIs was low and with higher frequency in adjuvanted vaccine (0.3% in aQIVc and 0.2% in aQIV) compared with QIVr (&lt;0.1%). Two of the 16 AESIs were classified as SAEs, one event of giant cell arteritis in a subject who received aQIVc which was assessed as possibly related to the study vaccine; the other one was coeliac disease in a subject who received aQIV and was classified as an unrelated SAE.

In addition, one non-serious AESI of polymyalgia rheumatica was reported in the aQIVc HD group considered as not related to the vaccine, and three additional non-serious AESIs of polymyalgia rheumatica were reported: one in the aQIV group and two in the QIVr group (Of note:  in Study V201\\_01 (Day 1 to Day 181), a SAE of polymyalgia rheumatica was reported in a subject who received aQIVc (30.3) assessed as possibly related to the investigational vaccine). Following the request of the rapporteur, the applicant submitted the narratives for all the events of polymyalgia rheumatica occurring in the V201\\_01 and V201\\_03. Moreover, the applicant has planned to monitor these events as part of this categorization, which will be analysed and reported in aggregate via the Periodic Safety Update Reports (PSUR) for aTIVc.

In addition, the applicant also proposed to include polymyalgia rheumatica as an adverse event of special interest. Therefore, there will be a review of case reports, as well as monthly signal detection activities that include quantitative analyses and medical review of the safety data.

The remaining AESIs were classified as non-serious and assessed as not related to the study vaccine; these AESIs were also classified as non-serious MAAEs.

From Day 182 to Day 365, one AESI was reported in each group and none were assessed as related to the study vaccine.

In the aQIVc HD group, two AESIs of giant cell arteritis were reported: one SAE considered related to the vaccine and one non-serious case assessed as potentially related by the sponsor (but not by the investigator).  During the procedure, the applicant discussed the potential mechanism in predisposed individuals, suggesting that the immune stimulation provided by adjuvanted vaccines could act as a trigger rather than a sole cause, for which it would be required a pre-existing susceptibility (age, genetic background, prior infections or autoimmunity) to culminate in GCA diagnoses. Nevertheless, the applicant accepted the inclusion of Giant cell arteritis (GCA) in table 1 of section 4.8 instead of the broader term of Vasculitis, as initially proposed.

The applicant did not consider including GCA as an important potential risk in the safety specification. Instead, the applicant plans to monitor the events of GCA as part of Vasculitis categorisation and to analyse them in aggregate in the PSUR. The applicant also proposes to monitor GCA as an AESI that will be part of the routine monitoring (including a review of case reports, monthly signal detection activities). Moreover, every report of GCA from the planned efficacy study will be included once it has started.

In addition, in the aQIVc HD group, two AESIs were reported (autoimmune thyroiditis and Bell's palsy) that were considered non-related by the investigator but potentially related by the sponsor. Bell's palsy is already included as an ADR in section 4.8 of the SmPC. Based on the narrative of the autoimmune thyroiditis (considered possibly related by the sponsor because the causal role of the study vaccine cannot be completely excluded), the applicant has been requested to include autoimmune thyroiditis as an important potential risk in the safety specification of the RMP. Instead of following this request, the applicant proposed to include autoimmune thyroiditis in the list of AESIs, and to monitor the cases as part of PSUR. Considering that it was an isolated report with no clinical symptoms this proposal is considered acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All MAAEs were considered as non-serious and the incidence of MAAEs was similar between groups from Day 1 to 181 (15.6% in aQIVc, 14.1% in QIVr and 15.1% in aQIV) and from Day 182 to Day 365 (7.9%, 7.4% and 7.9%, respectively). The most commonly reported MAAEs by SOC were 'Infections and infestations' in both periods. However, from Day 1 to Day 181 by PT the frequency of hypertension was 0.8%, 0.5%, and 0.6%, respectively and 0.4%, 0.3%, and 0.3%, respectively from Day 182 to Day 365. The applicant provided during the procedure a summary of the hypertension events considered MAAEs from Days 1 to 181 in studies V201\\_01, V201\\_07, and V201\\_03. There were reported 59 cases, of these, 32 hypertension events in aQIVc group. The 32 events were either mild or moderate in intensity, non-serious medically attended events. The applicant argued that there was non-related relationship of hypertension events to aQIVc, based on that the proportion of subjects reporting all-cause hypertension events in the aQIVc group was lower than the background rate of hypertension in this age group. None of the reports were related to aQIVc and were mostly reported in individuals who had pre-existing hypertension and significant risk factors for the event.

Regarding the safety profile by age, the reactogenicity (solicited local and systemic AEs) was higher in aQIVc HD followed by aQIV, and then QIVr in both age subgroups, as in the overall population. Comparing solicited local and systemic AEs by age, the percentage of subjects reporting any AE was higher in the 5064 years of age subgroup than in the ≥65 years of age subgroup. The percentage difference between age subgroups in aQIVc HD was approximately 15%.

No difference was observed regarding the incidence of unsolicited AEs between vaccine groups in both age groups and between age groups. However, in the aQIVc HD group, the percentage of subjects with related unsolicited AEs was slightly higher in the 50-64 years of age subgroup than the ≥65 years of age subgroup.

The percentage of SAEs was low and similar between vaccine groups in both age groups and slightly lower in the 5064 years of age subgroup than in the ≥65 years of age subgroup for all vaccines, possibly due to the older age.

It is noteworthy that all SAEs considered vaccine-related (two with aQIVc and two with aQIV) occurred in ≥65 years of age subgroup.

No notable difference in AESIs and MAAEs was observed between age groups.

In addition, in each of the aQIVc HD, QIVr, and aQIV groups, the percentage of subjects reporting solicited AEs (any, local, and systemic) and analgesic/antipyretic use tended to increase with increasing comorbidity risk score

Of note, concomitant administration of aQIVc HD with other vaccines was not assessed, particularly in the population over 50 years. During the procedure, the applicant clarified that the analysis of safety information coming from subjects who received concomitant vaccine/s will be incorporated in the applicant's review of spontaneous reports via routine pharmacovigilance monitoring. Additionally it is proposed to include an aggregate analysis of safety reports with co-administered vaccines as part of the Monitored Topics in Periodic Safety Update Reports.

In the two supportive studies (V201\\_07 and V201\\_01) the final formulation was assessed in groups of 262 and 101 participants, respectively and the safety data were included from both studies. Demographic characteristics generally were well balanced among the study vaccine groups.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In Study V201\\_07 and V201\\_01, as expected, the incidence of solicited local AEs was higher when increasing HA and/or MF59 adjuvant. However, no major differences were observed in the incidence of systemic AEs across different concentrations of HA and/or MF59 adjuvant.

The incidence of solicited local and systemic AEs in participates who received aQIVc HD were 58.6% and 49.8%, respectively in Study V201\\_07 and 54.5% and 45.5%, respectively in Study V201\\_01.

Regarding unsolicited AEs, there were no notable differences in the frequency across vaccine groups either in study V201\\_07 or study V201\\_01. Most unsolicited AEs reported were the same as the solicited AEs (Arthralgia, myalgia, fatigue, headache, pain, etc…) or included in the SOC infections and infestations.

Additionally, in Study V201\\_07, two events of increased blood pressure associated with aQIVc HD were documented.  During the procedure, the applicant submitted the detailed cases description based on the available eCRF-data of the two events of increased blood pressure: in the two cases, the participants had pre-existing hypertension and the events appeared to be acute immunization stress related responses (ISRR). However, the ISRR was not specifically assessed at the time point of blood pressure measurement.

None of the deaths reported in the supportive studies V201\\_07 and V201\\_01 were assessed related to the study vaccines. The number of SAEs during the studies was low, and the majority were not related to the study vaccine. There was 1 SAE of presyncope in aQIVc (45,1) group in V201\\_07 considered related by the investigator but non related by the sponsor, and 1 SAE of polymyalgia rheumatic (considered also an AESI) in aQIV (30,3) in V201\\_01 considered possibly related to the study vaccine.

In V201\\_07 no AESI was reported. In V201\\_01, 3 AESIs were reported, one SAE of polymyalgia rheumatic (indicated before), one non-serious AESI of psoriasis in the QIVc (15,0) group, considered as possibly related to study vaccine and one non-serious adverse event of rheumatoid arthritis in the aQIVc (30,3) group, considered as unrelated to the study vaccine.

No main differences were observed between groups in both studies regarding MAAEs, the incidence was low and the majority of MAAE by SOC was 'Infections and infestations' in all groups, in both studies. In both studies MAAE by SOC 'Vascular disorder' was also reported, with a frequency between 0.8%-1.5% in V201\\_07 and 0-2.8% in V201\\_01.  In addition, 2 events of deep vein thrombosis considered non serious were reported in aQIVc (45,1). Additional information was requested from the applicant regarding these two events. The applicant explained that one event occurred in a participant with multiple chronic conditions that increase baseline thrombotic risk, while the other one occurred in a participant using corticosteroids for asthma, that may lead to a more procoagulant state.

Therefore, the two events of DVT were considered as non-related to study vaccine by the presence of confounding factors.

In both supportive studies, the rate of solicited reactions (including local and systemic) was higher in subjects aged 50 to 64 years compared to subjects ≥65 years.

The use of antipyretic/analgesics was higher in subgroup 50 to 64 years than subjects ≥65 years, with the exception in subjects who received aQIVc (45,2) in V201\\_07, where the opposite occurred (aQIVc (45,2): 19.7%in subjects aged 50 to 64 years and 21.3% in subjects aged ≥65 years). However, the use of antipyretic/analgesics was reported for treatment and prevention, therefore, this information is not useful from a reactogenicity perspective. In addition, no notable difference was found in proportion of unsolicited AEs in subjects who received aQIVc (45,2) between subjects aged 5064 or ≥65 years.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, in the three studies, no scheduled clinical laboratory assessments were included. No laboratory assessments of haematology, blood chemistry, or urine chemistry were specified in the protocol.

## 5.4.6.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs proposed by the applicant for inclusion in the SmPC are described in section 5.4.3.2 above.

Following further discussion, the CHMP agrees on the inclusion of the table below in the SmPC. Compared with the applicant's initial proposal, the following modifications are agreed: the inclusion of syncope and injection-site swelling as ADRs in Table 46, and the replacement of vasculitis with giant cell arteritis.

Table 46. Adverse reactions

| MedDRA system organ class                            | Very common                  | Common                                                    | Rare                                                     |
|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| Nervous system disorders                             | Headache                     |                                                           | Syncope, dizziness, Bell's palsy                         |
| Vascular disorders                                   |                              |                                                           | Giant cell arteritis                                     |
| Gastrointestinal disorders                           |                              | Nausea                                                    |                                                          |
| Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders      | Arthralgia, myalgia          |                                                           |                                                          |
| General disorders and administration site conditions | Injection site pain, fatigue | Fever, injection site induration, injection site erythema | Injection site swelling, injection site pruritus, chills |

## 5.4.6.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

In this application, the benefit-risk profile of the candidate product, aTIVc, is supported with safety data from the pivotal aQIVc HD Study V201\\_03 and supportive studies V201\\_01, V203\\_01 and V200\\_10.

The vaccine aQIVc HD was well tolerated in adults ≥50 years of age. The majority of solicited local and systemic AEs reported were mild or moderate in severity and most of them were of short duration ( ≤ 3 days) and resolved within 7-days of vaccination. Of note, the rates of solicited events (local and systemic) were highest in aQIVc HD group, followed by the aQIV group (approximately, 10% more local AEs and 5% more incidence of systemic AEs were reported in aQIVc HD compared to aQIV), and lowest in the QIVr group. In addition, no notable differences in the frequency of unsolicited AEs were observed among the three groups.

Low incidences of SAEs, AESIs and MAAEs were reported during the study. However, some safety concerns were identified (giant cell arteritis, polymyalgia rheumatica and autoimmune thyroiditis). The CHMP agreed to include giant cell arteritis in the SmPC. The applicant did not propose the inclusion of polymyalgia rheumatica and autoimmune thyroiditis in the safety specification. Instead, the CHMP agreed with the applicant to include these events in the list of AESIs to be monitored through routine pharmacovigilance activities, including PSUR review.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Taking all the above into consideration, it is concluded that the safety profile of the candidate product aTIVc HD, is expected to be similar to that of aQIVc HD. As aTIVc HD contains one fewer strain than the quadrivalent vaccine, and therefore 45 µg less HA, it could potentially be associated with lower reactogenicity than aQIVc HD. Overall, the available safety data for aQIVc HD support the indication for aTIVc HD for prophylaxis of influenza from 50 years of age onwards.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

The applicant proposed the following summary of safety concerns in the RMP:

Table 47. Summary of safety concerns in the proposed RMP

| Summary of safety concerns   | Summary of safety concerns   |
|------------------------------|------------------------------|
| Important identified risks   | None                         |
| Important potential risks    | None                         |
| Missing information          | None                         |

## 6.1.2. Discussion on proposed safety specification

The applicant does not include any important identified and potential risk in the safety specification in the RMP.

During the procedure, it was discussed the inclusion of Giant Cell arteritis (GCA) and Autoimmune thyroiditis as an important potential risk. After reviewing the responses to the 1 st  RSI, the applicant indicated that they plan to monitor the events of GCA as part of Vasculitis categorisation and to analyse them in aggregate in the PSUR. In addition, the applicant proposed the inclusion of GCA and Autoimmune thyroiditis as AESIs.

## Missing Information - Special Populations:

Initially, the applicant did not propose any Missing Information. Following a request to justify the absence of safety data in subjects with underlying medical conditions (in particular autoimmune diseases), the applicant submitted a post hoc subgroup analysis for participants in study V201\\_03 based on comorbidity scores (0; 1; ≥ 2) and concluded that data in patients with autoimmune disorders were limited. Consequently, the applicant had agreed to include 'Use in Patients with Autoimmune disorders' as Missing information.

After PRAC discussion, PRAC recommends that 'Use in patients with autoimmune disorders' be not included as missing information in the RMP since no additional pharmacovigilance activities are planned to further characterise this safety concern and no additional risk minimisation measures are considered warranted.  Instead, the PRAC proposes that 'Use in patients with autoimmune disorders' be monitored through routine pharmacovigilance as part of the PSUR safety specification.

The MAH agrees to delete the missing information 'Use in patients with Autoimmune Disorders' and to proactively monitor the missing information 'Use in patients with Autoimmune Disorders' through routine pharmacovigilance as part of the PSUR safety specification.

## 6.2. Pharmacovigilance plan

## Routine pharmacovigilance activities beyond adverse reactions reporting and signal detection:

Routine pharmacovigilance activities beyond adverse reactions reporting and signal detection are not foreseen for the product aTIVc.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

To note: the annual Enhanced Safety Surveillance for all seasonal influenza vaccines approved and marketed in the EU, classified as a routine pharmacovigilance activity is not obligatory at the present time.

## Specific adverse reaction follow-up questionnaires for safety concerns :

None required

## Other forms of routine pharmacovigilance activities for adverse events following immunization :

The 'Interim guidance on Enhanced Safety Surveillance for seasonal influenza vaccines in the EU' (EMA/PRAC/222346/2014) requires the implementation of annual Enhanced Safety Surveillance for influenza vaccines. It should be noted, however, that at the DLP of this RMP this interim guidance is under review by the EMA (Health Threats and Vaccines strategy) (AF-HTV, 2023). In addition, at the Pharmacovigilance Risk Assessment Committee (PRAC) plenary meeting in April 2024, it was agreed to waive the requirement to submit enhanced safety surveillance data for all seasonal influenza vaccines (both national and centrally approved) while the 'Interim guidance on enhanced safety surveillance for seasonal influenza vaccines in the EU' (EMA/PRAC/222346/2014) is under review (PRAC, 2024).

The passive approach of the Enhanced Safety Surveillance is classified as a routine pharmacovigilance activity and in accordance with chapter 'V.B.6.1.2 RMP part III section Routine pharmacovigilance activities of the GVP Module V'. The implementation of the Enhanced Passive Safety Surveillance will depend on the applicable guidance and any waivers in place at the time.

## Additional pharmacovigilance activities

## Required additional pharmacovigilance activities

None required.

## Additional pharmacovigilance activities recommended under EMA guidelines

In the Guideline on Influenza vaccines - Non-clinical and Clinical Module (EMA/ CHMP/VWP/457259/ 2014) of July 2016, the EMA has emphasized the importance of brand-specific influenza vaccine effectiveness (IVE) data for ongoing safety and efficacy assessment. EMA outlined the necessity for manufacturers to provide post-authorization IVE estimates to complement pre-authorization CT data.

To address this requirement, the Innovative Medicines Initiative program launched the Development of Robust Innovative Vaccine Effectiveness (DRIVE) project in July 2017.

DRIVE represented a significant public-private partnership, with CSL Seqirus and other vaccine manufacturers collaborating with academic and public health institutions to establish a sustainable platform for generating brand-specific IVE data across Europe.

Over its 5-year operational period, DRIVE facilitated the collection and analysis of IVE data, contributing valuable insights into the real-world performance of various influenza vaccines.

However, the DRIVE project concluded in July 2022. Following its conclusion, the EMA granted deferrals to vaccine manufacturers, including CSL Seqirus, regarding the provision of brand-specific IVE data for the 2022-2023, 2023-2024, and 2024-2025 NH influenza seasons.

Consequently, DRIVE studies were not conducted during these seasons.

Hence, the implementation of any recommended IVE study involving aTIVc will depend on the applicable guidance and any waivers in place at the time of marketing authorisation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

To note: the development of Robust Innovative Vaccine Effectiveness (DRIVE) project is not conducted at the present time.

There are no on-going and planned additional pharmacovigilance activities for the product aTIVc.

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## 9.1. Therapeutic context

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, proposed therapeutic indication

Influenza is an infectious acute respiratory disease characterized by the abrupt onset of respiratory and systemic  symptoms,  such  as  fever,  cough,  sore  throat,  and  rhinitis  (Temte  and  Prunuske  2010). Influenza occurs in annual epidemics in the Northern Hemisphere and Southern Hemisphere, generally affecting individuals during the winter months in temperate climates. In tropical climates, influenza can occur all year round.

Influenza in humans can be caused by the influenza virus type A, B, and C, with the type A and type B viruses being the most clinically relevant (WHO 2018). Type A viruses are associated with both annual epidemics and pandemics, and type B viruses contribute to annual epidemics. Type A viruses are further divided into different subtypes, of which the A/H3N2 and A/H1N1 viruses are the most clinically relevant for the annual influenza disease burden (CDC 2021). For influenza B, only a single type is known to exist, but  two  distinct  genetic  lineages  have  been  identified:  Yamagata  and  Victoria.  Circulation  of  the B/Yamagata lineage has not been confirmed since March 2020. All influenza viruses experience some form of antigenic drift, but this is most pronounced in the influenza A virus for which new circulating variants  are  frequently  observed  over  the  influenza  season.  Such  evolution  of  the  influenza  virus influences the host specificity and pathogenicity of these viruses, which can differ geographically, from season to season, and within a season.

Influenza is an acute and highly contagious viral infection with global circulation. Globally, there are an estimated 1 billion  cases  of  influenza  disease  every  year,  of  which  3  to  5  million  are  severe  cases, resulting in 290,000 to 650,000 influenza-related respiratory deaths (WHO 2019; Iuliano et al. 2018). In the United States (US), the influenza-related hospitalization rate over 15 seasons was estimated to be  309  per  100,000  in  individuals  65  years  and  older  (Zhou  et  al.  2012).  Although  the  rate  of hospitalization associated with influenza is lower in subjects aged 50 to 64 years compared with those aged 65 years and older, the highest number of influenza illnesses and medical visits is reported for the 50-64 years age cohort, which indicates a high burden of disease in this age group (CDC 2021).

Vaccination  is  the  primary  method  for  preventing  influenza  and  its  potentially  severe  complications. Moderate-to-high efficacy of conventional unadjuvanted, egg-based, standard dose influenza vaccines has been demonstrated in the healthy adult population (18-50 years of age) (Samuel and Miller 2019). In contrast, the efficacy of conventional vaccines is significantly lower in elderly individuals compared with  younger  adults  due  to  aging  of  the  immune  system  (i.e.,  immunosenescence)  and  underlying medical conditions that can both increase the risk of influenza complications and interfere with immune responses (Goodwin et al. 2006; Sasaki et al. 2011; Dorrington and Bowdish 2013). In view of the limitations of conventional influenza vaccines to prevent disease in older adults, there continues to be an unmet need for a new generation of influenza vaccines that provide more consistent and broader coverage against all seasonal virus subtypes and variants (Reber et al. 2012; Wong and Webby 2013).

## 9.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

For a detailed description, please see also section 2.1 of this document.

aTIVc  combines  3  technologies  to  overcome  immunosenescence,  namely,  cell-based  technology,  an adjuvant, and a higher antigen dose.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Cell culture-derived antigens may provide a better match to circulating influenza strains, addressing the issue of potential mismatch due to egg adaptation, which could improve vaccine effectiveness (Lambert and Fauci 2010; Hegde 2015). Hence, the use of cell-based technology in the production of aTIVc.

Overcoming immunosenescence can be further achieved by enhancing the immune response with the addition of an adjuvant to the vaccine formulation, such as the MF59 adjuvant, which is a squalene and water  emulsion  adjuvant.  The  addition  of  MF59  increases  antigen  uptake,  macrophage  recruitment, macrophage migration, and the spectrum of antibody recognition of HA epitopes (Calabro et al. 2011; O'Hagan et. 2012). When added to influenza vaccines, MF59 increases vaccine immunogenicity against both homologous and heterologous strains. Fluad, CSL Seqirus' trivalent seasonal influenza egg-based vaccine adjuvanted with MF59, has been licensed for use in Europe since 1997 and in the US since 2015. This  adjuvanted  trivalent  influenza  vaccine  (aTIV)  has  been  shown  to  generate  significantly  higher geometric mean  hemagglutination inhibition (HI) titres and rates of seroconversion than a nonadjuvanted  trivalent  influenza  vaccine  (TIV)  comparator  in  elderly  subjects  (Frey  et  al.  2014). Inclusion of higher doses of the MF59 adjuvant in the aTIVc vaccine is expected to enable: (i) increased magnitude  of  immune  response  by  stimulating  higher  antibody  responses;  (ii)  increased  breadth  of immune response, by expanding antibody repertoire; and (iii) increased duration of immune response, with higher antibody response at 6 months after vaccination, which is important in case of prolonged influenza seasons and/or late season outbreaks (Frey et al. 2014).

Another way to improve the benefit of influenza vaccines in the elderly may be to increase the vaccine antigen amount per dose above the 15 µg of HA per strain found in standard dose inactivated vaccines. In a Phase 1 study, vaccination with higher doses of influenza virus antigen (up to 2 times the standard HA antigen dose) and MF59 adjuvant (up to 3 times the standard MF59 dose) has been demonstrated to increase  the  magnitude  of  the  immune  response  by  stimulating  higher  antibody  responses  to  virus antigens  (Otten  et  al.  2020).  The  use  of  serological  surrogates  as  a  measure  of  vaccine  efficacy  is generally accepted for regulatory purposes.

In summary, the MF59-adjuvanted trivalent subunit inactivated cell-derived influenza vaccine (aTIVc) is expected to offer potential important advantages over currently existing/licensed influenza vaccines in the ≥ 50 years of age category (e.g., Fluad, Fluzone High-Dose, Flublok) by combining the benefits of a cell-based platform, adjuvanted technology, and high dose of HA antigens.

The clinical development program to support licensure of aTIVc in individuals ≥ 50 years of age is based on the results of supportive dose finding Study V201\\_01, supportive dose confirmation Study V201\\_07, and the pivotal aQIVc Study V201\\_03 (a confirmatory controlled trial for immunogenicity and safety), which tested the aQIVc HD formulation against other (a)QIVc formulations and comparator vaccines; data from an additional supportive Study V200\\_10 (proof of concept) are also included, as this study first investigated the addition of adjuvant to QIVc, testing aQIVc at a standard dose of HA antigens and MF59.

The manufacturing process and formulation of aTIVc are the same as that of aQIVc, with the exception of the additional B strain included in aQIVc. Therefore, clinical study experience with aQIVc provides data for assessment of aTIVc.

The data package to support the favourable benefit/risk profile of aTIVc in individuals ≥ 50 years of age includes immunogenicity and safety data from 4 completed studies. No studies are currently ongoing.

The design, conduct and analysis of studies in the development of aQIVc/aTIVc comply with international and regional standards for clinical research in humans, and for investigation of the immunogenicity and safety of seasonal influenza virus vaccines.

As  a  first  step,  having  confirmed  that  the  aQIVc  HD  formulation  (45,2)  elicited  a  superior  immune response to the licensed, non-adjuvanted, equivalent, cell-based Flucelvax Quadrivalent (QIVc) for all 4

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

strains  in  the  Phase  2b  dose-confirmation  immunogenicity  and  safety  study  (V201\\_07),  enhanced influenza vaccines that are the standard of care for older adults were included as comparators in Phase 3.

For pivotal Study V201\\_03, since aQIVc HD contains the same adjuvant (MF59) as aQIV, albeit in a higher dose, the enhanced influenza vaccine aQIV was chosen as a comparator influenza vaccine to demonstrate  the  effect  of  the  increased  amount  of  adjuvant  and  increased  antigen  dose.  With  the approval  of  aQIV  in  the  UK  and  EU  for  adults  50  years  and  older,  demonstration  of  immunological superiority of aQIVc HD over aQIV in both the 50 years and older and 65 years and older age groups would provide the basis to apply for licensure in these regions.

QIVr is another enhanced influenza vaccine due to its high dose of recombinant HA antigen content. Inclusion of this vaccine as a second comparator in pivotal Study V201\\_03 provided further regulatory flexibility, allowing adaptation to the preferences of national advisory divisions.

The chosen dual comparator study design is adequately sized and powered to meet the study endpoints and  considered  sufficiently  robust  to  assess  the  effect  of  aQIVc  HD  formulation  in  terms  of immunogenicity and safety separately for each comparator.

In this MAA, the comparison against aQIV is the basis of the data for licensure.

All studies were designed, implemented, and reported in accordance with the International Council for Harmonisation of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use (ICH) Guidelines for Good Clinical Practice, with applicable local regulations.

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

For a detailed description of the main clinical studies supporting this application, please refer to section 5.3.2 of this document.

Study V201\\_03, a Phase 3, Observer-Blind, Controlled, Multicenter, Clinical Study to Evaluate Immunogenicity and Safety of a High-Dose MF59-Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Cell-derived Influenza Vaccine (aQIVc HD) in Comparison with a Non-adjuvanted Quadrivalent Recombinant Influenza Vaccine (QIVr) and an MF59-Adjuvanted Quadrivalent Subunit Egg-derived Influenza Vaccine (aQIVc), in Adults Aged 50 Years and Older.

The application also includes three additional supportive studies that helped find the final dosage for the vaccine:

- V200\\_10, a Phase 2, randomized, stratified, controlled, observer-blind, multi-center proof-ofconcept study that compared immunogenicity and safety of aQIVc (formulation 15,1) to QIVc, aQIV and QIV2.
- V201\\_01, a Phase 2, randomized, stratified, controlled, observer-blind, multi-center, antigen, and adjuvant dose-ranging clinical study that tested seven different formulations: 3 antigen dosages (15, 30 and 45 µg HA/strain) and 3 adjuvant dosages (9.75, 19.5 and 29.25 mg of squalene), including the final selected dosage aQIVC (45,2).
- V201\\_07 a Phase 2b, randomized, stratified, controlled, observer-blind, multi-center, antigen, and adjuvant dose-confirmation clinical study testing three different vaccine formulations, all with 45 µg HA/strain and different adjuvant dosage (no adjuvant, 9.75 or 19.5 mg of squalene).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.3. Favourable effects

aTIVc was compared to aQIV and to QIVr in pivotal study V201\\_03, in order to establish if the formulation with increased quantity of antigen and of adjuvant confers improved immunogenicity, in subjects 50 years and above. In this regard, two primary objectives were to demonstrate non-inferiority of aTIVc with its comparators. This study also had a primary endpoint of demonstrating lot-to-lot consistency between three consecutive lots.

Taking  all  the  primary  analysis  together,  the  lot-to-lot  consistency  endpoint  was  met  and  the  noninferiority of aQIVc HD was demonstrated vs aQIV but not vs QIVr. From the EU perspective, neither the lot-to-lot nor the comparison with QIVr would have been required as part of the study design to comply with  EU  regulation.  Therefore,  the  data  most  important  for  this  assessment  are  those  from  the comparison between the study vaccine and aQIV. On this basis, the primary analysis of the V201\\_03 study can be considered successful.

In  the  key-secondary  endpoints,  non-inferiority  between  aQIVc  HD  and  aQIV  was  demonstrated  in subjects 65 years of age and older. Additionally, superiority in subjects 50 years of age and older was demonstrated between aQIVc HD and aQIV and in the 65 years and above population.

Taking all  results  together,  aQIVc  HD  shows  an  improved  immunogenicity  compared  to  aQIV  in  the overall population (50 years and above) for which the indication is sought and in the older population (65 years and above) that present a higher risk of influenza-derived complications.

The improved immunogenicity demonstrated in the pivotal Phase 3 study is supported by the three supportive studies, which showed the advantage of increasing the antigen (from 15 to 45 µg HA/strain) and the adjuvant dose (up to 19.50 mg of squalene).

The studied population was representative of the target population (adults 50 years and above), with a good (about 50%) representation of the &gt; 65 years of age, with comparable results in different age groups.

The results between the different types of assays (HI titres by the cell-derived and respectively eggderived target virus as well as MN titres) were comparable.

aQIVc HD elicited an immune response (HI titres and seroconversion rate) at D29, that was maintained above the pre-vaccination levels up to 12 months post-vaccination.

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

No clinical studies have been performed with the trivalent formulation of aTIVc. Nevertheless, the manufacturing process and formulation of aTIVc are the same as that of aQIVc, with the exception of the additional B strain included in aQIVc. Therefore, the favourable effects shown for aQIVc are considered applicable to aTiVc.

Efficacy is inferred from comparative immunogenicity studies.

When compared to QIVr, in subjects &gt; 50 years of age, NI was not demonstrated for the A/H3N2 strain. The clinical meaning of this finding is not known.

Concomitant administration with other vaccines in the elderly has not been studied. Though based on clinical experience Flucelvax (QIVc) can be given at the same time as other vaccines, this is the first vaccine combining high dose of antigen and high dose of adjuvant and interference with other vaccines is not known.

A limitation that is common to all influenza vaccines is the fact that efficacy depends on the degree of antigenic match between vaccine and circulating strains and therefore the efficacy of the seasonal influenza vaccines could vary in different seasons. An efficacy study is planned but the design has not been outlined.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

The clinical safety database to support the MA of aTIVc is derived from the clinical development of aQIVc. The manufacturing process and formulation of aTIVc are the same as that of aQIVc, with the exception of the exclusion of the B/Yamagata viral antigens in accordance with on WHO recommendations.

According to the data submitted, the vaccine aQIVc HD (also called aQIVc (45,2)) was well tolerated in adults ≥ 50 years of age with a good safety profile. The majority of solicited local and systemic AEs were mild  or  moderate  in  severity  and  of  short  duration  ( ≤ 3  days)  and  resolved  during  the  7-day  period following vaccination. The rates of solicited events (local and systemic) were higher in aQIVc (45,2) group  followed  by  the  aQIV  group  (approximately,  10%  more  incidence  of  local  AEs  and  5%  more incidence of systemic AEs were reported in aQIVc compared to aQIV), and lower in the QIVr group. In addition, no notable difference was observed in the frequency of unsolicited AEs among three groups.

The main and most frequently reported solicited local AE in subjects who received aQIVc (45,2) was injection site pain and the most frequently reported solicited systemic AEs were fatigue and headache, followed by arthralgia and myalgia. Fever was reported in 3.5% of subjects, with severe fever reported in 0.2% of subjects.

Low incidences of SAEs, AESIs and MAAEs were reported during the study. Four SAEs were considered related to study vaccine, 2 of them in aQIVc group (Giant cell arteritis and syncope) and both of them were included in the SmPC; and the other 2 in aQIV group (diabetic ketoacidosis and ischaemic stroke). The event of diabetic ketoacidosis was classified as possibly related by the investigator but as not related by the sponsor. The assessor endorses the sponsor criteria and considers that the event of the diabetic ketoacidosis was not related to aQIV.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

There are no data available for aTIVc HD; however, it is expected to have a similar safety profile to that of aQIVc HD or even with probably less reactogenicity than the aQIVc HD as it contains less total HA antigen. It is noted that aQIVc was well tolerated, with good reactogenicity and safety profile in subjects aged ≥50 years.

One related SAE of Giant cell arteritis was reported from the pivotal study and another AESI of giant cell arteritis was reported considered as related by the sponsor. Therefore, giant cell arteritis will be monitored as part of Vasculitis categorisation and analysed in aggregate in the PSUR, as well as being considered an AESI that will be part of the routine monitoring.

In addition, autoimmune thyroiditis and polymyalgia rheumatica will be proposed as AESIs and will be monitored as part of PSUR.

Concomitant administration of aQIVc HD with other vaccines was not assessed, particularly in the population over 50 years. Therefore, it cannot be excluded that the reactogenicity could be even larger.

No data are available for immunocompromised individuals or subjects with underlying medical conditions (in particular autoimmune diseases). Consequently, the MAH agrees to proactive monitor the missing information 'Use in patients with Autoimmune Disorders' through routine pharmacovigilance as part of the PSUR safety specification.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

Influenza is an acute and highly contagious viral infection with global circulation. Globally, there are an estimated 1 billion cases of influenza disease every year, of which 3 to 5 million are severe cases, resulting in 290,000 to 650,000 influenza-related respiratory deaths. There are several enhanced vaccines (either high dose of antigen or adjuvanted) authorised in the EU and indicated for older subjects.

aTIVc HD is the first vaccine combining high dose of antigen (45µg/strain) and high dose of adjuvant MF59 (19.5mg), intended to enhance the immune response in adults 50 years of age and more.

Although the clinical development was conducted with the quadrivalent vaccine, the immunogenicity data are considered fully applicable to the aTIVc HD.

The efficacy of aTIVc HD was inferred from comparative immunogenicity versus an MF59 adjuvanted (containing 9.75 mg of squalene) vaccine (Fluad).

Efficacy of aTIVc HD in preventing influenza in subjects 50 years of age and older has been demonstrated by showing superior immunogenicity of aQIVc HD than the already authorised adjuvanted vaccine (Fluad Tetra, aQIV). The beneficial effect of increasing HA antigen and adjuvant doses is also supported by three other clinical studies in the same population.

An additional comparative immunogenicity versus a high dose (45µg antigen per strain) vaccine (Supemtek) was also carried out. In this comparison, the NI was not fulfilled for one of the strains (A/H3N2); This finding is reflected in the SmPC to adequately inform physicians; however the clinical meaning of this finding is not known.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The available safety data for aQIVc HD support the indication for prophylaxis of influenza from 50 years of age onwards for aTIVc HD.

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

The benefit/risk balance for aTIVc is considered positive and supports the use of the vaccine for prevention of influenza in individuals 50 years of age and older.

## 9.6.3. CHMP conclusions

The benefit/risk balance for aTIVc is considered positive and supports the use of the vaccine for prevention of influenza in individuals 50 years of age and older.
